Letter dated 24 March 2026 from the Panel of Experts on Libya
established pursuant to Security Council resolution 1973 (2011)
addressed to the President of the Security Council
On behalf of the members of the Panel of Experts on Libya established pursuant
to Security Council resolution 1973 (2011), I have the honour to transmit herewith,
in accordance with paragraph 23 of resolution 2769 (2025), the final report on its
work.
The attached report was provided to the Security Council Committee established
pursuant to resolution 1970 (2011) concerning Libya on 19 February 2026 and was
considered by the Committee on 24 March 2026.
I would appreciate it if this letter and the report were brought to the attention of
the members of the Security Council and issued as a document of the Council.
(Signed) Salma Arka
Coordinator

S/2026/224
Final report of the Panel of Experts established pursuant to
resolution 1973 (2011) concerning Libya
Summary
As part of a newly established status quo, armed groups have become the main
actors shaping governance outcomes in the State of Libya through coercive and cartel-
like tactics. Operating through political front lines, these groups pervaded Libyan
institutions, utilizing armed violence, political interference and economic pressure.
Armed groups instrumentalized violence – without accountability – to provide
an umbrella of impunity to individuals who could generate ever-increasing streams of
revenue. All relevant institutions were impelled to operate in an environment that
shielded and enabled these armed groups to further their interests exclusively and
assert hegemony over the State of Libya, as all accountability mechanisms were
rendered ineffective.
The aftermath of the May 2025 events exhibited not only the outright impunity
in which armed groups operate, but also the most significant reshuffling of Tripoli-
based armed groups since August 2022. The Libyan Arab armed forces (LAAF)
completed the restructuring of their presence in southern Libya to exert greater control
over smuggling routes.
Libya increasingly served as a key operating environment for terrorist groups
and individuals and regional criminal networks. In southern Libya, the presence of
terrorist-affiliated groups, coupled with their sustained recruitment and revenue-
generating activities, constituted latent security risks. Their footprint fed into wider
regional instability rather than being translated into violent attacks within Libyan
territory. These activities, which were transnational in nature, entrenched both
terrorist and criminal ecosystems, which became inextricably interlinked.
The use of Libyan territory by LAAF, including by Subul al-Salam battalion, for
the facilitation of the cross-border movements of fighters and arms and related
materiel reinforced the role of Libya as a regional logistical and operational hub, with
direct repercussions for internal security. These dynamics resulted in Libyan
instability being extended across borders through the supply of fighters, arms and
related materiel into neighbouring countries’ conflicts, particularly in support of the
Rapid Support Forces in the Sudan. In turn, these activities projected violence back
into Libyan territory, materializing in cross-border armed movements and clashes on
Libyan soil.
Armed groups were responsible for incidents that obstructed or undermined the
municipal council elections. Acts that fell within the scope of paragraph 11 of Security
Council resolution 2571 (2021) included, inter alia, forced closure of election centres;
confiscation of voter cards and ballot boxes; threats to electoral staff; and forced
closure of and direct attacks on the offices of the High National Elections
Commission. Such acts also included threats to and arrests of candidates and their
families who are not affiliated with the local armed actor in control, forcing some
candidates to withdraw and to relocate from their municipality.
The Panel found armed groups in both the west and east responsible for
systematic violations of international humanitarian and human rights law, including
arbitrary detention, enforced disappearance, torture and other ill-treatment, denial of
fair trial and right to privacy, and the arbitrary deprivation of life.
2/299 26-02825
[[page 3]]
S/2026/224
The arms embargo was continuously violated and further flouted openly through
the announcement of military cooperation between Member States and armed actors
across Libya. Since 2022, Libyan armed group leaders from both the west and the
east have implemented a bilateral, symbiotically beneficial pact enabling them to
circumvent the arms embargo. Means of transportation under the direct control of
Libyan armed groups and their affiliates and the reliance on smuggling as a
procurement strategy by both the Government of National Unity and LAAF hindered
the implementation of the embargo. A previously identified network of shell
companies, operated by two identified individuals, was responsible for multiple
transfers, including transfer attempts intercepted by Italy in 2024 and Spain in 2025.
Various types of military and civilian dual-use items diversified the arsenals of
armed groups. Given their more accessible nature, dual-use items were increasingly
procured and utilized. Their indirect procurement channels and lower regulatory
controls enabled armed groups to acquire such items as force multipliers.
The scale and level of the organization of illicit petroleum exports – both crude
oil and refined products – reached unprecedented levels during the reporting period.
This was only feasible due to the direct involvement of Ibrahim Al Dabiba and
Saddam Haftar, both of whom provided an umbrella of impunity for individuals that
effectively stripped the National Oil Corporation of its capacity to safeguard the
interests of the State of Libya. As a result, the oil and fuel sector became characterized
by a system of impunity, in which export and import transactions, exploitation
agreements and service contracts were routinely rubber-stamped to serve the interests
of competing networks affiliated with armed groups. A key by-product of this system
is the Arkenu Oil Company, which was used as a Trojan horse to divert more than
$3 billion between January 2024 and November 2025.
The Panel identified a criminal network led by Maeen Ali Sharfeddin, who
established a multilayered operation with international reach, capable of controlling
the supply chain; diverted refined petroleum products at ports and other locations;
ensured unhindered access for vessels entering and departing Libya without
interference from law enforcement; and evaded any form of accountability.
Illicit exports of a variety of refined petroleum products expanded from
Benghazi to other ports in eastern Libya. New export infrastructure was installed in
commercial sections of Benghazi and Ras Lanuf harbours. Tubruq port emerged as a
grey market for illicit exports, with port services and documentation creating a
semblance of regularity to feign the appearance of legitimate exports. None of these
export locations are under the control of the National Oil Corporation.
By the end of the reporting period, the newly introduced reinvestment
exemption contained in paragraph 14 of Security Council resolution 2769 (2025) had
still not been fully operationalized, with only one exemption request and one request
for guidance having been made under that paragraph. Member States and relevant
financial institutions indicated to the Panel that they faced interpretative and
operational challenges in implementing the paragraph. The Panel supported the
Security Council Committee established pursuant to resolution 1970 (2011)
concerning Libya in efforts to provide guidance to all Member States for the effective
implementation of this new provision concerning the reinvestment of the Libyan
Investment Authority’s frozen cash reserves in low-risk time deposits and fixed-
income instruments.

I. Background
A. Introduction
1. The present final report, provided to the Security Council pursuant to paragraph 23
of resolution 2769 (2025), covers the period from 26 October 2024 to 14 February
2026, following the end of the reporting period of the Panel’s previous report
(S/2024/914).1 It includes updates on ongoing investigations detailed in its previous
report. A table of abbreviations and acronyms can be found in annex 1. An overview
of the evolution of the sanctions regime concerning Libya can be found in annex 2.
2. In conducting its investigations, the Panel complied with the best practices and
methods recommended by the Informal Working Group of the Security Council on
General Issues of Sanctions (see S/2006/997). The Panel maintained the highest
achievable standard of proof.
3. To counteract any risk to the Panel’s objectivity, impartiality and independence,
the Panel adapted its methodology to fast-developing new technologies, including the
use of artificial intelligence. The Panel continued to treat open-source media only as
lead information and complemented existing corroborative methods with additional
evidentiary elements, especially in the establishment of violations and instances of
non-compliance.
4. The Panel relied on corroborated evidence and adhered to its standards in
respect of the opportunity to reply. Further information on methodology can be found
in annex 3. The Panel maintained transparency, objectivity, impartiality and
independence in its investigations.
5. The annexes are being circulated mostly in the language of submission and
without formal editing. Owing to the word limits on reports of monitoring
mechanisms, the Panel provides details relating to investigations in the annexes.
B. Cooperation with stakeholders and institutions
6. Member States, organizations and individuals consulted are listed in annex 4.
The correspondence records of the Panel are in annex 5. The Panel submitted 50
letters with updates or analysis to the Committee on issues of interest. The Panel
travelled to 14 Member States in the implementation of its mandate. It also, through
in-person meetings and electronic platforms, maintained contact with Member States
and other interlocutors, including other Panels of Experts and the Analytical Support
and Sanctions Monitoring Team pursuant to Security Council resolutions 1526 (2004)
and 2253 (2015) concerning Islamic State in Iraq and the Levant (Da’esh), Al-Qaida
and the Taliban and associated individuals and entities. The Panel benefited from
logistical support provided by the United Nations Support Mission in Libya. The
Panel also had exchanges with the European Union military operation in the
Mediterranean (Operation IRINI).
7. Following the Panel’s appointment under resolution 2769 (2025), the Panel
immediately, on 4 April 2025, submitted a request to the Libyan authorities for the
issuance of visas. In the absence of a response, the Panel resubmitted the request on
22 August 2025. On 7 October 2025, responsible Libyan authorities granted three-
month multiple-entry visas to the Panel experts. The Panel travelled to Libya from
16 to 26 November 2025, where it met with the Libyan authorities and other relevant
interlocutors in Tripoli from 16 to 23 November and met with the Libyan Arab armed
__________________

forces (LAAF) in Benghazi from 24 to 26 November. The Panel met with 24 different

II. Acts that threaten the peace, stability or security of Libya or
obstruct or undermine the successful completion of its
political transition
A. Libyan armed groups dynamics
1. Key developments in the security landscape of Tripoli
8. On 12 May 2025, Abdelghani Al-Kikli, also known as Ghneiwa, the former head
of the Stability Support Apparatus, was killed during an operation coordinated by the
444 Brigade at its Takbali camp in Tripoli. This operation formed part of a broader
campaign against the Stability Support Apparatus, which involved coordinated action
by armed groups mobilized in support of the Prime Minister, Abdulhamid Al Dabiba.
The Panel found that the killing of Al-Kikli amounted to an arbitrary deprivation of
life under international human rights law (see para. 55 below). The operation was
coordinated by the 444 Brigade and carried out under the direct instruction of
Abdulhamid Al Dabiba, in his capacity as Minister of Defence, and General
Mahmoud Hamza, commander of the 444 Brigade.
9. His killing was immediately followed by an organized military operation to take
control of the Stability Support Apparatus headquarters in Abu Salim and other
strategic locations under its control.2 An attempt was subsequently made to dismantle
the Deterrence Apparatus for Combating Organized Crime and Terrorism and
establish control over its stronghold in Suq al-Jum‘ah district (Tripoli). In contrast to
the coordinated operation against the Stability Support Apparatus, the actions against
the Deterrence Apparatus were initiated by Mahmoud Hamza,3 who has a long-
standing personal conflict with the Deterrence Apparatus leader Abdelraouf Kara.
This operation was undertaken without consensus and preparation among armed
groups aligned with the Government of National Unity, enabling the Deterrence
Apparatus to mobilize local support within its area of influence and resist effectively.4
Clashes continued until mid-May, when a stalemate was reached. Thereafter, tensions
remained high within Tripoli and no significant progress towards a sustainable
agreement had been reached.
10. Armed groups opportunistically exploited the consecutive episodes of violence
to advance their interests. They sought control over State institutions, reinforcing
their position not only within the broader security landscape, but also within their
existing alliances. Control over the physical premises of State institutions represented
both economic and political opportunities: it (a) enabled revenue-generation through
the implementation of racketeering schemes and (b) allowed armed groups to act as
gatekeepers, regulating access to the institutions under their control. During the May
2025 events, the Joint Operation Force, a Misratah-based armed group, took control
of the National Oil Corporation headquarters and two facilities of the Libyan
Intelligence Service, namely, its Sabaa headquarters and a Tripoli training facility
(see annex 6).
11. The Stability Support Apparatus under Al-Kikli’s leadership had established a
network of security agencies, reinforcing a system of coercion and racketeering,
__________________

6/299 26-02825

S/2026/224
aimed at controlling key State institutions.5 The military operation did not result in a
restoration of the rule of law. Instead, it reshaped the armed-group landscape in
western Libya, resulting in, inter alia, growing tensions between Misratan-dominated
armed groups and other actors in the western region and increased domination over
Tripoli and State institutions by Misratan armed group leaders. In that regard, Ibrahim
Al Dabiba was the fulcrum of the security, political and economic decisions taken by
his uncle, the Prime Minister, Abdulhamid Al Dabiba. This enabled him to consolidate
his position as one of the key armed group actors (see annex 7).6
(a) Polarized western Libya
12. Since the clashes in May 2025, geographic and political tensions among western
Libyan armed groups have escalated due to rivalry between Misratah- and Tripoli-
aligned forces and armed groups from Zawiyah to the Tunisian border. Details about
the growing tensions along territorial lines among armed groups in western Libya,
which resulted in increasing instability, can be found in annex 8.
(b) New rule among Tripoli-based armed groups
13. Prior to the killing of Al-Kikli, fighters from Misratah had been building up their
capacity and numbers within Tripoli and Tajurah (approximately 25 km south-east of
Tripoli). These forces were primarily composed of the Joint Operation Force, which
further reinforced its positions following the May 2025 events. This armed group has
established itself in Tripoli ever since, controlling security around the National Oil
Corporation headquarters and exerting pressure on its leadership to obtain service
contracts (see annex 6).
14. The events of May 2025 further highlighted the role of Ibrahim Al Dabiba, who
directly sponsored a recruitment campaign supporting the offensive against the
Deterrence Apparatus for Combating Organized Crime and Terrorism. The Panel
found that this recruitment campaign was launched in Misratah to enlist fighters with
weekly payments ranging between 700 and 5,000 Libyan dinars (equivalent to 129
and 921 dollars, respectively), depending on whether recruits possessed a vehicle or
weapons.7 At the time of writing, some of these recruits were based in the Andalus
neighbourhood of Tripoli, where Abdulhamid and Ibrahim Al Dabiba reside. The
degree of control over these newly recruited forces remained volatile, with efforts
under way to integrate some of them under the Ministry of Defence and the Ministry
of the Interior.
15. The influence of Ibrahim Al Dabiba extended beyond armed groups, as he
assumed the role of kingmaker in the light of his security, political and economic
functions. He played a central role in several pivotal developments in Libya, including
through his direct involvement in the agreement between the Al Dabibas and the
Haftars, leading to the establishment of the Arkenu deal (paras. 98 and 104–106). His
sway over key economic sectors was based on his alliance with multiple armed group
leaders, with Al-Kikli, in the past, and, more recently, with Abdulsalam Al-Zobi.
16. Hailing from Misratah, Al-Zobi became the most prominent figure among the
Tripoli-based armed groups. He exerted his influence to generate revenue, both
through racketeering schemes and fuel smuggling (see annex 9, sect. D), positioning
himself strategically within the military structure. He established and retained
command of 111 Brigade, whose members have been in control of the southern part
__________________

S/2026/224
of Tripoli ever since. His influence grew after he was appointed by Abdulhamid Al
Dabiba as undersecretary of the Ministry of Defence. Despite his lack of military
background, he held the power to greenlight military operations in western Libya and
to decide on assignments, appointments and promotions within the Libyan Army. This
was manifested in his direct administration of the military salary system and his
objection to the implementation of a salary system that would threaten these revenue-
generating schemes.
(c) Obstruction of a centralized salary system
17. In late August 2025, the Ministry of Finance, in collaboration with the Central
Bank of Libya, began implementing a long-standing project to automate the
disbursement of public sector salaries. Under this system, the Central Bank pays
salaries directly to public sector employees, removing the Ministry of Finance from
the payroll management process. Each employee’s salary is linked to a unique
identification number in the Central Bank database.
18. The primary objectives of this system are to: (a) simplify the payment of salaries
within the public sector; (b) strengthen control over public expenditure, where
salaries account for approximately 65 per cent of the national budget; and (c) combat
identity theft and the existence of ghost or fraudulent employees.
19. Since the announcement of the implementation of the system, three main
stakeholders refused to cooperate and actively sought to obstruct it. The Government
of National Stability declared its refusal on the grounds of protecting the privacy
rights of its workforce against data collection. A similar position was adopted by the
Ministry of the Interior and the Ministry of the Defence of the Government of
National Unity. As national identity cards are State-issued, the Panel assessed the data
protection arguments to be irrelevant.
20. The armed group leaders Imad Trabelsi and Abdulsalam Al-Zobi, who exercised
de facto control over the Ministry of the Interior and the Ministry of Defence,
respectively, engaged in coordinated pressure tactics, including threats, with the aim
of keeping their respective administrations outside the new system.
21. Following his appointment as undersecretary of the Ministry of Defence and
promotion to the rank of colonel by Abdulhamid Al Dabiba in July 2024, one of
Al-Zobi’s first acts was to transfer almost 1,000 soldiers to the Border Guards and
Critical Facilities Directorate under its command and reassign all payments of salaries
(see annex 10 and para. 72) under the administration of 111 Brigade.8 The measure
granted this armed group direct control over salary disbursement and reinforced
Al-Zobi’s grip over security forces beyond 111 Brigade, effectively by-passing the
formal military structure and the authority of the Chief of the General Staff.
2. Efforts by the Libyan Arab armed forces to assert control over
smuggling activities
22. On 11 January 2025, the LAAF general command issued a decree dissolving
128 brigade, previously commanded by Hassan Al-Zadma, and reconstituted it as 15
brigade, which was subsequently redeployed to Jufrah. The Panel found that this
decision was taken to curtail Al-Zadma’s growing influence in southern Libya. Senior
LAAF leadership exhibited unease over Al-Zadma’s expanding power base, including
his reliance on support from the Awlad Sulayman tribe, his high degree of operational
autonomy and his direct engagement with foreign actors.9 These foreign engagements
__________________
8 Confidential sources (Libyan officials).
9 Confidential sources (armed group members).
8/299 26-02825
[[page 9]]
S/2026/224
enabled Al-Zadma to, inter alia, provide logistical support to the Rapid Support
Forces in the Sudan (see paras. 32-35).
23. Prior to Al-Zadma’s dismissal, local armed groups had generated sustained
revenues from cross-border smuggling activities, primarily of fuel, arms and
narcotics, coordinated under his supervision. Following Al-Zadma’s removal, all
areas previously under his control were taken over by Saddam Haftar; 87 battalion
assumed control over border zones and key smuggling routes in southern Libya. The
groups loyal to Al-Zadma were expelled from restructured smuggling networks,
which were placed under the authority of newly appointed commanders loyal to
Saddam Haftar.10 These measures constituted a systematic dismantling of Al-Zadma-
aligned networks and a reassertion of centralized control over trafficking corridors
linking Libya to the Niger, Chad and the Sudan.
24. Similar dynamics emerged in eastern and south-eastern Libya, notably in
Kufrah, which represents a strategic area for LAAF leadership, given its proximity to
international borders and its role in transborder movements, including of fighters and
goods. As of December 2025, Kufrah airport was under the de facto control of Subul
al-Salam battalion, reinforced by elements of 166 brigade and 87 battalion.11 This
reinforcement indicated the disquietude of LAAF regarding Subul al-Salam’s
growing influence in the Kufrah area, which the group consolidated through its tribal
ties with the local Tubu population. The deployment of these LAAF land forces to
Kufrah further demonstrated Saddam Haftar’s effort to contain the influence of Subul
al-Salam and prevent the group from developing such operational autonomy. Subul
al-Salam’s ability to maintain independent international contacts and engage in
transborder trafficking and smuggling activities constituted a direct challenge to the
authority of LAAF in the Kufrah area. The reinforced presence of units directly under
the command of Saddam Haftar was intended to place Subul al-Salam under closer
supervision and guarantee exclusive LAAF control over strategic routes and
installations.
25. The Panel found that the overall LAAF strategy was systematically
implemented to ensure that revenue-generating areas were under the control of
fighters integrated into a chain of command led by a member of the Haftar family.
B. Regional dynamics
1. Security threats in the region posed by terrorist groups and individuals
Revenue-generating activities and financing networks (annex 11)
26. Although the Panel identified no terrorist attacks in Libya during the reporting
period, cells and individuals affiliated with listed terrorist entities, including Islamic
State in Iraq and the Levant-Libya (ISIL-Libya) (QDe.165) and the Organization of
Al-Qaida in the Islamic Maghreb (QDe.014), remained present in the Fazzan region
and continued to engage in criminal activities to generate revenue. Human trafficking
and smuggling became the main source of earnings,12 with terrorist groups exploiting
irregular migratory flows transiting through southern Libya.
27. Terrorist groups continued to exploit natural resources in the region. Gold
smuggling constituted a source of revenue for local groups and individuals linked to
listed terrorist entities, in particular Jama’a Nusrat ul-Islam wa al-Muslimin
(QDe.159). These activities relied on well-established transnational smuggling
__________________

11 Confidential sources (armed group members).
12 Confidential sources (from southern Libya).
26-02825 9/299

networks operating across southern Libya, Chad, the Niger and Mali. In addition,
cocaine trafficking along the same Sahelian routes13 – where groups affiliated with
Da’esh have increased their influence – remained an important source of funding.
28. Individuals and groups affiliated with listed terrorist entities operating in Libya
also increasingly used cryptocurrency to move and store funds,14 reflecting an evolution
in financial practices aimed at reducing traceability and evading oversight
mechanisms.
2. Recruitment, facilitation and movement of terrorist fighters
29. Terrorist cells affiliated with ISIL-Libya continued to play an active role in
recruitment activities in Libya. These cells leveraged their involvement in human
trafficking and smuggling to identify and transfer individuals with specific skill sets
to staff the ranks of terrorist groups operating in the Sahel region.15
30. The Panel newly identified three cases in which individuals linked to listed
terrorist entities established trade companies in Libya that were used as cover
structures to finance the movement of at least six fighters from the Syrian Arab
Republic to Libya in batches throughout 2025. These activities underscored the
continued relevance of Libya as a transit and facilitation hub for terrorist networks.
31. Terrorist groups operating in and through Libya increasingly used online
platforms and advanced digital tools, including artificial intelligence, to generate
targeted messages in support of their recruitment efforts. These practices enhanced
the reach and efficiency of recruitment activities, contributing to the continuance of
terrorist networks despite limited operational capabilities on the ground.
C. Regional security dynamics
1. Reconfiguration of Libya-based networks of support to the Rapid
Support Forces16
32. Following the dismissal of Al-Zadma (see para. 23), the logistical system of
support to the Rapid Support Forces, operating through southern Libya, underwent a
significant reconfiguration. The Panel found that, between January 2025 and January
2026, Subul al-Salam and its leader, Abdulrahman Hashim Al-Kilani, incrementally
consolidated control over supply chains used to deliver fighters, arms and related
materiel from Libya to the Rapid Support Forces in the Sudan. This control, combined
with knowledge of the region and tribal connections, enabled the group to use
multiple routes to support the Rapid Support Forces. In response to regional security
developments, Subul al-Salam and their Rapid Support Forces counterparts alternated
between routes, maintaining parallel logistical pathways to ensure continuity and
efficiency in the transportation of fighters, fuel, arms and related materiel in an
interconnected and centralized manner (see annex 12).
33. The Panel found that Subul al-Salam was involved across multiple stages of the
supply chain to the Rapid Support Forces. Subul al-Salam exercised functional
control over key logistical, security and facilitation components required to sustain
the transportation of fighters, fuel, arms and related materiel, including militarized
vehicles. In early June 2025, it supported the armed operations of the Rapid Support
Forces by deploying units on the ground, providing fighters to Rapid Support Forces-
__________________
13 Confidential sources (from Libya, Chad and Mali).
14 Confidential sources (including Libyan institutional sources).
15 Confidential sources.
16 Confidential sources (armed groups from Libya, the Sudan and Chad, and officials).
10/299 26-02825
[[page 11]]

affiliated factions, escorting them across Libyan territory and facilitating access to
fuel and spare vehicle parts. These actions culminated in the advance of the Rapid
Support Forces into the Uwaynat area. The Panel found that Subul al-Salam, through
its support to the Rapid Support Forces, weakened border security in southern Libya,
including through the facilitation of cross-border illicit activities and the
empowerment of non-State armed actors in the Fazzan region.
34. The continued flows of illicit arms (see para. 62) created a parallel market,
leading to the diversion and redirection of arms and related materiel destined for the
Rapid Support Forces to markets in Libya, the Niger and Chad, further entrenching a
war economy along the southern borders of Libya. The Panel documented one case
in which ammunition of a type previously documented by the Panel in a seizure of
materiel exported from Libya17 and later identified in Misratah – originally destined
for the Rapid Support Forces was diverted and resold to individuals involved in gold
trafficking in the Niger and linked to Da’esh (7.62 x 39 mm calibre, packaging marked
“7.62S 120PC 352” and locally referred to as “malleable [layinah] bullets”).
35. The Rapid Support Forces-affiliated fighters remained present in Libya during
the reporting period, resulting in armed clashes between the Sudanese parties on
Libyan territory, notably in June and November 2025. This presence went beyond
tactical incursions.18 The Panel assessed that the Rapid Support Forces benefited
from: (a) a rear base under the auspices of LAAF, located approximately 75 km south-
south-west of Kufrah, for the coordination of logistical operations from Libyan
territory; (b) access to the Maateen al-Sarrah airbase; and (c) the use of facilities
located near Kufrah airbase19 that served both as transit points for Colombian fighters
and as sites for the modification of vehicles imported through Libya (see para. 61).
From these facilities, joint movements for both militarized vehicles and Colombian
fighters (see para. 38) departed towards the Sudan.
2. Regional security cooperation and the projection of State-like authority in
southern Libya
36. On 13 November 2025, LAAF and the Chadian National Army announced the
formation of a joint force to secure the Libya-Chad border. The Libyan component of
this force was drawn from Subul al-Salam. This arrangement placed Subul al-Salam
in charge of controlling key routes used to supply the Rapid Support Forces through
Libya and Chad, largely across territory under the effective control of Tubu
communities. In practice, this amounted to an inter-State-like security arrangement,
operationalized by LAAF by means of local armed proxies, leveraging tribal ties to
exercise control over territory.
37. These types of arrangements reflect a broader trend20 in which LAAF is striking
security agreements with Member States to present itself as a legitimate security actor
and act as a State-like counterpart. Under the command of Saddam Haftar, LAAF has
increasingly imposed itself as a regional security provider, particularly in the areas of
counter-terrorism, border security and the control of cross-border movements of
individuals and goods.

17 S/2013/99, para. 171.
18 S/2024/914, annex 12.
19 24°14'38.9"N, 23°14'04.3"E.
20 S/2024/914, para. 29.

3. Foreign fighters, mercenaries
(a) Transfer of foreign fighters through Libya
38. Libyan territory was used for the transfer of mercenaries to other conflict areas
in the region. With the support of LAAF, through Subul al-Salam, Colombian fighters
transited through Libya to support the Rapid Support Forces in the Sudan, with
Kufrah serving as a key stopover (see para. 35). Libya functioned not only as a transit
point, but also as an operational, logistical and tactical base, further reinforcing
regional spillover dynamics. In the first half of November 2025, the Sudanese Armed
Forces conducted air strikes targeting shipments of vehicles and foreign fighters on
Libyan territory in an attempt to disrupt supply chains supporting the Rapid Support
Forces.21
(b) Syrian fighters as force multipliers
39. Syrian fighters remained present in Libya throughout the reporting period.22 In
western Libya, Syrian fighters were deployed at several locations in Tripoli, including
Takbali camp, Salah Eddine camp, the Abu Salim area, and Yarmouk military base,
as well as in Misratah. They participated in armed confrontations during the May
2025 events (see para. 9). As tensions escalated, Tripoli-based armed groups
mobilized Syrian fighters to bolster their military operations, reflecting a continued
reliance on foreign combatants as readily deployable force multipliers in times of
crisis. Persistent rivalries among Libyan armed groups sustained the presence of
Syrian fighters, whose availability reinforced volatility on the ground and undermined
efforts to advance stabilization and military reunification initiatives.
40. In eastern and southern Libya, the Panel found that Syrian fighters were present
at strategic military sites, including the Maateen al-Sarrah airbase,23 which was
significantly enhanced during the reporting period in support of elements that used to
be part of the private military company formerly known as ChVK Wagner.
4. Role of the 5+5 Joint Military Commission in military reunification
41. During the reporting period, the activities of the 5+5 Joint Military Commission
remained restricted to ceasefire-related matters (see annex 13). No concrete progress
was made towards the establishment of a unified joint military unit, and the
Commission’s work continued to focus on confidence-building measures and the
preservation of existing ceasefire arrangements.
42. The Chiefs of General Staff in both western and eastern Libya continued to play
largely symbolic roles, lacking the authority and influence necessary to oversee or
enforce substantive steps towards military reunification. This situation was further
compounded by the appointment of general Khaled Haftar as chief of staff in eastern
Libya and the recent death of the former Chief of the General Staff of the Libyan
Army, General Mohammed al-Haddad. These developments further reduced the
prospects for the chiefs of staff to exercise a meaningful coordination role in any
reunification process.
43. Members of the Commission continued to consider that military reunification
could not be advanced in the absence of political unity. This assessment contributed
to a restrictive interpretation of the Commission’s role, under which military
reunification was viewed as contingent upon broader political agreements rather than
__________________
21 Confidential sources (armed groups from Libya and the Sudan).
22 Confidential sources (armed groups and Syrian fighters).
23 21°41'21" N, 21°49'41" E.

as a process that could be pursued incrementally and separately through technical or
security sector arrangements.
44. The Panel observed growing interest among Libyan actors in the establishment
of joint military or security bodies. This interest appeared to be driven in part by
expectations that progress towards joint structures could facilitate a gradual
alleviation of arms embargo restrictions. The Panel also found that alternative or
parallel formulas were being explored to encourage military reunification outside the
Commission framework. However, in the absence of a unified command structure and
effective oversight mechanisms, such initiatives risked remaining ad hoc and created
incentives for attempts to circumvent the arms embargo under the guise of
coordination or cooperation.
D. Acts that violate applicable international human rights law or
international humanitarian law, or acts that constitute human
rights abuses
1. Acts that obstruct or undermine the completion of the elections in Libya
45. While the first round of municipal council elections took place in November
2024 as planned, the second round, scheduled for August 2025, was staggered across
three stages, in August, October and December 2025, due to several incidents that fell
within the scope of paragraph 11 of resolution of 2571 (2021).
46. The Panel identified at least 16 incidents across Libya in the lead-up to the
second round of municipal elections that partially took place in August 2025.24 These
incidents, considered together with other disruptions, obstructed or undermined the
municipal council elections. The Panel found that armed groups were directly and
indirectly involved with the day-to-day running of local authorities while playing a
key role in the organization of the municipal council elections by providing security
arrangements. Moreover, in the lead-up to municipal elections and thereafter,
disruptions were caused by the continuous attempts of Libyan political figures to
change municipal boundaries as currency to reward armed groups for their support,
allowing them to gain and maintain control over local governance.
47. The Panel found that all incidents identified involved perpetrators that were
either armed actors or security apparatuses operating under the de facto authority of
armed actors. They include, inter alia, forced closure of election centres; confiscation
of voter cards and ballot boxes; threats to the staff of the High National Elections
Commission by armed groups or their local representatives; and forced closure of and
direct attacks on the offices of the High National Elections Commission by armed
actors. Such acts also included threats to and the arrest of candidates and their families
who are not affiliated with the local armed actor in control, forcing some candidates
to withdraw and to relocate out of the municipality. More details on acts that
obstructed or undermined the elections can be found in annex 14.
2. Violations of international humanitarian law and international human rights law
committed in the context of deprivation of liberty
48. Acts that threaten peace, security and stability in Libya persisted on a large scale
with flagrant impunity. The lack of accountability contributed to an environment in
which perpetrators openly claimed responsibility for some of these acts. The extradition
of Osama Najim back to Libya in January 2025 contributed to a widespread perception
among victims and lawyers that international accountability mechanisms had been
__________________
24 Confidential sources (victims, witnesses, electoral actors).
26-02825 13/299
[[page 14]]
S/2026/224
ineffective and irrelevant.25 Despite the limitations in the domestic justice system and
erosion of trust in the ability of the international justice system to hold perpetrators
accountable, many victims still resorted to approaching the State prosecution system.
Rights-holders chose this avenue despite limited prospects for accountability and
reparations for the gross human rights violations that they had allegedly suffered.
49. The Panel investigated 14 incidents of serious violation of international
humanitarian law and international human rights law that had taken place in the
Tripoli detention facility controlled by the Deterrence Apparatus and the Internal
Security Agency, as well as in the LAAF-controlled detention facilities located in
Sabha, Sirte, Benghazi and Bayda’.26 Armed groups continued to systematically use
detention to:
(a) Harass, detain and torture to muzzle targeted civilians, including
journalists, activists and municipal election candidates, for expressing a dissenting
opinion against the status quo. This included three cases where electoral candidates
and their relatives were detained;
(b) Settle personal scores with civilians and political opponents in the
community;
(c) Exert and maintain control over the population outside the remits of the
Libyan State judicial institutions;
(d) Instil fear in the civilian population to frighten or coerce them into
compliance and impose any new changes stemming from shifts in armed groups
dynamics.
More details on violations of international humanitarian law and international human
rights law in the context of detention can be found in annex 15.
(a) Responsibility of the Deterrence Apparatus for combating organized crime
and terrorism
50. The Panel identified five cases of violations of international humanitarian law
and international human rights law committed by individuals under the effective
command of the Deterrence Apparatus. Violations continued to follow a consistent
pattern of unlawful deprivation of liberty; enforced disappearance; incommunicado
detention; torture, cruel, inhuman and degrading treatment, including the withholding
or denial of adequate healthcare and sexual abuse; violations of the right to privacy;
denial of fair trial rights; and arbitrary deprivation of life, in one case due to torture
and medical negligence.
51. As previously reported by the Panel, the detention of individuals relied on the
employment of a coercive quasi-judicial system that still lacked any independent
judicial oversight.27 Victims and witnesses continued to identify Osama Najim as
responsible for administering and facilitating unlawful arrest, the mistreatment of
detainees in the Mitiga detention facility, the use of Libyan and foreign detainees
alike for forced labour, and recruitment of detainees. The recruitment was part of the
effort of the Deterrence Apparatus to create a digital army. Members of the Deterrence
Apparatus continued to threaten victims and witnesses for providing their testimonies.

(b) Responsibility of the Internal Security Agency in Tripoli
52. In one case documented by the Panel, activist Abdel Mun’im Al-Maryami was
arbitrarily detained by the Internal Security Agency in Sorman, west of Tripoli, on
30 June 2025. On 4 July 2025, his death was announced under suspicious
circumstances while in custody at the office of the Attorney General, after he had
been handed over by the Internal Security Agency. In an official statement, the
Attorney General said that Al-Maryami had been handed over to his office on 3 July
2025 and that he had been due to be released, when he fell down the stairwell shaft
and sustained life-threatening injuries that led to his death on 4 July 2025. With all
evidence considered in relation to his treatment and the detention conditions, the
Panel found that his detention and subsequent death amounted to violations of, among
other things, the prohibition of arbitrary and unlawful deprivation of liberty, inhuman
and degrading treatment, detainees’ fair trial rights and the prohibition of arbitrary
deprivation of life.28
(c) Responsibility of the Libyan Arab armed forces
53. The Panel investigated eight cases of arbitrary and unlawful detention, enforced
disappearance, torture and other ill-treatment, denial of fair trial and right to privacy,
and the arbitrary deprivation of life in temporary and permanent places of detention
under the control of LAAF. These included Gernada detention facility in Bayda’,
Kweifiya detention facility in Benghazi, and a detention facility under the exclusive
control of the Tariq bin Ziyad brigade in Sidi Faraj in Benghazi.
54. In three cases, detainees were subjected to systemic and brutal beatings to
extract confessions that led to their deaths in custody within days to a couple of
months. One additional case documented is in relation to the unlawful detention of a
minor. In two of the cases, the LAAF authority in charge filmed detainees during their
time in detention, in violation of their right to privacy. This propensity to film
detainees in their custody is consistent with a widely used practice by LAAF units;
on 5 May, a short video emerged purportedly showing Ibrahim Al-Dersi, a member
of the House of Representatives, shackled around the neck and exhibiting visible
signs of torture. The fate of Al-Dersi has remained unknown since his arbitrary
detention in May 2024.29
3. Violations of international humanitarian law and international human rights law
committed in the context of the May 2025 events
55. On 12 May 2025, Abdelghani Al-Kikli, former head of the Stability Support
Apparatus, was killed alongside seven of his guards in Takbali camp in Tripoli under
the control of 444 Brigade.30 The Panel found that the killing of Al-Kikli and his
guards amounted to an arbitrary deprivation of life. The act was carried out under the
direct instruction of Abdulhamid Al Dabiba, in his capacity as Minister of Defence,
and Mahmoud Hamza, commander of the 444 Brigade. The Panel also reviewed video
evidence of members of the 444 Brigade mistreating the dead bodies of those killed
individuals.31 Five other of Al-Kikli’s guards survived the attack but sustained gunshot
wounds during the face-off and have remained forcibly disappeared ever since. In
response to the Panel’s findings, the Government of National Unity confirmed the
killing of Al-Kikli alongside other Stability Support Apparatus elements who

with its findings. The Government of National Unity also stated that an investigation
was still ongoing. Details of the response of the Government of National Unity to the

56. During immediate subsequent operations to seize control of Abu Salim, any
civilians who were deemed to be aligned with Al-Kikli were attacked in the hours and
days after his killing. These attacks were conducted by armed military elements, some
of whom belonged to 444 Brigade. These attacks included attempts at deprivation of
liberty, and the ransacking, burning and destruction of private property, forcing many
to flee Abu Salim.
57. Armed confrontations ensued between the 444 Brigade and the Deterrence
Apparatus that persisted for several days, leading to approximately a dozen casualties,
including civilians. In one incident, a projectile landed on Mitiga detention facility,
leading to the death of one detainee and the injury of others. The Panel found that the
Deterrence Apparatus had launched attacks from next to Mitiga detention facility on
13 May 2025, exposing the area to direct attacks. Irrespective of who attacked first,
under international humanitarian law, direct attacks on civilians and civilian objects,
such as prisoners and prisons, are prohibited. Moreover, launching attacks from next
to a detention facility violates the obligation to take the necessary precautions to
protect civilians and civilian objects from the dangers resulting from military
operations, including by avoiding locating, to the maximum extent feasible, military
objectives within or near densely populated areas.32
III. Arms embargo
A. Overview
58. Pursuant to paragraphs 9 to 13 of resolution 1970 (2011), as modified by
subsequent resolutions, the Panel monitored the arms embargo and investigated and
identified violations33 and instances of non-compliance.34 The Panel identified 15
incidents of the transfer of arms and related materiel and three cases of military
training activities in violation of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011).35
59. In many aspects, the arms embargo continued to be stripped of substantive
impact and rendered largely symbolic, as Libyan armed actors and some Member
States alike openly flouted the arms embargo and announced military cooperation
with armed actors in the west and east. The Panel found that, since 2022, both sides
__________________
32 Customary international humanitarian law; article 58 of Additional Protocol I of 1977 to the
Geneva Conventions of 12 August 1949.
33 The Panel considers “a violation” to have occurred when there has been a physical transfer of
arms and military materiel, training, or the provision of materiel support that is not explicitly
covered by exemptions or exceptions to the arms embargo.
34 An instance of “non-compliance” refers to those occurrences where an entity has not taken the
appropriate action, as contained in the resolution, for example: (a) to prevent “a violation”, by not
inspecting aircraft or vessels bound for Libya; or (b) to provide required or requested information
to the Committee and/or its Panel. Non-compliance may also refer to a situation that objectively
is a violation, but where the responsible party could not reasonably be expected to (a) initiate an
exemption request or (b) have known at the time that a transfer would amount to a violation.

[[page 17]]
S/2026/224
had implemented a bilateral mutually beneficial arms procurement pact. The initiative
for that pact came from influential Libyan businessmen based in the United Arab
Emirates. It was negotiated between high-level representatives of the Government of
National Unity and LAAF, under the patronage of Ibrahim Al Dabiba and Saddam
Haftar. Under this arrangement, certain materiel, such as armoured vehicles, could be
jointly procured by the parties.36 This scheme allowed third parties to support Libyan
actors without compromising their relationship with either side. It also enabled LAAF
and Tripoli-based armed groups to receive military equipment under the cover of the
Government of National Unity, in violation of the arms embargo.37
60. Some Member States extended support across the political divide in Libya,
evidenced by the presence of identical uncrewed aerial vehicles and ground control
stations at Mitiga, Misratah and Khadim airbases (see annex 17). Armed groups
adopted new strategies to circumvent the arms embargo: (a) LAAF and the Deterrence
Apparatus attempted to use private jets to transport materiel; and (b) armed groups
across Libya utilized a shipping line controlled by the United Arab Emirates-based
dual national of Libya and Saint Kitts and Nevis, Ahmed Alushibe,38 which operated
its own vessels and containers, avoiding scrutiny by external parties. Interdictions of
embargoed cargo remained rare, even when transiting through third countries (see
recommendation 8).
61. During the reporting period, there was a growing utilization of dual-use39 items
by armed groups, which are put to military use after importation to Libya, with or
without being weaponized. This is particularly observable for soft-skin pickup trucks
and uncrewed aerial vehicles. A previously unseen number – over 2,500 in 2025
alone – of soft-skin Toyota Land Cruiser 70-type pickup trucks were imported to
Benghazi throughout 2025 in regular shipments. They were collected and processed
in a military facility in Kweifiya, 11 km east of Benghazi,40 and transferred onwards
to a conversion facility in Kufrah, where they were militarized, including for further
export to the Sudan (see para. 35).
62. Military and civilian cargo flights to military bases in eastern Libya at least
doubled between October 2024 and October 2025, compared with the previous
reporting period, especially on the United Arab Emirates-eastern Libya airbridge,
involving multiple airlines and flight paths, with some flights between Boosaaso,
Somalia and eastern Libya (see annex 18).
B. Implementation of new arms embargo exemptions and exceptions
under Security Council resolution 2769 (2025)
63. In paragraph 6 of resolution 2769 (2025), the Security Council introduced a new
exemption measure to the arms embargo permitting the provision of technical
assistance and training solely to promote the reunification process of Libyan military

and security institutions, as notified to the Committee in advance. Paragraph 6 was
invoked only twice, by one Member State. Panel engagements with relevant
stakeholders revealed that the reason for the rare reliance on paragraph 6 was twofold:
(a) disagreements among the Libyan parties on who should participate; and (b) to a
lower degree, the unclear role of the Presidential Council in the process. In one of the
two cases in which paragraph 6 was invoked, the notification was incomplete (see
table 3, annex 19).
64. In paragraphs 6 and 7 of resolution 2769 (2025), the Security Council also
introduced new provisions in relation to temporary entries into Libya of arms and
related materiel. The Panel revised its methodology accordingly, developing profile
indicators specifically for temporary entries of military aircraft and naval vessels, to
assist in determining the likelihood of violations and other occurrences, thus
narrowing the focus of Panel investigations (see annex 3, appendix 3.E).
65. Five Member States conducted 124 military cargo flights to Libya. The Panel
requested each to provide details about the purpose of the flights and the cargo
delivered. Two Member States indicated that no arms embargo-relevant items were
transported; one Member State responded that the flights were conducted to address
the logistics needs of its armed forces military advisers who were in Libya to provide
training, assistance and consultancy services to the Libyan armed forces in line with
the established legal framework;41 two did not respond (see annex 20).
66. Naval vessels of several Member States visited western and eastern Libyan
ports. The Panel identified the periodic visits of Turkish naval vessels to Khums as
falling under six profile indicators for temporary entries of naval vessels. Türkiye
responded that visits had been conducted to ensure the safety and security of Turkish
advisory and training personnel.42 The Panel assessed that the explanation given was
not commensurate with bi-weekly or monthly rotations and the almost constant
presence of two, sometimes three, Turkish naval vessels in Khums port. The Panel
found that these visits did not fulfil the exemption or exception requirements of
paragraphs 6 or 7 of resolution 2769 (2025), thus constituting a violation of paragraph 9
of resolution 1970 (2011) (see table 3).
67. None of these Member States provided any documentation that would allow the
Panel to establish whether paragraph 7 of resolution 2769 (2025) applied to these
temporary entries (table 3). Paragraph 7 de facto established an incentive for Member
States to employ military means of transport to evade being found in violation of the
arms embargo (see recommendation 2).
C. Maritime violations, non-compliance and other maritime issues
Maritime transfers
68. Larger, non-lethal military equipment, such as naval-type vessels and armoured
vehicles, continued to be transferred to Libya by sea by means of commercial carriers.
As in previous cases, the Panel noted a shirking of responsibility among the
commercial actors involved, which, for maritime transfers, almost always implicates
upwards of five parties. In exchanges with the Panel, parties repeatedly cited the long-
standing reputations of business partners, robust internal sanctions compliance
policies, and allegedly cogent documentation, which the Panel assessed as invalid
justification for serious due diligence shortcomings. Moreover, parties consistently
sought to evade responsibility by invoking the sanctity of contractual indemnity
__________________
41 See, however, S/2024/914, para. 81.
42 Letter dated 12 January 2026.
18/299 26-02825
[[page 19]]
S/2026/224
clauses and sanctions disclaimers. While such defences may bear relevance in
determining contractual obligations and civil liabilities, they are irrelevant in the
context of a United Nations arms embargo. In such commercial transfers, all parties,
State and non-State alike, become gatekeepers for the implementation of the embargo;
they are subject to an enhanced due diligence standard commensurate with their
respective roles and functions, and thus bear collective responsibility for violations.
In all cases investigated by the Panel, shell companies, nominal consignees and
entities previously reported by the Panel were used as shippers or consignees in cargo
manifests. These features constituted clear red flags that should have prompted the
parties to re-evaluate and apply strict compliance measures (see recommendations 3
and 9).
1. Vessel transfers (annex 21)
69. The Panel identified transfers of 23 vessels to Benghazi. Of these, it classified
five as fast patrol vessels and two as fast interceptor craft, and thus naval-type vessels,
owing to their design, structure and designation. Two types of vessels had been
exhibited as models of naval-type vessels, with gun mounts, at a naval defence
exhibition in April 2025.43 The Panel inspected fast patrol vessels of the same make
and model after their interception en route to Benghazi (see paras. 89 to 90).
70. All these transfers were organized by the network headed by Amro Salem Ismael
Ibrahim, who is based in the United Arab Emirates.44 This network was previously
found to be in violation of the arms embargo for vessel transfers.45 The Panel
identified an additional member of the network, Omar Mahmoud Hasan Mustafa.46
The Panel found these two individuals and several entities to be in violation of
paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011) for the transfer of the naval-type vessels, and
thus military equipment, to Libya (see tables 1 and 3). The Panel found several
instances that constituted non-compliance with paragraph 19 of resolution 2213
(2015) for failure to inspect the cargo vessels, including during transit, despite
indicators of prohibited items (see table 3). The Panel documented repeated
occurrences of such non-compliance,47 which significantly undermined the effective
implementation of the arms embargo (see recommendation 8).

2. Armoured vehicles: the case of MV Aya 1 (annex 22)
71. On 18 July 2025, Operation IRINI intercepted the MV Aya 1 (IMO 9293557)49
en route from Port Rashid, United Arab Emirates to Benghazi, Libya. Operation IRINI
identified missing cargo documentation and inspected, at sea, a sample of 6 containers
out of a total of 332. The inspection revealed 12 militarized vehicles in some of the
containers onboard. Based on photographic evidence provided by Operation IRINI,
the Panel assessed these vehicles as military equipment.
72. Operation IRINI contacted the carrier, which is ultimately controlled by Ahmed
Alushibe, regarding the cargo documentation and informed the Libyan Ministry of
Defence of the matter. The vessel was ordered to Astakos port, Greece, where it
arrived on 27 July 2025; however, no further inspections were carried out. The Libyan
Ministry of Defence claimed 154 of the vehicles on board by providing an end-user
certificate dated 6 May 2025, signed by the Prime Minister, Abdulhamid Al Dabiba,
in his capacity as Minister of Defence, and by the director of military procurement.
The Ministry of Defence contended that the Committee had approved a related arms
embargo exemption request for use of the vehicles by the Border Guards and Critical
Facilities Directorate. Subsequently, on 1 August 2025, Operation IRINI released the
vessel on the basis of its exchanges with the Libyan Ministry of Defence, on the
condition that it sail to Tripoli instead of Benghazi to unload the vehicles. The vessel
sailed towards Tripoli but changed course and called in at Misratah, Benghazi and
Tubruq.
73. The Panel is aware that the above-mentioned arms embargo exemption request
invoked by the Libyan Ministry of Defence, dated 29 July 2025, had been made under
paragraphs 2 (d) and 2 (e) of resolution 2769 (2025), thus simultaneously invoking
an exception and submitting a request for approval. The exception would only apply
to forces declared under the control of the Libyan Government; however, the intended
recipient, the Border Guards and Critical Facilities Directorate, which is under the
control of Abdulsalam Al-Zobi (see para. 16), has not been declared as such.50 No
approval was granted by the Committee as at the time of writing of the present report.
74. The end-user certificate was technically invalid, as one of the signatories was
not among those notified to the Committee at the time of notification.51 The list of
signatories was eventually updated in December 2025. The Ministry of Defence was
not the consignee indicated in the cargo manifest of any of the vehicles contained in
the end-user certificate. Should the Ministry of Defence be identified as a consignee,
it would face challenges in financing business transactions because a higher level of
due diligence is applied to it by the banking sector as opposed to the private
companies falsely identified as the recipient of the cargo.52
__________________
49 Flag State: Panama. Vessel later renamed as Zulfa 1.
50 See S/2024/914, para. 60.
51 See S/2024/914, para. 61.
52 Ministry of Defence, Tripoli, 22 November 2025.
20/299 26-02825
[[page 21]]

75. Of the 154 vehicles contained in the end-user certificate, most of which were
Toyota Land Cruiser 70-type-based, 123 were unloaded in Misratah and 31 in
Benghazi. An additional 86 armoured vehicles of the same base type were on board
the MV Aya 1 and offloaded in Misratah. Of these 86 armoured vehicles, the Panel
identified the Ministry of Defence and the Ministry of the Interior as consignees for
16 and 23 of them, respectively. The remaining consignees for these vehicles were:
(a) four automobile import companies in Misratah (21 vehicles) and (b) Al-Katiba 55
in Tripoli (26 vehicles), a Warshafana-based armed group reported by the Panel for
violations of international human rights law in the Mayah detention centre.53
76. The Panel assessed that this transfer: (a) included at least 240 vehicles subject
to the arms embargo;54 (b) was part of the procurement scheme described in paragraph
59 above, intended at circumventing the arms embargo; and (c) was implemented
through a purpose-built transportation channel under the control of Alushibe. The
transfer of the 154 vehicles contained in the end-user certificate and of the additional
86 armoured vehicles did not fall under the exception of paragraph 2 (d) of resolution
2769 (2025), as neither the Border Guards and Critical Facilities Directorate nor
Al-Katiba 55 were declared under the control of the Libyan government. It was also
not approved in advance under paragraph 2 (e) of resolution 2769 (2025). The transfer
of the 240 vehicles to Libya was thus a violation of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970
(2011) (see table 3). The Panel further assessed that, in relation to this transfer,
Operation IRINI failed to implement its mandate under paragraph 5 of resolution 2292
(2016), as amended by paragraph 2 of resolution 2733 (2024).
D. Arms and related materiel transfers
77. The Panel identified an additional 11 incidents of arms transfers55 into Libya
that constituted violations of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011) (see table 3).
Violations are presented in chronological order and in tabular format (see annex 23).
Infographics and details about the violations can be found in annexes 24 and 25.
Annex 26 contains a summary of the types of arms and military materiel transferred
into Libya in violation of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011) since the imposition
of the embargo. The summary illustrates the variety and technical complexity of the
weapons and military materiel now available in Libya and serves as a baseline to
assist in the identification of any future violations.
78. The Panel also identified three incidents of equipment transfers that appeared
on open-source platforms, but was unable to independently corroborate them beyond
geolocation or other means (see annex 27). This included the forces affiliated with
the Government of National Unity operating an advanced anti-tank guided missile
type in a military exercise and two armoured personnel carrier types previously
unidentified in western Libya.56
79. During a military parade held on 26 May 2025 at the “military city”, about 60 km
to the south-west of Benghazi, which was attended by numerous foreign military and
diplomatic representatives, including some at deputy ministerial levels, LAAF
boasted an extensive arsenal of arms and related materiel. It included an advanced

surface-to-air defence system, a large quantity of armoured personnel carriers, various
types of infantry fighting vehicles, a heavy-duty helicopter and a large number of
uncrewed aerial vehicles and electronic warfare systems.57 LAAF provided its views
to the Panel regarding the parade in the context of the arms embargo.58 The Panel
found that the justifications provided either did not apply or were not convincing;
rather, the statement illustrated the determination of LAAF to circumvent the arms
embargo. The Panel found LAAF to be in violation of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970
(2011) for several transfers (see annex 24).
E. Dual-use items
80. Compared with military equipment, dual-use items face lower regulatory
barriers and rely on commercial supply chains, making them not only more cost-
effective, but also more accessible for actors under an arms embargo. The Panel found
that dual-use items were customarily shipped from third countries, and rarely from
the producing country directly to Libya. This was also observable during the above-
mentioned military parade, at which LAAF displayed dual-use items with military
applications, such as: (a) soft-skin vehicles and civilian vessels used for mobility of
troops and materiel, as well as for providing a mobile platform for mounted
weaponry; (b) various types of uncrewed aerial vehicles for intelligence, surveillance,
target acquisition and reconnaissance roles, or as uncrewed combat aerial vehicles;
(c) anti-uncrewed aerial vehicle equipment for protecting military establishments; and
(d) all-terrain vehicles used for multiple purposes (see annex 24). The Panel assessed
that such items could not only close gaps in military capacity, but also serve as a
quantitative force projection.
81. When such dual-use items are employed for military purposes and the end-user
is an armed actor in Libya, the arms embargo applies. Furthermore, some dual-use
items could be qualified as falling under paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011), in the
category of paramilitary equipment (see recommendation 9).
F. Military training sessions
82. The Panel identified three military training sessions provided to Libyan armed
actors, two by Belarus and one by Italy, in violation of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970
(2011) (see annexes 28, 29 and 30). The Panel also identified five instances of training
provision that appeared on open-source platforms, but was unable to independently
corroborate them beyond geolocation or other means (see annex 31).
G. Aviation violations and incidents of non-compliance
1. Military transport aircraft
83. Egypt provided military flight assistance with four military transport aircraft to
LAAF paratroopers during the 26 May 2025 LAAF military parade. While temporary,
this provision of assistance did not fall under the exception of paragraph 7 of
resolution 2769 (2025) and thus constituted a violation of paragraph 9 of resolution
1970 (2011) (see table 3 and annex 32).

2. Civilian aircraft
84. During the previous reporting period, only one airline, Sapsan Airline, operated
on the United Arab Emirates-eastern Libya airbridge, using its two aircraft.59 In
comparison, during the period covered by the present report, 11 cargo aircraft
operated on this airbridge by four Kyrgyzstan-registered airlines, specifically Flysky
Airlines, New Way Cargo Airlines, Sapsan Airline and Skyline Airline. These airlines
conducted 458 flights, twice as many as identified during the previous reporting
period, via multiple flight paths including some between Boosaaso, Somalia and
Benghazi and Kufrah, Libya. Cargo flight arrivals identified at Kufrah airbase, which
had been seldom used for such purposes before February 2025,60 increased tenfold,
from 24 in 2024 to 239 in 2025.61 A hangar at the north end of the airbase was being
rebuilt as at the time of writing.
85. Flysky and Sapsan also conducted 30 flights between Türkiye and eastern Libya
from March to September 2025.
86. The Panel also newly identified an air route between Belarus and eastern Libya,
operated by six cargo aircraft of three Belarusian-registered airlines: Rada Airlines,
Rubystar Airways and TransAVIAexport Airlines. These airlines conducted 42 flights
between November 2024 and the end of October 2025, which coincided with the
scheduled training that took place for the 87 battalion of LAAF by Belarus.
87. An additional Belarusian commercial airline, Belavia Airlines, provided flights
for the 36 special operations forces battalion and 87 battalion of LAAF after they
completed training in Belarus.62 The Panel found that Belavia conducted an additional
38 flights using its four-passenger aircraft from Minsk to Benghazi in November 2024
and December 2025. Contemporaneously, both Belavia and the three above-
mentioned Belarusian airlines started to intensify their flight frequency to Benghazi
in line with the commencement of the training for 87 battalion in Belarus (see table 3
and annexes 28 and 29).
88. These flights met the Panel’s air delivery profile indicators (see annex 3), which,
when considered collectively, indicated a violation of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970
(2011) (see table 3 and annex 18).
H. Member State action: interception by Spain (annex 33)
89. On 27 August 2025, Spain, in implementing paragraph 19 of resolution 2213
(2015), intercepted the MV Lila Mumbai (IMO 9253143)63 en route from Port Rashid,
United Arab Emirates, to Benghazi, Libya, as the vessel was approaching Ceuta,
Spain, for provisioning. Subsequently, cargo consisting of 10 vessels was offloaded
in Algeciras, Spain (see table 2). The Panel inspected these vessels on 6 November
2025 and established that they consisted of: (a) two fast patrol vessels of the same
make and model that had been already transferred to Libya (see para. 69); (b) six fast
interceptor craft with gun mounts; and (c) two fast landing craft. Five of the
interceptor craft and the two fast landing craft were at different stages of completion,
indicating that shipbuilding work would be undertaken after arrival in Benghazi.
Owing to the vessels’ design, structure and designation, the Panel classified all as

shipyard but as a repair shop. This shows how “grey” outlets, posing as shipyards –
although without the requisite industrial licence – can be used as expendable front
companies for exporting embargoed items to keep the reputation of the producing
primary shipyard intact.65

2. Italy (annex 35)
92. On 20 and 21 May 2025, the Panel inspected the uncrewed aerial vehicle
components seized by the Italian authorities in June 2024.67 The Panel found that:
(a) the components consisted of two incomplete military grey fuselages with
associated wings and V-tail surfaces; (b) the fuselages had cabling, hydraulics and
fuel tanks; and (c) each wing contained hardpoints for the attachment of three pylons.
The components were disguised and misdeclared as wind power equipment.
93. The Panel assessed that the components were consistent with the Fei Long-1
(Flying Dragon-1 or FL-1) medium-altitude long-endurance uncrewed aerial vehicle.
Owing to the multi-role platform capabilities of the FL-1, including the possibility of
attaching pylons for payload mounting, the FL-1 components would be considered as
spare parts, whose delivery to Benghazi would have fallen within the scope of
paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011).
94. The Panel identified the involvement of Omar Mustafa (see paras. 70 and 90) as
representing the consignee, Danube Shipping Company (Benghazi), for the uncrewed
aerial vehicle components. The Panel identified the same company as consignee of
several vessels, including naval-type vessels, delivered and intended to be delivered
to Benghazi.68

95. The Panel identified an additional responsible party for a previously reported
violation of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011) for vessel transfers (see table 3 and
annex 36).
