IV. Petroleum: the most significant source of revenue for
armed groups
A. Framework of armed group control
97. The Panel previously highlighted how armed groups infiltrated the oil sector by
diverting State revenues derived from the main natural resource and source of income
of Libya.73 The Panel found that armed group actors,74 mainly Ibrahim Al Dabiba and
Saddam Haftar, developed and honed their capabilities to exert control over the
National Oil Corporation at every level of the decision-making process. The effect
was twofold: not only did it enable armed groups to expand their influence and
military capabilities, but it also affected the public interest by directly undermining
the permanence of the Libyan State by preventing its institutions from accessing oil
revenues.
98. Ibrahim Al Dabiba and Saddam Haftar struck a mutually beneficial deal by
creating the first prototype of a structure to access crude oil revenues, namely, Arkenu
Oil Company (hereinafter Arkenu).75 It was agreed that the primary beneficiary of
this first prototype would be LAAF, while Ibrahim Al Dabiba would be the main
beneficiary of a subsequent iteration of Arkenu. Ibrahim Al Dabiba fulfilled his end
of the deal by securing a direct share of oil revenues for LAAF. Considering LAAF
control over the areas where most Libyan oil fields are located, Saddam Haftar’s buy-
in was instrumental to bring Ibrahim Al Dabiba’s plan to fruition. The latter
subsequently exerted pressure on officials within the oil sector and played a central
role in the suspension of the Minister of Oil and Gas in March 2024 with the aim of
installing a replacement amenable to the implementation of the Arkenu deal.
However, once Arkenu came about, he was ultimately left without a supporting
counterpart, as Saddam Haftar and LAAF reneged on their promise to guarantee
control of “Arkenu 2.0”, namely, Juliana Company, once it had been created.76
99. More broadly, armed group actors instrumentalized violence – without
accountability – to provide an umbrella of impunity to individuals who, through their
__________________
73 See S/2024/914, paras. 94–114.
74 “Armed group actors” refers to individuals that wield power vis-à-vis armed groups, including
through directing or financing them.
75 See S/2024/914, para. 96.
76 Confidential sources (Libyan energy sector officials, armed group members).
26-02825 29/299
[[page 30]]
S/2026/224
technical expertise and established networks, could generate ever-increasing streams
of revenue. In a symbiotic relationship, these individuals actively furthered the
interests of armed group actors and vice versa. In the oil and fuel sector, this climate
of impunity led to routine rubber-stamping of export and import transactions,
exploitation agreements and service contracts in order to serve competing networks
supported by armed group actors. This resulted in the systemic inability of State
institutions, including the National Oil Corporation and Brega Petroleum Marketing
Company, to effectively monitor and/or control crude oil exports, crude oil revenues,
imports, distribution and exports of refined petroleum, and domestic fuel
consumption. With all relevant institutions dragooned into operating in an
environment that shielded and enabled these individuals, the prospect of any
meaningful oversight of the oil sector all but vanished. Consequently, accountability
mechanisms were rendered ineffective, allowing illicit practices to thrive.
100. Oil revenues provided an illustrative example. The Libyan State budget depends
on hydrocarbon revenues for more than 90 per cent of its income.77 The above-described
situation prevented the relevant institutions from obtaining a comprehensive and
accurate account of oil production levels and the revenues generated therefrom. In
2025, the amount of oil revenue entering Libya stood at $18.78 billion (excluding
royalties and taxes), despite the suspension of the offset procedure in April 2025.78
On the basis of production levels79 and the average Brent crude oil price, estimated
revenues should have been around $27 billion.
B. Farhat Bengdara, Al Masraf Bank and the National
Oil Corporation
101. Farhat Bengdara was serving as Chairman of the Arab Bank for Investment and
Foreign Trade (United Arab Emirates), better known as Al Masraf Bank, when he was
appointed Chairman of the National Oil Corporation in 2022. His appointment was
backed by the Haftar family, following his role in facilitating the diversion of
$300 million from an Al Masraf bank account belonging to the Libyan Foreign Bank
in 2019 to support the Libyan National Army’s offensive against Tripoli, including
through the financing of Project Opus.80 He retained both chairmanship positions,
despite the conflict of interest.
102. The Panel found that under Bengdara’s leadership, the National Oil Corporation
was subjected to the increasing control of armed group actors through deliberate
financial, contractual and governance practices that thwarted institutional oversight.
These practices included an attempt to pressure National Oil Corporation subsidiaries
to channel operational funds through Al-Masraf Bank, restructuring the National Oil
Corporation to nullify internal checks and balances, and directing oil services
contracts toward entities controlled by or associated with armed group actors, in
particular Saddam Haftar and Ibrahim Al Dabiba. Detailed findings on Farhat
Bengdara can be found in annex 9, section B.
103. In response to the Panel’s findings, Bengdara explained that he had no oversight
in relation to the respective funds held at Al Masraf Bank. However, he explained that
those funds were attached to a loan to three companies based in the United Arab
Emirates, without providing details. Bengdara further explained that he had played a
__________________
77 See International Monetary Fund, “IMF Executive Board concludes the 2025 article IV
consultation with Libya”, press release No. 25/217, 25 June 2025.
78 Confidential sources (Libyan officials).
79 National Oil Corporation, “NOC Achieves Highest Average Crude Oil Production in a Decade”,
1 January 2026.
80 Confidential sources (Libyan officials); and S/2021/229, paras. 86–92 and annex 76.
30/299 26-02825
[[page 31]]
S/2026/224
peripheral role in the conclusion of both Arkenu contracts and that they had been
approved by the Government of National Unity. Based on the cumulative and
substantial evidence collected by the Panel, his claims were found not to be credible.
C. Rafat Al-Abbar and the Arkenu deal
104. An individual called Rafat Al-Abbar,81 acting as a proxy for Saddam Haftar,
played a central role in establishing and enforcing the contractual relationship
between the National Oil Corporation and Arkenu, in contravention of Libyan law
and contrary to the interests of the Libyan State. Al-Abbar exercised de facto control
over decision-making within the National Oil Corporation during and after the
Bengdara era by establishing a shadow governance structure, obstructing oversight
mechanisms and ensuring compliance through pressure exerted at multiple
institutional levels (see annex 9, section C). The Panel found that Arkenu – under the
control of Saddam Haftar – diverted more than $3 billion in oil revenues to bank
accounts outside Libya,82 failed to pay taxes due to the State and did not comply with
core contractual obligations, particularly investment commitments and limits on
authorized export volumes.
105. The Panel found that the Arkenu contract was implemented in a manner that
systematically undermined National Oil Corporation oversight and enabled large-
scale illicit oil exports. Despite a contractual obligation to invest $1 billion in the
Sarir and Mesla oil fields, only a marginal portion of this amount was invested, and
production declined rather than increased (see figure I). Moreover, amendments to
the contract fundamentally altered its terms to the benefit of Arkenu, allowing it to
export quantities far exceeding contractual limits, amounting to at least 42.1 million
barrels between January 2024 and December 2025. These exports were facilitated
through established international traders and newly created broker companies. The
Panel further found that Arkenu served as a prototype for similar arrangements, to be
used as a Trojan horse to feign private investment, enabling armed group actors to
illicitly export petroleum. The Panel found a correlation between Arkenu’s operations
and the increased military capabilities of LAAF, which posed a direct threat to the
political transition, peace, security and stability in Libya. Detailed findings on Rafat
Al-Abbar and Arkenu Oil Company can be found in annex 9, section C (see also
recommendations 1, 10 and 11).
106. During a meeting with the Panel,83 Al-Abbar denied that he played any role in
the oil sector beyond advising the National Oil Corporation chairman and
representing the business interests of the National Oil Corporation vis-à-vis a Member
State. Based on the irrefutable and substantial amount of evidence collected by the
Panel, his claims were found not to be credible.

D. Illicit maritime exports of refined petroleum
107. Since the Panel’s previous report,84 illicit maritime exports of refined petroleum
have continued unabated, and have expanded from their epicentre, Benghazi old
harbour, to commercial terminals at Tubruq port and Ras Lanuf harbour, none of
which are under the control of the National Oil Corporation. Between October 2025
and January 2026, Benghazi old harbour was upgraded significantly. While diesel
remained the primary illicitly exported product, less refined products were also
illicitly exported.
108. The Panel observed two modi operandi: (a) clandestine exports from all three
above-mentioned locations, using (i) evolving obfuscation techniques, (ii) new ship-
to-ship transfer locations and (iii) on-site storage in Port Said, Egypt; and (b) grey-
market exports from Tubruq port involving unsuspecting carriers, professional port
services, cargo documentation and certificates of origin. Since November 2024, at
least 50 tanker vessels have illicitly exported diesel and other refined petroleum
products from Libya, amounting to around 992,636 metric tons; diesel from Benghazi
old harbour alone amounted to 578,000 metric tons (688 million litres). These
numbers include only identified tankers and voyages.
__________________
84 S/2024/914, paras. 106–110 and annexes 62–65.
32/299 26-02825
[[page 33]]
S/2026/224
1. Clandestine exports
109. Illicitly exported diesel was sold mainly as bunker fuel at ship-to-ship locations;
some tanker shipments were delivered to shore in Egypt. Sporadically, shipments by
tankers reached Berbera, Somalia and Port Sudan, the Sudan, and one shipment was
destined for a location in South America. Shipments by container reached the Syrian
Arab Republic, Türkiye and the United Arab Emirates (see paras. 114 and 115).
110. These exports were predominantly undertaken: (a) by tanker vessels controlled
by the previously reported85 criminal network (see paras. 117 to 120); and (b) in
containerized flexi-tanks (paras. 113 to 115).
2. Grey market exports
111. The Panel found: (a) often-incomplete cargo documentation revealing red flags;
(b) fraudulent and invalid origin certificates issued by the Tubruq Chamber of
Commerce, a non-authorized body; and (c) uneconomical and unusual cargo loading
from tanker trucks, despite the existence of an official National Oil Corporation
maritime petroleum terminal nearby. The Panel therefore assessed that most carriers
were either complicit in or indifferent to their involvement in illicit exports.
112. The Panel found illicit exports undertaken by the Arabian Gulf Oil Company
and Benghazi-based oil companies for 28 deliveries to Greece, consisting of kerosene (1)
and very-low-sulfur fuel oil (27). The Panel also identified shipments of very-low-
sulfur straight-run fuel oil to Egypt (2), Belgium (3), Germany (2), Spain (1) and
Malta (1).
3. Containerized exports
113. The Panel newly identified illicit exports of refined petroleum products using
flexi-tanks loaded in containers, transported either by unknowing or complicit
carriers.
114. With respect to unknowing carriers, the Panel identified shipments of at least 99
containers from Benghazi destined for the Syrian Arab Republic, Türkiye and the
United Arab Emirates. The petroleum was misdeclared as paint solvent or metal scrap;
one sample revealed a mix of diesel and Jet A-1 fuel.
115. With respect to complicit carriers, the Panel identified petroleum transfers from
Tubruq and Benghazi to the United Arab Emirates by the MV Aya 1 (IMO 9293557),
ultimately controlled by Ahmed Alushibe (see paras. 60 and 72), including a transfer
of at least 22 containers with flexi-tanks filled with heavy fuel oil.
116. A detailed account of the investigations related to illicit exports of refined
petroleum products can be found in annex 9, section F.
E. Criminal network growing through illicit export of petroleum
117. A previously identified criminal network86 continued to operate primarily out of
Benghazi and expanded its activities to Tubruq and Ras Lanuf, consolidating its role
as a leading facilitator of illicit petroleum exports from Libya. The network’s
operational capacity, global reach and nimble financial capabilities enabled sustained
illicit exports with repercussions extending beyond Libya, adversely affecting
regional and international peace and security. The network relied on a complex web
__________________
85 S/2024/914, para. 110 and annex 65.
86 Ibid., para. 110 and annex 65.
26-02825 33/299
[[page 34]]
S/2026/224
of shell companies, maritime assets and complicit actors to obscure ownership, evade
oversight and launder proceeds derived from fuel smuggling.
1. Network structure, methods and international reach
118. The Panel found that the network was led by Maeen Ali Sharfeddin, a Libyan-
Turkish dual national. Sharfeddin controlled multiple companies registered in
Türkiye and the United Arab Emirates that lacked concrete commercial activity and
were used to facilitate illicit financial flows. The Panel found that Sharfeddin operated
in close coordination with Ali Al-Mashai and LAAF top command, whose control
over Benghazi old harbour and associated armed personnel enabled the
transformation of the harbour into a fuel smuggling hub. The Panel identified 11
network members based outside of Libya who played a direct role in supporting the
illicit network activities. The network operated at least 24 tanker vessels through
single-purpose shell companies with legal and administrative facilitation provided by
network members to circumvent due diligence. The Panel further found that the
network increasingly relied on ship-to-ship transfers and onshore storage in Egypt to
conceal the Libyan origin of cargo and to distribute fuel to international buyers.
2. Criminal acts at sea and institutional impunity
119. The Panel found that the network engaged in acts meeting the threshold of
serious crimes under international maritime law, comprising an armed robbery against
ships (against MT Florian (IMO 9046590) in December 2023) and an act of piracy
under article 101 of the United Nations Convention on the Law of the Sea (against
MT Anhona (IMO 9354521) in May 2024). To the Panel’s knowledge, no
investigation has been launched by Libyan authorities.
120. The Panel further found that network members travelled to two Member States,
relying on both forged documentation and bribery, in an attempt to obstruct
enforcement actions. Table 4 provides an overview of the network members. Detailed
evidence on the network’s structure, vessel movements, financial facilitation and
criminal incidents can be found in annex 9, section H.

F. Focal point established pursuant to Security Council resolution
2146 (2014) and accountability mechanism
121. As demonstrated above, the National Oil Corporation and its subsidiaries, as
well as Brega Petroleum Marketing Company, were completely prevented from
working independently and were exposed to the predatory influence of armed group
actors. The implementation of national and international mechanisms aimed at
addressing illicit petroleum exports has failed. The focal point mechanism pursuant
to resolution 2146 (2014), whose role is to inform the Security Council Committee
established pursuant to resolution 1970 (2011) concerning Libya of illicit petroleum
exports, has proven to be inoperable and thus ineffective. This is caused by an
environment in which armed group actors have pressured any State official that would
threaten their interest. During its past two mandates, the Panel had to mitigate the risk
of reprisals against the official holding the focal point position by reducing
engagement to a minimum. Other national mechanisms aimed at curbing illicit
petroleum exports, such as the committee for the review of fuel consumption
established by the Attorney General, were equally unsuccessful, as they faced

immediate adverse reactions by armed groups, including direct threats and fabricated

V. Asset freeze
A. Implementation of paragraph 14 of Security Council resolution
2769 (2025): progress to date
122. In paragraph 14 of resolution 2769 (2025), the Security Council decided to allow
the Libyan Investment Authority’s frozen cash reserves to be invested in low-risk time
deposits and fixed income instruments, with appropriate financial institutions selected
by the LIA, in accordance with specified conditions and notification procedures.
Under the same provision, the Council requested the Panel to assess the effect and
performance of these investments in its annual final reports. At the time of writing of
the present report, the Committee had received one notification from a Member State
invoking paragraph 14 of resolution 2769 (2025), transmitting a request from the
Libyan Foreign Investment Company to reinvest its frozen assets, on which the
Committee was yet to take action. No other notifications seeking the Committee’s
approval for reinvestments of frozen cash reserves under paragraph 14 of resolution
2769 (2025) had been submitted. The Panel assessed that the situation did not stem
from a lack of willingness on the part of the Libyan Investment Authority, but rather
from interpretative challenges encountered by Member States and relevant financial
institutions in implementing provisions contained in paragraph 14.
123. During the reporting period, the Committee received one request for guidance
regarding the reinvestment of the Authority’s frozen cash reserves under paragraph 14
of resolution 2769 (2025). The request raised several issues related to the practical
modalities of potential reinvestments under paragraph 14, as well as obstacles that
might be encountered during their operationalization, including with respect to
compliance with the asset freeze regime.
124. At the Committee’s request, the Panel supported efforts to provide guidance to
Member States and relevant financial institutions for the effective implementation of
paragraph 14 of resolution 2769 (2025), by preparing an implementation assistance
notice. In preparing this notice, the Panel sought to clarify the classification of frozen
cash reserves eligible for the exemption introduced under paragraph 14, proposed the
establishment of notification and reporting procedures consistent with the asset freeze
regime, and provided clarification on the interpretation of the terms “case-by-case
basis” and “jurisdiction” in the context of potential Libyan Investment Authority
reinvestments. The proposed notice was before the Committee at the time of writing.
In the Panel’s assessment, timely issuance of a dedicated implementation assistance
notice on paragraph 14 of resolution 2769 (2025) would enable more effective
preservation of Libyan frozen assets, reduce the risk of their erosion and help to
address challenges arising from the nature of frozen cash assets held in financial
institutions that do not offer cash custody services (see recommendation 4).
B. Implementation of the asset freeze and/or travel ban on
designated individuals
1. Ahmad Oumar Imhamad Al-Fitouri (LYi.023)
125. The Panel determined that Ahmad Oumar Imhamad Al-Fitouri (LYi.023) was
killed on 12 December 2025 in the western city of Sabratah. Libya, the country of
nationality and residence, has yet to communicate the death certificate to the Panel.
36/299 26-02825
[[page 37]]
S/2026/224
2. Quren Salih Quren Al Qadhafi (LYi.004) and Hannibal Muammar
Qadhafi (LYi.010)
126. During the reporting period the Committee received additional identifying
information concerning other designated individuals, including passport copies,
related to Quren Salih Quren Al Qadhafi (LYi.004) and Hannibal Muammar Qadhafi
(LYi.010) (see annex 37). The Committee may wish to take this information into
consideration, including with regard to the possible updating of the sanctions list (see
recommendation 7).
C. Challenges faced by a non-listed individual
127. During the reporting period, the Committee received an exemption request
related to a non-listed individual under the 1970 (2011) sanctions regime, whose
assets had been frozen in a Member State on the basis of an alleged association with
a designated individual. The Panel recalls that designations under the Libya sanctions
regime are personal and nominative in nature. The Panel further notes that association
with a sanctioned individual, in and of itself, does not constitute a basis for
designation in the absence of a specific listing decision by the Committee or the
Security Council.
VI. Recommendations
128. The Panel recommends:
To the Security Council:
Recommendation 1. To include in the forthcoming resolution a measure
prohibiting any payments for crude oil exported from
Libya to be credited outside the account of the National
Oil Corporation at the Libyan Foreign Bank in order to
ensure the permanence of the Libyan State and hinder the
financing of armed groups [see para. 104].
Recommendation 2. To modify paragraph 7 of resolution 2769 (2025) to
include a notification requirement for activities currently
described therein as an exception to the arms embargo
[see para. 67].
To the Committee:
Recommendation 3. To urge Member States that, in the implementation of
paragraph 19 of resolution 2213 (2015), national
authorities and private sector companies should exercise
more vigilance regarding the actual end-users of dual-use
items exported from their territories or by their
companies, in particular for such items that could fall
under the category of paramilitary equipment [see
para. 68].
Recommendation 4. To issue an implementation assistance notice to facilitate
harmonized implementation across Member States of
paragraph 14 of resolution 2769 (2025) concerning the
reinvestment of the Libyan Investment Authority’s frozen
cash reserves [see para. 122].
26-02825 37/299
[[page 38]]
S/2026/224
Recommendation 5. To consider designation of individuals and entities
determined by the Committee to have violated the arms
embargo and/or provided support for armed groups or
criminal networks through illicit exploitation of crude oil
or refined petroleum in Libya and illicit export of crude oil
or refined petroleum from Libya pursuant to paragraph 18
of resolution 2769 (2025) [see para. 121].
Recommendation 6. To consider the designation of vessels under resolution
2146 (2014) determined by the Committee to have
illicitly exported refined petroleum products from Libya,
in the absence of a functioning focal point pursuant to
resolution 2146 (2014) [see para. 121].
Recommendation 7. To update the sanctions list entries of Quren Salih Quren
Al Qadhafi (LYi.004) and Hannibal Muammar Qadhafi
(LYi.010) to reflect additional identifying information
[see para. 126].
To Member States:
Recommendation 8. To fully implement paragraphs 19, 20 and 21 of resolution
2213 (2015) relating to inspections “in their territory,
including seaports and airports, […] [of] vessels and
aircraft bound to or from Libya […], if […] the cargo
contains [embargoed items]”, and to report to the
Committee accordingly [see para. 70].
Recommendation 9. To urge their national authorities and private companies
under their jurisdictions to exercise necessary vigilance
in the export of military and civilian dual-use items, and
paramilitary equipment and their spare parts, to Libya,
particularly with regard to the actual end-users and export
conducted through third parties [see para. 68].
To the Government of Libya:
Recommendation 10. To reinstate the National Oil Corporation as the exclusive
marketer and exporter of crude oil by immediately
suspending the contract with Arkenu Oil Company, and
refrain from entering into similar contractual
arrangements, in order to stop illicit exports of petroleum,
preserve the permanence of the Libyan State and support
the reunification process [see para. 105].
Recommendation 11. To ensure transparent and independent access to relevant
oversight mechanisms by providing them with an
accurate account of all activities under the National Oil
Corporation and its subsidiaries, including imports to and
exports from Libya of refined petroleum products and
awarding of service contracts [see para. 105].

1. By resolution 1970 (2011), the Security Council expressed grave concern at the situation in Libya, condemned the
violence and use of force against civilians and deplored the gross and systematic violation of human rights. Within that
context, the Council imposed specific measures on Libya, under Chapter VII of the Charter of the United Nations, including
the arms embargo, which relates to arms and related materiel of all types, including weapons and ammunition, military
vehicles and equipment, paramilitary equipment, and spare parts for the aforementioned, in addition to the provision of
armed mercenary personnel. The arms embargo covers both arms entering and leaving Libya. The Council also imposed
travel ban and asset freeze measures, and listed individuals as subject to one or both measures, in the resolution. Furthermore,
the Council decided that the travel ban and the asset freeze were to apply to the individuals and entities designated by the
Committee established pursuant to resolution 1970 (2011) concerning Libya involved in or complicit in ordering, controlling
or otherwise directing the commission of serious human rights abuses against persons in Libya.
2. By resolution 1973 (2011), the Council strengthened the enforcement of the arms embargo and expanded the scope
of the asset freeze to include the exercise of vigilance when doing business with Libyan entities, if States had information
that provided reasonable grounds to believe that such business could contribute to violence and use of force against civilians.
Additional individuals subject to the travel ban and asset freeze were listed in the resolution, in addition to five entities
subject to the freeze. The Council decided that both measures were to apply also to individuals and entities determined to
have violated the provisions of the previous resolution, in particular the provisions concerning the arms embargo. The
resolution also included the authorization to protect civilians and civilian populated areas under threat of attack in Libya. In
addition, it included a no-fly zone in the airspace of Libya and a ban on flights of Libyan aircraft.
3. On 24 June 2011, the Committee designated two additional individuals and one additional entity subject to the
targeted measures. By resolution 2009 (2011), the Council introduced additional exceptions to the arms embargo and
removed two listed entities subject to the asset freeze, while allowing the four remaining listed entities to be subjected to a
partial asset freeze. It also lifted the ban on flights of Libyan aircraft.
4. By resolution 2016 (2011), the Council terminated the authorization related to the protection of civilians and the no-
fly zone. On 16 December 2011, the Committee removed the names of two entities previously subject to the asset freeze.
5. In resolution 2040 (2012), the Council directed the Committee, in consultation with the Libyan authorities, to review
continuously the remaining measures with regard to the two listed entities – the Libyan Investment Authority and the Libyan
Africa Investment Portfolio – and decided that the Committee was, in consultation with the Libyan authorities, to lift the
designation of those entities as soon as practical.
6. In resolution 2095 (2013), the Council further eased the arms embargo in relation to Libya concerning non-lethal
military equipment.
7. By resolution 2144 (2014), the Council stressed that Member States notifying to the Committee the supply, sale or
transfer to Libya of arms and related materiel, including related ammunition and spare parts, should ensure such notifications
contain all relevant information, and should not be resold to, transferred to, or made available for use by parties other than
the designated end user.
8. By resolution 2146 (2014), the Council decided to impose measures, on vessels to be designated by the Committee,
in relation to attempts to illicitly export crude oil from Libya and authorized Member States to undertake inspections of such
designated vessels.
9. By resolution 2174 (2014), the Council introduced additional designation criteria and requested the Panel to provide
information on individuals or entities engaging or providing support for acts that threaten the peace, stability of security of
Libya or obstructing the completion of the political transition. The resolution strengthened the arms embargo, by requiring
prior approval of the Committee for the supply, sale or transfer of arms and related materiel, including related ammunition
and spare parts, to Libya intended for security or disarmament assistance to the Libyan government, with the exception of
non-lethal military equipment intended solely for the Libyan government. The Council also renewed its call upon Member
States to undertake inspections related to the arms embargo and required them to report on such inspections.
10. By resolution 2213 (2015), the Council extended the authorizations and measures in relation to attempts to illicitly
export crude oil from Libya until 31 March 2016. The resolution further elaborated the designation criteria listed in
resolution 2174 (2014).
44/299 26-02825
[[page 45]]
S/2026/224
11. By resolution 2214 (2015), the Council called on the 1970 Committee concerning Libya to consider expeditiously
arms embargo exemption requests by the Libyan government for the use by its official armed forces to combat specific
terrorist groups named in that resolution.
12. By resolution 2259 (2015), the Council confirmed that individuals and entities providing support for acts that threaten
the peace, stability or security of Libya or that obstruct or undermine the successful completion of the political transition
must be held accountable, and recalled the travel ban and asset freeze in this regard.
13. By resolution 2278 (2016), the Council extended the authorizations and measures in relation to attempts to illicitly
export crude oil, while calling on the Libyan Government of National Accord (GNA) to improve oversight and control over
its oil sector, financial institutions and security forces.
14. By resolution 2292 (2016), the Council authorized, for a period of twelve months, inspections on the high seas off
the coast of Libya, of vessels that are believed to be carrying arms or related materiel to or from Libya, in violation of the
arms embargo.
15. By resolution 2357 (2017), the Council extended the authorizations set out in resolution 2292 (2016) for a further 12
months.
16. By resolution 2362 (2017), the Council extended until 15 November 2018 the authorizations and measures imposed
in resolution 2146 (2014), in relation to attempts to illicitly export crude oil from Libya. These measures were also applied
with respect to vessels loading, transporting, or discharging petroleum, including crude oil and refined petroleum products,
illicitly exported or attempted to be exported from Libya.
17. By resolution 2420 (2018), the Council further extended the authorizations, as set out in resolution 2292 (2016) and
extended in resolution 2357 (2017), for a further 12 months from the date of adoption of the resolution.
18. By resolution 2441 (2018), the Council extended until 15 February 2020 the authorizations and measures imposed
in resolution 2362 (2017), in relation to attempts to illicitly export crude oil from Libya.
19. By resolution 2473 (2019), the Council further extended the authorizations, as set out in resolution 2292 (2016) and
extended in resolutions 2357 (2017) and 2420 (2018), for a further 12 months from the date of adoption of the resolution.
20. By resolution 2509 (2020), the Council extended until 30 April 2021 the authorizations and measures imposed in
resolution 2146 (2014), as amended in paragraph 2 of resolutions 2362 (2017) and 2441 (2018), and modified the designation
period in paragraph 11 of resolution 2146 (2014) to be one year, and requested the Panel to report on any information relating
to the illicit export from or illicit import to Libya of petroleum, including crude oil and refined petroleum products.
21. By resolution 2526 (2020), the Council further extended the authorizations, as set out in resolution 2292 (2016) and
extended in resolutions 2357 (2017), 2420 (2018), and 2473 (2019), for a further 12 months from the date of adoption of
the resolution.
22. By resolution 2571 (2021), the Council extended until 30 July 2022 the authorizations and measures imposed in
resolution 2146 (2014), as amended in paragraph 2 of resolutions 2362 (2017), 2441 (2018) and 2509 (2020), in relation to
attempts to illicitly export petroleum, including crude oil and refined petroleum products, from Libya.
23. By resolution 2578 (2021), the Council further extended the authorizations, as set out in resolution 2292 (2016) and
extended in resolutions 2357 (2017), 2420 (2018), 2473 (2019), and 2526 (2020) for a further 12 months from the date of
adoption of the resolution.
24. By resolution 2635 (2022), the Council further extended the authorizations, as set out in resolution 2292 (2016) and
extended in resolutions 2357 (2017), 2420 (2018), 2473 (2019), 2526 (2020) and 2578 (2021) for a further 12 months from
the date of adoption of the resolution.
25. By resolution 2644 (2022), the Council extended until 30 October 2023 the authorizations and measures imposed in
resolution 2146 (2014), as amended in paragraph 2 of resolutions 2362 (2017), 2441 (2018), 2509 (2020) and 2571 (2021)
in relation to attempts to illicitly export petroleum, including crude oil and refined petroleum products, from Libya.
26. By resolution 2684 (2023), the Council further extended the authorizations, as set out in resolution 2292 (2016) and
extended in resolutions 2357 (2017), 2420 (2018), 2473 (2019), 2526 (2020), 2578 (2021) and 2635 (2022) for a further 12
months from the date of adoption of the resolution.

27. By resolution 2701 (2023), the Council further extended until 1 February 2025 the authorizations and measures
imposed in resolution 2146 (2014), as amended in paragraph 2 of resolutions 2441 (2018) and 2509 (2020); affirmed the
Council’s readiness to consider changes, when appropriate, to the asset freeze at the request of the Government of Libya,
including allowing the LIA, which is under a specific asset freeze measure, to reinvest frozen liquid assets for the purpose
of preserving their value and benefiting the Libyan people at a later stage.
28. By resolution 2733 (2024), the Council further extended the authorizations, as set out in resolution 2292 (2016) and
extended in resolutions 2357 (2017), 2420 (2018), 2473 (2019), 2526 (2020), 2578 (2021), 2635 (2022), and 2684 (2023)
for a further 12 months from the date of adoption of the resolution; and modified paragraph 5 of resolution 2292 (2016) to
require Committee approval for certain modes of disposal of seized items.
29. By resolution 2769 (2025), the Council further extended until 1 May 2026 the authorizations and measures imposed
in resolution 2146 (2014), as amended in paragraph 2 of resolutions 2441 (2018) and 2509 (2020); introduced a new
exemption to the arms embargo for any technical assistance or training by Member States to Libyan security forces intended
solely to promote the process of reunification of Libyan military and security institutions, as well as the temporary
introduction into Libya of weapons or other military equipment intended solely for use by the non-Libyan providers of such
technical assistance and training, for the delivery of such assistance and for their protective use, requiring an advance
notification to the Committee; asserted that the arms embargo is not to be applied to military aircraft or naval vessels,
temporarily introduced into the territory of Libya solely to deliver items or facilitate activities otherwise exempted or not
covered by the arms embargo, including humanitarian assistance, as well as arms and related materiel for defensive purposes;
decided to allow the LIA’s frozen cash reserves to be invested in low-risk time deposits and fixed income instruments, with
appropriate financial institutions selected by the LIA, in accordance with specified conditions and notifications procedures;
and further elaborated the designation criteria.
30. By resolution 2780 (2025), the Council further extended the authorizations, as set out in resolution 2292 (2016) and
extended in resolutions 2357 (2017), 2420 (2018), 2473 (2019), 2526 (2020), 2578 (2021), 2635 (2022), 2684 (2023) and
2733 (2024) for a further 6 months from the date of adoption of the resolution.
31. By resolution 2804 (2025), the Council further extended the authorizations, as set out in resolution 2292 (2016) and
extended in resolutions 2357 (2017), 2420 (2018), 2473 (2019), 2526 (2020), 2578 (2021), 2635 (2022), 2684 (2023), 2733
(2024) and 2780 (2025) for a further six months from the date of adoption of the resolution.

1. The Panel ensured compliance with the methodological standards recommended by the Informal Working Group of
the Security Council on General Issues of Sanctions (S/2006/997). Those standards call for reliance on verified, genuine
documents and concrete evidence and on-site observations by the experts, including taking photographs, wherever possible.
When physical inspection is not possible, the Panel will seek to corroborate information using multiple, independent sources
to appropriately meet the highest achievable standard, placing a higher value on statements by principal actors and first-
hand witnesses to events.
2. The Panel used satellite imagery of Libya procured by the United Nations from private providers to support
investigations, as well as open-source imagery. Commercial databases recording maritime and aviation data were referenced.
Public statements by officials through their official media channels were accepted as factual unless contrary facts were
established. Any mobile phone records from service providers were also accepted as factual. While the Panel wishes to be
as transparent as possible, in situations in which identifying sources would have exposed them or others to unacceptable
safety risks, the Panel decided not to include identifying information in this document and instead placed the relevant
evidence in United Nations secure archives.
3. The Panel continued to treat open-source media only as lead information. Taking into account fast-developing
technologies such as the use of artificial intelligence, and to counteract any risk to the Panel’s objectivity, impartiality and
independence, the Panel adapted its evidentiary threshold methodology vis-à-vis how it qualifies a violation or non-
compliance versus general reporting of trends and patterns. To make a specific finding on a violation or non-compliance,
the Panel complemented existing corroborative methods – such as geolocation – with additional evidentiary elements
including independent and technical sources. In determining whether a violation or non-compliance occurred, the Panel
adhered to the evidentiary standard of preponderance of evidence.
4. The spelling of toponyms within Libya often depends on the ethnicity of the source or the quality of transliteration.
The Panel has adopted a consistent approach in the present update. All major locations in Libya are spelled or referenced as
per the UN Geospatial Information Section (GIS) map at appendix 3.A.
5. The Panel has placed importance on the rule of consensus among the Panel members and agreed that, if differences
and/or reservations arise during the development of reports, it would only adopt the text, conclusions and recommendations
by a majority of five out of the six members. In the event of a recommendation for designation of an individual or a group,
such recommendation would be done based on unanimity.
6. The Panel is committed to impartiality in investigating incidents of non-compliance by any party.
7. The Panel is equally committed to the highest degree of fairness and has offered the opportunity to reply to Member
States, entities and individuals involved in the majority of incidents that are covered in this update. Their response has been
taken into consideration in the Panel’s findings. The methodology for this is provided in appendix 3. B.
8. The Panel had no opportunity to review the edited version of the Report in English language, nor its translations into
the other five United Nations official languages.
9. The Panel’s methodology in relation to its investigations concerning IHL, IHRL and human rights abuses, is provided
in appendix 3.C.
10. The Panel’s methodology in relation to its investigations concerning vessels and aircraft in the context of the arms
embargo is contained in appendix 3.D.
11. The Panel’s methodology in relation to its investigations concerning specifically temporary entries of military aircraft
and naval vessels is contained in appendix 3.E.

Appendix 3.B: ‘The opportunity to reply’ methodology used by the Panel
1. Although sanctions are meant to be preventative not punitive, it should be recognized that the mere naming of an
individual or entity99 in a Panel’s report could have adverse effects on the individual. As such, where possible, individuals
concerned should be provided with an opportunity to provide their account of events and to provide concrete and specific
information/materiel in support. Through this interaction, the individual is given the opportunity to demonstrate that their
alleged conduct does not fall within the relevant listing criteria. This is called the ‘opportunity to reply’.
2. The Panel’s methodology on the opportunity to reply is as follows:
(a) Providing an individual with an ‘opportunity to reply’ should be the norm;
(b) The Panel may decide not to offer an opportunity of reply if there is credible evidence that it would
unduly prejudice its investigations, including if it would:
(i) Result in the individual moving assets if they get warning of a possible recommendation for
designation;
(ii) Restrict further access of the Panel to vital sources;
(iii) Endanger Panel sources or Panel members;
(iv) Adversely and gravely impact humanitarian access for humanitarian actors in the field; or
(v) For any other reason that can be clearly demonstrated as reasonable and justifiable in the
prevailing circumstances.
3. If the circumstances set forth in 2 (b) do not apply, then the Panel should be able to provide an individual an
opportunity to reply.
4. The individual should be able to communicate directly with the Panel to convey their personal determination as to
the level and nature of their interaction with the Panel.
5. Interactions between the Panel and the individual should be direct, unless in exceptional circumstances.
6. In no circumstances can third parties, without the knowledge of the individual, determine for the individual its level
of interaction with the Panel.
7. The individual, on the other hand, in making their determination of the level and nature of interaction with the Panel,
may consult third parties or allow third parties (for example, legal representative or his/her government) to communicate on
his/her behalf on subsequent interactions with the Panel.
__________________
99 Hereinafter just the term individual will be used to reflect both.

Appendix 3.C: Violations relating to IHL, IHRL, and acts that constitute human rights abuses
investigative methodology
1. The Panel’s methodology, in relation to its investigations concerning IHL, IHRL and human rights abuses, is set out
as below:
(a) All Panel investigations are initiated based on verifiable information being made available to the Panel, either
directly from sources or from media reports.
(b) In carrying out any investigations on the use of explosive ordnance against the civilian population, the Panel
will rely on at least three or more of the following sources of information:
(i) At least two eye-witnesses or victims;
(ii) At least one individual or organization (either local or international) that has also independently
investigated the incident;
(iii) If there are casualties associated with the incident, and if the casualties are less than ten in number,
the Panel obtains copies of death certificates and medical certificates. In incidents relating to mass
casualties, the Panel relies on published information from the United Nations and other
organizations;
(iv) Technical evidence, which includes imagery of explosive events such as the impact damage, blast
effects, and recovered fragmentation. In all cases, the Panel collects imagery from at least two
different and unrelated sources. In the rare cases where the Panel has had to rely on open-source
imagery, the Panel verifies that imagery by referring it to eye or by checking for pixilation distortion;
a. In relation to air strikes, the Panel often identifies the responsible party through crater
analysis or by the identification of components from imagery of fragmentation; and
b. The Panel also analyses imagery of the ground splatter pattern at the point of impact
from mortar, artillery, or free flight rocket fire to identify the direction from which the
incoming ordnance originated. This is one indicator to assist in the identification of the
perpetrator for ground fire when combined with other source information.
(v) The utilisation of open source or purchased satellite imagery wherever possible, to identify the exact
location of an incident, and to support analysis of the type and extent of destruction. Such imagery
may also assist in the confirmation of timelines of the incident;
(vii) Access to investigation reports and other documentation of local and international organizations that
have independently investigated the incident;
(vii) Other documentation that supports the narrative of sources, for example, factory manuals that may
prove that the said factory is technically incapable of producing weapons of the type it is alleged to
have produced;
(viii) In rare instances where the Panel has doubt as to the veracity of available facts from other sources,
local sources are relied on to collect specific and verifiable information from the ground. (For
example, if the Panel wished to confirm the presence of an armed group in a particular area);
(ix) Statements issued by or on behalf of a party to the conflict responsible for the incident; and/or
(x) Open-source information to identify other corroborative or contradictory information regarding the
Panel’s findings.
(c) In carrying out its investigations on depravation of liberty and associated violations the Panel relies on the
following sources of information:
(i) The victims, where they are able and willing to speak to the Panel, and where medical and security
conditions are conducive to such an interview;
(ii) The relatives of victims and others who had access to the victims while in custody. This is
particularly relevant in instances where the victim dies in custody;
(iii) Interviews with at least one individual or organization (either local or international) that has also
independently investigated the incident;
(iv) Medical documentation and, where applicable, death certificates;
(v) Documentation issued by prison authorities;
(vi) Interviews with medical personnel who treated the victim, wherever possible;
(vii) Investigation and other documentation from local and international organizations that have
independently investigated the incident. The Panel may also seek access to court documents if the
detainee is on trial or other documentation that proves or disproves the narrative of the victim;
50/299 26-02825
[[page 51]]
S/2026/224
(viii) Where relevant, the Panel uses local sources to collect specific and verifiable information from the
ground, for example, medical certificates;
(ix) Statements issued by the party to the conflict responsible for the incident; and/or
(x) Open-source information to identify other corroborative or contradictory information regarding the
Panel’s findings.
(d) In carrying out its investigations on other violations, which can include forced displacement and threats
against medical workers, the Panel relies on information that includes:
(i) Interviews with victims, eyewitnesses, and direct reports where they are able and willing to speak to
the Panel, and where conditions are conducive to such an interview;
(ii) Interviews with at least one individual or organization (either local or international) that has also
independently investigated the incident;
(iii) Documentation relevant to verify information obtained;
(iv) Statements issued by the party to the conflict responsible for the incident; and/or
(v) Open-source information to identify other corroborative or contradictory information regarding the
Panel’s findings.
(e) Upon completion of its investigation, wherever possible, the Panel provides those responsible with an
opportunity to respond to the Panel’s findings in so far as it relates to the attribution of responsibility. Detailed
information on incidents will not be provided when there is a credible threat that would threaten Panel sources.
(f) If a party does not provide the Panel with the information requested, as called upon by paragraphs 14 and 15
of resolution 2644 (2022), the Panel may consider this for reporting to the Committee.
2. The Panel will not include information in its reports that may identify or endanger its sources. Where it is necessary
to bring such information to the attention of the Council or the Committee, the Panel may include more source information
in confidential annexes.
3. The Panel will not divulge any information that may lead to the identification of victims, witnesses, and other
particularly vulnerable Panel sources, except: (a) with the specific permission of the sources; and (b) where the Panel is,
based on its own assessment, certain that these individuals would not suffer any danger as a result. The Panel stands ready
to provide the Council or the Committee, on request, with any additional imagery and documentation to supports the Panel’s
findings beyond that included in its reports. Appropriate precautions will be taken though to protect the anonymity of its
sources.

Appendix 3.D: Use of maritime and air delivery profile indicators
1. In the context of the arms embargo, the Panel uses maritime and air delivery profile indicators100 to assist in
determining the likelihood of violations and occurrences, and thus determine the focus of Panel investigations. These
indicators of suspicious activities and documentation, when considered collectively, indicate that a vessel or aircraft is
likely to be carrying illicit cargo (see tables 3.D.1 and 3.D.2). Multiple indicators are required before a vessel, aircraft or
airline is classified as of interest to the Panel or reported as being in violation of or in non-compliance with the arms
embargo. This annex summarises these indicators.

Appendix 3.E: Use of specific profile indicators for investigations concerning temporary entries into
Libya of military aircraft and naval vessels
1. In resolution 2769 (2025), the Council introduced new provisions in relation to the arms embargo, specifically the
exemption and exception contained in paragraphs 6 and 7 respectively. Thus, the Panel adapted its methodology to
identify instances of possible non-compliance with or violation of the arms embargo. The Panel developed specific profile
indicators for temporary entries of military aircraft and naval vessels to assist in determining the likelihood of violations
and occurrences and thus narrowing the focus of Panel investigations. These indicators, when considered collectively,
indicate that a vessel or aircraft may be a) undertaking activities not subject to the exemption of paragraph 6; or b)
carrying cargo not subject to the exception of paragraph 7 (table 3.E.1). Multiple indicators are required before a
temporary entry into Libya of a military aircraft or naval vessel is classified as of interest to the Panel and thus prompting
an investigation. This annex summarises these indicators.

60/299 26-02825
[[page 61]]
S/2026/224
1. The Joint Operation Force (JOF), based in Misrata, has close links to Prime Minister Abdelhamid Dbeibah. A Prime
Minister-issued executive decree created the group back in 2013, which has evolved since then. Ever since, it benefited from
sustained political and financial support from the GNU. JOF operated under a broad mandate covering counter-terrorism
activities, operations against criminal networks and customs, in particular at Misrata port. In July-August 2022, JOF directly
supported Abdelhamid Dbeibah against Fathi Bashagha during the clashes for the Prime ministership.102 To that end, JOF
engaged in clashes against other Misrata-based armed groups who were in favour of Bashagha.
2. The growing role of the Misrata-based force in Tripoli highlighted Abdelhamid Dbeibah’s efforts to assert greater
influence over Tripoli’s security landscape. It also reflected his strategy to secure the loyalty of a powerful coalition of
armed groups in western Libya, aligned with his interest. Following the killing of Abdelghani al-Kikli, former head of the
Stability Support Apparatus (SSA), JOF attacked and took control of two Libyan Intelligence Service (LIS) facilities: Salah
al-Din training facilities and the Sabaa headquarters and detention facilities. On 28 May 2025, JOF fighters spearheaded a
violent forced entry into the National Oil Corporation (NOC) headquarters in Tripoli, to pressure officials into awarding
supplier contracts to companies controlled by or associated with them.103 Authorities, including NOC leadership,
downplayed the incident, which is indicative of the continued impunity enjoyed by such armed groups, particularly when
targeting state institutions.
3. Since then, JOF maintained an active presence along the Misrata-Tripoli corridor, where it established checkpoints
and conducted raids against rival armed groups. These included armed clashes with the SSA - most notably in Khoms - prior
to Kikli’s killing, and with DACOT in the weeks that followed.104
__________________
102 S/2023/673, Annex 10.
103 Confidential Sources (Libyan officials and armed groups members)
104 Confidential Report, May 2025.
26-02825 61/299
[[page 62]]
S/2026/224
1. Ibrahim Dbeibah is the nephew of Prime Minister Abdelhamid Dbeibah and officially serves as National Security
Advisor within the Prime Minister’s Office. His influence derives primarily from his father, Ali Dbeibah, who was
instrumental in facilitating and bankrolling Abdelhamid Dbeibah’s rise to power. Buttressed by his father, Ibrahim Dbeibah
largely operates without any checks or balances.
2. Ibrahim Dbeibah positioned himself at a unique intersection of the security, economic, and political spheres. He
benefited from sustained support from Misrata-based armed groups as well as influential Misratan figures across economic
and religious circles. His direct collaboration with armed group leaders enabled him to rise as a key actor within the armed
group ecosystem. Prior to the killing of Abdelghani al-Kikli, he and Ibrahim Dbeibah were in direct competition over the
control of the Libyan Post, Telecommunications and Information Technology Company (LPTIC), despite prior mutually
beneficial agreements. Like other armed group actors, Ibrahim Dbeibah consolidated his influence by placing proxies in key
positions within State institutions and employing threat-based tactics against senior Libyan officials.
62/299 26-02825
[[page 63]]
S/2026/224
1. The Panel found that tensions in western Libya were increasingly being fuelled along geographical lines, with
growing friction between a predominantly Misrata-led coalition and a constellation of local armed groups operating across
the western region, from Zawiyah to the Tunisian border. Tensions between Abdelghani al-Kikli and armed group actors
aligned with Prime Minister Abdulhamid Dbeibah prior to the May 2025 events were directly linked to appointments within
military and State institutions that were widely perceived as over-representing Misrata, thereby threatening the existing
balance of power among western Libyan armed groups.
2. On 1 June 2025, Presidential Council President Mohammed el-Menfi appointed Hassan Busriba, hailing from
Zawiyah, as commander of the Stability Support Apparatus (SSA) in Tripoli. On 24 June 2025, Busriba and forces under
his command entered Tripoli and took control of the SSA headquarters in Janzour. This marked the first instance of a
Zawiyah-based armed group establishing a presence in Tripoli, in a move widely interpreted as an attempt to undermine
Prime Minister Dbeibah’s influence over the Tripoli security architecture.
3. In July 2025, a meeting held in Janzour at the initiative of Hassan Busriba brought together leaders of armed groups
from across western Libya, including Zawiyah, Warshafana, al-Ajilat, Sabrata, Zuwara, and Zintan. The primary objective
of the meeting was to coordinate responses among these groups to what they perceived as an existential threat posed by the
growing dominance of Misrata-aligned armed groups. While the meeting did not result in the establishment of a formal or
structured military alliance, it nonetheless reinforced coordination and cooperation among participating groups against
Tripoli-based armed actors supporting Dbeibah.
4. Since the May 2025 clashes, tensions have continued to escalate, manifested through a series of incidents and armed
confrontations, predominantly in Zawiyah (approximately 60 kilometres west of Tripoli). These incidents are assessed as a
direct consequence of sustained rivalry between Tripoli-based armed groups aligned with Prime Minister Dbeibah and armed
groups originating from Zawiyah and other parts of western Libya.
26-02825 63/299
[[page 64]]
S/2026/224
A. Framework of the armed groups’ control
1. In its previous report, the Panel highlighted the infiltration of the oil sector by armed groups with the objective of
diverting State revenues derived from Libya’s key natural resource and main source of income.105 The Panel’s investigations
found that armed group actors,106 mainly Ibrahim Dbeibah and Saddam Haftar, have developed and honed their capability
to exert control over the National Oil Corporation (NOC) at every level of the decision-making process. The impact was
twofold: not only did it enable armed groups to expand their influence and their military capabilities, but it also affected
public interest by directly undermining the permanence of the Libyan State, as State institutions were prevented from
accessing oil revenues.
2. Armed group actors instrumentalised violence – without accountability – to provide an umbrella of impunity to
individuals who, through their technical expertise and established networks, could generate an ever-increasing stream of
revenue. In a symbiotic relationship, these individuals actively supported the interests of armed group actors and vice versa.
In the oil and fuel sector, this environment of impunity has led to routine rubber-stamping of export and import transactions,
exploitation agreements, and service contracts, to serve competing networks supported by armed group actors. This has
resulted in the systemic incapacity of Libyan institutions, including the NOC and Brega Company, to effectively monitor
and/or control crude oil exports, crude oil revenues, imports, distribution and exports of refined petroleum, and domestic
fuel consumption. With all relevant institutions dragooned into operating in an environment that shielded and enabled these
individuals, the prospect of any meaningful oversight of the oil sector has all but vanished. As a result, accountability
mechanisms were rendered ineffective, allowing illicit practices to thrive.
3. Oil revenues provided an illustrative example. The Libyan State budget depends on hydrocarbon revenues for more
than 90 percent of its income.107 The situation described above prevents the relevant institutions from obtaining a
comprehensive and accurate account of oil production levels and the revenues generated therefrom. In 2025, the amount of
oil revenue entering Libya in USD only reached USD 18.78 billion (excluding royalties and taxes), despite the suspension
of the offset procedure in April 2025. Based on production levels108 and the average Brent price, estimated revenues should
have been around USD 27 billion.
B. Farhat Bengdara, the NOC and Al Masraf Bank
4. Farhat Bengdara was appointed Chairman of the NOC in July 2022 and resigned effective 16 January 2025. During
his tenure, Bengdara also remained Chairman of the Board of the Arab Investment and Foreign Trade Bank, commonly
referred to as Al-Masraf Bank, despite several Libyan State institutions having raised concerns about an apparent conflict
of interest. The Panel found that, while serving as Chairman of the NOC, Bengdara colluded with several armed group actors
to schematize the allocation of oil services contracts to newly established entities associated with these actors. The Panel
further found that he worked in collusion with armed group actors to divert Libyan oil and gas revenues.
1. USD 300 million appointment
5. Bengdara was appointed as Chairman of the NOC with the backing of the Haftar family, who had threatened to block
oil fields should the GNU not appoint a chairman originating from Benghazi.109 Beyond the political optics, the Panel found
that Bengdara had previously curried favour with the Haftars, having facilitated the diversion of USD 300 million from Al-
Masraf Bank that belonged to the Libyan Foreign Bank (LFB) to support the Libyan national army’s offensive against
Tripoli in 2019110, including the financing of Project Opus.111 That financial support triggered the political backing that led
to Bengdara’s appointment as Chairman of the NOC.
__________________
105 S/2024/914 para. 94 to 114.
106 “Armed group actors” refers to individuals that wield power vis-à-vis armed groups, including through directing or financing
them.
107 https://www.imf.org/en/news/articles/2025/06/25/pr-25217-libya-imf-executive-board-concludes-the-2025-article-iv-
consultation, 25 June 2025.
108 https://noc.ly/en/noc-achieves-highest-average-crude-oil-production-in-a-decade/, 1 January 2026.
109 CS (Libyan energy sector officials).
110 CS (Libyan energy sector and banking sector officials).
111 S/2021/229, paras. 86-92 and annex 76.
64/299 26-02825
[[page 65]]
S/2026/224
6. In 2019, the LFB, which is also a partial shareholder of Al-Masraf Bank, held a time-deposit account at Al-Masraf
Bank valued at USD 300 million. After the failed offensive on Tripoli and the signing of the ceasefire agreement in 2020,
the LFB sought, beginning in 2021, to recover these funds. Al-Masraf Bank initially justified its inability to release the funds
by citing administrative delays, and subsequently claimed that the funds had been used as collateral in a transaction that had
no formal approval from the LFB. The LFB was eventually able to recover USD 70 million of the original 300 million in
March 2025.112 Despite threats of legal action, at the time of writing, the remainder of the amount had yet to be recovered.
2. NOC under the influence of armed groups
7. Following his appointment in 2022, Bengdara set the NOC’s objective of daily crude oil production to reach two
million barrels by the end of 2025. To support that objective, in April 2022 the GNU approved the allocation of LYD 34
billion (USD 7 billion) to the NOC.113 The Panel found that instead, Bengdara used the NOC objective to increase production
as a ploy to put in place a system allowing armed groups actors to divert part of these funds.
8. Bengdara leveraged his position as both Chairman of the NOC and Al-Masraf Bank, backed by armed group actors,
to pressure the chairmen of production companies – majority-owned by the NOC – to open bank accounts at Al-Masraf for
the subsidiaries’ operational expenses, including those related to subcontractors. This was aimed at strengthening the
financial position of Al-Masraf and impeding effective oversight by Libyan State entities over the movement of funds. The
chairmen of the subsidiaries complied with opening such accounts but kept their use to a minimum because they recognized
the broader implications.114
9. Bengdara also played a central role in facilitating the awarding of subcontracts to companies that did not meet the
requirements set out under the relevant Libyan laws. He designed a system involving a different set of NOC-owned
companies outside of Libya, positioning them as gatekeepers for access to funding and the granting of operational contracts.
Companies such as the Dubai branch of Mediterranean Oil Services Co. (Medoil) and London-based Murzuq Oil Services
were used to generate revenue for armed group actors and their associates within the oil sector.115
10. Bengdara also restructured the NOC, with the primary objective of forestalling internal checks and balances and
minimizing external oversight. This included reshuffling of professional staff, creation of new positions and departments,
reorganization of working methods and reshaping of contractual relationships with international partners. For instance, he
established a “Strategic Planning” department outside the NOC’s formal structure, which operated as a vehicle for
negotiating contracts outside the applicable legal and regulatory frameworks.116 Additionally, he created a specific position
in charge of the marketing of crude oil, to bypass and supersede existing decision-making procedures, enabling the export
of crude oil outside the remits of established internal oversight.117
11. Bengdara further used his position and connections with armed groups to force NOC subsidiaries to enter into
contractual arrangement, both in oil production and the import of fuel. The Panel found that he instructed subsidiaries to
contract with specific service companies that were either directly controlled by armed groups or subcontracted by structures
under their control, resulting in overcharging for services rendered, if rendered at all.118 Bengdara oversaw the lapse of long-
standing partnerships in fuel supply contracts all at once, including for the offset mechanism,119 which subsequently
favoured smaller traders over oil majors and refineries. These changes coincided with the sustained high levels of imports
of fuel that could be managed internally by the NOC, without the oversight of external financing via the LFB (see below
section D).120 Bengdara also directly coordinated with Al-Kikli for the appointment of the former Waha Oil Company
chairman121 who was arrested in February 2025 by the Attorney General Office on financial crime charges.122

S/2026/224
12. Overall, the Panel found that, during his tenure as Chairman of the NOC, Bengdara implemented staffing and
governance practices that significantly weakened the institution’s capacity to act as an independent State entity by enabling
control by armed group actors or their associates. After his resignation, Bengdara’s immunity was lifted and oversight bodies
launched investigations leading to the Attorney General issuing an arrest warrant.123 He has been residing outside of Libya
since, avoiding travel to western Libya, but has travelled to Benghazi on several occasions.
C. Rafat al-Abbar and the Arkenu deal
13. The Panel previously reported about Arkenu Oil Company,124 established in 2023 as Libya’s first private oil company,
which is indirectly controlled by Saddam Haftar through his proxies, in particular Rafat al-Abbar. During this reporting
period, the Panel found that: a) Arkenu actively diverted over USD 3 billion in oil revenue to bank accounts outside Libya;
b) the manner in which the contractual relationship was established between Arkenu and the NOC was not in accordance
with the relevant Libyan laws; c) taxes due were not paid to the Libyan State; and d) the key terms of the contract were not
implemented by Arkenu, particularly the investment obligations and the quantities of oil that Arkenu was authorized to
export.
14. Throughout the Bengdara era, Rafat al-Abbar played an instrumental role in controlling decision-making processes
within the NOC (appendix 9.A). Following Bengdara’s exit, he ensured that, at key levels of the institution, pressure was
exerted to further the interests of Saddam Haftar and his close associates. Al-Abbar identified and targeted NOC employees
at different levels and established a shadow decision-making structure within the NOC by leveraging his alliance with
Saddam Haftar. The absence of effective support from the institutional hierarchy, internal or external oversight mechanisms,
or law enforcement authorities, forced compliance at all levels within the NOC.
15. Al-Abbar was instrumental in securing the contract between the NOC and Arkenu and in enforcing its
implementation outside the legal and contractual framework, contrary to the interests of the NOC. One aspect of this control
involved monitoring and actively obstructing any attempts to activate oversight mechanisms or to produce accurate data
regarding Arkenu’s activities.
16. The “management committee,” as defined in the contract between Arkenu and the NOC, served as the primary forum
for both parties to review contractual obligations and monitor implementation. Belqacem Shengeer was appointed chairman
of this joint committee as the NOC’s representative with the support of Al-Abbar. Shengeer is a former member of the NOC
Board of Directors and was the technical architect behind the creation of Arkenu. Despite formally representing the NOC
in Tripoli, he resides in Benghazi.
17. Shengeer was able to prevent any attempts by the NOC to protect Libya’s interests. He did not provide any report of
the committee activities, impeding any oversight. Shengeer directly represented the interests of Saddam Haftar and members
of the LAAF top command. The non-implementation of two major aspects of the contract illustrates how Arkenu benefitted
from the LAAF’s capacity to obstruct any effort by the NOC to safeguard its institutional and financial interests.
1. The USD 1 billion investment
18. Under the contract, Arkenu was required to invest USD 1 billion to improve the production capacity of the Sarir and
Mesla oil fields, both serviced by the NOC subsidiary Arabian Gulf Oil Company (AGOCO). The Panel found that only a
small fraction of this amount was in effect invested through implementing partners. In fact, production at the two fields did
not increase following the signature of the Arkenu contract, but rather declined, with most monthly production remaining
below the baseline established in the contract (table 9.1).
__________________

124 S/2024/914, paragraph 96.

Table 9.1
Sarir/Mesla oil fields production 2021-2025
2. The production share
19. Under the initial Arkenu contract signed in March 2023, Arkenu was authorized to export a production share of 40
per cent of the production increase. In December 2024, an amendment to the contract changed the terms of the original
contract entirely, stipulating that 40 per cent of the overall production be allocated to Arkenu, without any new obligation
for Arkenu or compensation for the NOC. The Panel found that the quantities exported by Arkenu significantly exceeded
the limits set out in the contract. Between January 2024 and December 2025, the Panel determined that Arkenu exported at
least 42.1 million barrels of crude oil through 42 shipments to destinations worldwide.
20. To export such large quantities of crude oil, Arkenu relied on a) subsidiaries of well-established and large traders,
such as BGN Energy FZE (UAE) and b) newly created broker companies in Libya, enabling other top commanders of the
LAAF to benefit directly from oil exports without any contractual relationship with the NOC. The company Al-Gandal
embodied a key example of this system. Al-Gandal was registered in early 2023, with front individuals listed as its owners.
Al-Gandal operated as a sales representative for Arkenu, seeking to secure export contracts with oil shipping companies by
widely advertising and eventually negotiating sales – exclusively below the NOC market price – through social media
messaging applications. Once an agreement was reached via these applications, clients were instructed to travel to Dubai to
sign contracts in person and to open bank accounts at banks selected by the network, facilitating the concealment of
significant financial transactions. The Panel reviewed a sample contract put forward by Al-Gandal, providing for the export
of two million barrels of crude oil per month for a period of one year (appendix 9.B), with the price fixed at USD 8 below
NOC international market rates per barrel.125
21. Since the establishment of Arkenu, armed groups based in western Libya have sought to promote similar contractual
arrangements that would allow the export of petroleum products or natural gas by entities other than the NOC, thereby
opening the door to unchecked control over Libya’s natural resources. The Panel found that pressure exerted by armed group
actors on the concerned Libyan authorities – coupled with infighting – was the primary factor preventing the conclusion of
a production contract for the NC7 gas field through the establishment of a new company that would have been called Juliana.
__________________
125 CS (oil sector operator).
26-02825 67/299
[[page 68]]
S/2026/224
22. The Panel found that Arkenu served as a litmus test for similar arrangements to be used as a Trojan horse by armed
group actors – particularly networks under the control of Saddam Haftar and Ibrahim Dbeibah – to feign private investment
into the oil sector and provide a facade of legitimacy for the illicit export of petroleum. This compelled NOC to grant
companies under the control of these armed group actors access to international petroleum markets. Owing to Arkenu’s
establishment in contravention of the relevant Libyan laws, the non-implementation of contractual obligations and over-
exploitation of contractually assured shares, and exports without any benefit for the Libyan State, the Panel found that
Arkenu’s exports fell into the category of illicit exports of crude oil from Libya under paragraph 18 of resolution 2769
(2025).
23. The Panel found that the timeline of the diversions of oil revenues through Arkenu corresponds with: a) the east
desisting from disputing the lack of access to Libyan public funds; and b) the observed significant improvements in the
military capabilities of LAAF, representing a direct threat to the political transition process and to the peace and security in
Libya.
D. Fuel supplies for illicit export
24. Illicit export of refined petroleum products,126 both by land and by sea, continued to represent a major source of
income for armed groups. Illicit petroleum export from the west remained fragmented, with most exports being transported
overland in a smuggling-type fashion. In contrast, access to fuel supplies in areas under GNU control were centralized by
Abdelsalam al-Zobi, who leveraged his position in the military structure and his access to Misrata port to organize the
diversion of large quantities of fuel. Exports from the east moved at wholesale volumes, mainly by sea but also by land, and
demonstrated a high level of organization under the enabling umbrella of the LAAF. The two main protagonists of the fuel
supply chain – the NOC for imports, Brega Company for internal distribution – lost the ability to challenge vastly inflated
demands by what is considered the “big consumers”, particularly by the General Electricity Company of Libya (GECOL).127
25. While the Panel’s previous findings on GECOL128 elicited a public reaction from the company’s leadership, including
its chairman Mohamed al-Mashay, there was no official response to the Panel.129 Despite public criticism and the announced
launch of an investigation by the Attorney General, Al-Mashay retained access to State resources and continued to collude
with armed groups actors in both western and eastern Libya. Al-Mashay continued to facilitate the diversion of fuel officially
allocated to GECOL unchecked, thereby contributing to broader patterns of public fund misappropriation and financing of
armed groups. The Panel also established that there is a direct relationship between Al-Mashay and LAAF top command.
26. Imported diesel remained the primary illicitly exported product. GECOL’s demand for diesel remained at highly
exaggerated levels. Despite several requests to meet Chairman Al-Mashay, the Panel was only able to meet with GECOL
representatives at the technical level. In turn, they provided a large amount of data to explain GECOL electricity production.
However, that data failed to substantiate claims that all fuel ordered by GECOL was used for electricity production;130 while
diesel was certainly a source for electricity generation, only part of the diesel shipments destined to the power plants were
actually used for that purpose. GECOL pursued a consistent policy of a lack of transparency towards oversight bodies;
during periodic meetings aimed at projection planning and alleviating fuel shortages, GECOL leadership routinely refused
and wilfully ignored requests for transparency regarding their consumption of liquid fuels and natural gas. Thus, GECOL
continued to operate outside the Libyan legal framework, due to the relationship of its chairman with armed group actors.
27. While the NOC is responsible for importing fuel, Brega Company supplies it to the big consumers in Libya. The
handover of responsibility is upon arrival at ports. Brega Company usually receives a bulk fuel load from the NOC onboard
a tanker vessel, which it then allocates to multiple consumers, the largest of which by far is GECOL. However, prior to
delivery to the designated consumers, the fuel was diverted by armed groups, including the allotments for GECOL. For
instance, LAAF directly diverted shipments by sea before they could reach their intended destination.
28. Upon arrival at ports under LAAF control, fuel was directly diverted onwards. While Brega Company may retain de
jure responsibility under contractual or statutory frameworks, it does not exercise effective control at the points of diversion.
Therefore, it is unable to prevent or formally report the diversion without exposing its personnel to serious risks and targeted
__________________

imported refined product, in particular not gasoline or diesel.
