retaliation by armed group actors. The diversion occurs as a result of coercion and circumstances beyond Brega Company’s
reasonable control.
E. Overland fuel smuggling
29. LAAF was involved in coordinating overland fuel smuggling operations, which were routed through ports and
delivery logistics under their control. Fuel intended for GECOL in the east is largely redirected and transported along
established smuggling routes toward Libya’s southern borders, particularly through Al-Al-Kufra and into Sudan. These
operations were conducted under the protection of armed elements and structured to bypass institutional oversight
mechanisms. The persistence of such activities undercut State revenues and led to the deterioration of regional security by
entrenching cross-border illicit networks (paras. 31-39 of the main part of this report).
F. Illicit maritime exports of refined petroleum (appendix 9.C)
30. Since the Panel’s last report,131 illicit maritime exports of refined petroleum continued unabated, with a
diversification in products and locations. While diesel remained the primary product, less refined products were also
exported, such as heavy fuel oil (HFO) and straight-run fuel oil. Benghazi old harbour remained the epicentre for exports
until October 2025, when vessel visits drastically decreased in the context of a modernization of the harbour’s fuel loading
infrastructure, which was completed in January 2026. The Panel further identified that illicit exports expanded to commercial
cargo terminals at other locations, namely Tubruq port and Ras Lanuf harbour since November 2023 and October 2025
respectively. None of these terminals are under the control of the NOC.132 Obfuscation and trafficking patterns further
evolved, differing along the lines of clandestine exports out of all three locations, and grey market exports from Tubruq (see
also appendix 9.C).
31. Illicitly exported diesel was sold as bunker fuel (as marine gasoil, MGO) by tanker vessels at STS locations and
delivered to shore in Greece (para. 37 below) and Egypt (para. 49 below). On rare occasions, direct shipments went as far
as Berbera, Somalia and Port Sudan, Sudan; a shipment of mixed product to a South American country did not arrive (para.
51 below). Less refined or mixed product was shipped by container to the Syrian Arab Republic, Türkiye and the United
Arab Emirates (paras. 39 and 40 below).
32. Over the reporting period, at least 36 tanker vessels illicitly exported diesel from Benghazi old harbour, amounting
to around 578,000 metric tons (688 million litres). The Panel identified that the estimated volume of all exported refined
product across the eastern Libyan ports was close to one million metric tons. As this number only includes those tankers
and voyages that the Panel was able to identify, the actual number is likely higher.
1. Clandestine exports
33. Vessels engaging in clandestine exports continued operating without AIS for extended periods, including during port
calls at Benghazi and sometimes at Tubruq and Ras Lanuf. Multiple STS loading instances were added to the transfer chains
to further conceal the origin of the cargo. The previously identified STS locations133 remained in use, although visits at
Hurds Bank, off port limits (OPL) Malta,134 declined over the reporting period, while visits at the location OPL Port Said,
Egypt,135 significantly increased. That location partly falls within the 18 nautical miles zone where Egypt levies fees for
commercial activities like STS transfers.136 The Panel identified two additional STS loading locations where transfers
between vessels took place: a) in the vicinity of the location that the Panel previously identified as the site where vessels
disconnected their AIS when approaching or leaving Benghazi,137 about 130 nautical miles north of Benghazi;138 and b)

about 60 nautical miles north-north-east off Alexandria, Egypt.139 Some vessels also visited known petroleum STS locations
such as OPL Kaloi Limenes and OPL Larnaca.
2. Grey market exports outside the NOC umbrella
34. Grey market exports were undertaken by vessels that continued transmitting their AIS signals during port calls at
Tubruq port. The Panel had not identified them as previously having loaded cargo in Benghazi old harbour and found that
some of the carriers were unaware that they had been engaging in illicit exports from Tubruq. Notwithstanding the fact that
the NOC export regulations for refined petroleum products are not publicly available, these carriers were under the
impression that they were engaged in legitimate business, having called at a commercial port in a regular fashion, with port-
side services rendered by professional companies,140 and having received the relevant documentation. However, the Panel
found that their justifications were unsatisfactory: a) all cargo documentation reviewed by the Panel showed red flags and
some of it was incomplete; b) cargo was loaded from tanker trucks, which is not only highly uneconomical, as it requires
vessels to remain berthed for extended periods, but it is also uncommon to use truck-to-ship loading for the size of tanker
vessels that called at Tubruq port, which averaged at 8,000 DWT; c) this is in particular the case where a maritime petroleum
terminal operated by the NOC is in the immediate vicinity. The Panel therefore assesses that most carriers were either
complicit in or indifferent to their involvement in illicit exports.
35. In relation to recurring vessel visits, the Panel found that they were implementing schedules based on contracts
between the Arabian Gulf Oil Company (AGOCO) or Benghazi-based oil companies141 and foreign petroleum traders, with
certificates of origin issued by the Tubruq Chamber of Commerce (appendix 9.D). As that entity is not authorized to provide
such documentation, with all exports requiring explicit authorization by the NOC, the Panel assessed that documentation as
both fraudulent and invalid.
36. The Panel reviewed documentation and identified: a) three Turkish companies and one German company responsible
for a total of 28 deliveries of VLSFO (27) and kerosene (1) to Greece.142 The Panel also identified shipments of very-low-
sulphur-straight-run fuel oil to Belgium (3), Egypt (2), Germany (2), Malta (1) and Spain (1).
37. More information regarding the export locations, trafficking patterns, and vessels involved can be found in appendix
9.C.
3. Containerized exports
38. Illicit exports of refined petroleum products were also undertaken using flexi-tanks loaded in containers, transported
both by carriers who were unaware of their involvement in illicit exports and by those who were complicit.
39. In the first category, the Panel identified shipments from Benghazi destined to the Syrian Arab Republic, Türkiye
and the United Arab Emirates, which comprised at least 99 containers loaded with flexible tanks. The cargo documentation
misrepresented the containers’ content as paint solvent or metal scrap, when in fact it was illicitly exported refined petroleum
products. A sample analysis from one of these shipments revealed that the cargo that had been declared as mixed
hydrocarbon and specified as paint solvent, was in fact a mix of diesel and Jet A1 fuel.
40. In the second category, the Panel identified that in August 2025, the MV Aya 1 (IMO 9293557, now Zulfa 1), owned
by UDS Shipping Services LLC (UAE) and ultimately controlled by Ahmed Alushibe (paras. 72 and 115 and table 3 of the
main part of the report), exported at least 22 containers (around 492 MT) with flexi-tanks filled with heavy fuel oil from
Tubruq to the United Arab Emirates. The cargo manifest of the same shipment referred to other containers, exported from
Tubruq and Benghazi indicating refinery byproducts. This was not the only transfer of this type; previous exports had also
included other types of refined petroleum.
G. Alushibe and the banks
41. Ahmed Alushibe is a Libyan national who rose rapidly within the banking sector over the past 10 years with the
support of Saddam Haftar. At the time of writing, he owned several banks in Libya, including Al-Wahda Bank and Al-
Masraf Al-Tijari Al-Watani. The Panel found that Alushibe used the banking sector, with the support of armed group actors,

140 E.g. Alwatania Modern Shipping Co. Ltd.

S/2026/224
to fraudulently obtain letters of credit (LCs) from the CBL. Attempts to conduct an official investigation into the LCs stymied
when an Alushibe-owned bank in Benghazi actively blocked such initiatives. Alushibe resold the foreign currency obtained
through the LCs at black market rates in Libya for LYD to economic actors based outside Libya, enabling him to reach a
significant financial position in Libyan dinars. The Panel found that Alushibe subsequently used the funds at his disposal to
purchase shipments of diverted fuel from armed group actors operating in both western and eastern Libya. With their support,
and his maritime transport capabilities, he facilitated the illicit export of this fuel from Libya, particularly through ports
under the control of armed groups, and resold it for profit. These activities enabled Alushibe to accumulate substantial
financial gains and expand his influence. He also used a vessel under his control to illicitly export petroleum from Libya
(see above para.).
42. Alushibe had been a close associate of Farhat Bengdara (section D above). Several shell companies used by Alushibe,
including UDS Shipping Services LLC, are registered at the Al-Masraf Tower in Dubai, where the headquarters of Al-
Masraf Bank is also located.
H. Criminal network growing through illicit export of petroleum
43. The criminal network previously identified by the Panel143 continued to operate out of Benghazi and expanded its
operations to Tubruq and Ras Lanuf (see above section F). This network proved to be the forerunner in illicit exports of
petroleum from Libya. The combination of its global reach and wherewithal impacted peace and security well beyond
Libya’s borders.
1. Maeen Ali Sharfeddin and the criminal network
44. The Panel identified additional members as well as the leader of the network, Maeen Ali Sharfeddin, a Libyan-
Turkish dual national based in Türkiye (network members and roles in table 9.2). Sharfeddin owns and controls several
companies registered in Istanbul and Dubai, most of which presented minimal physical infrastructure, obscure business
activities, and opaque managerial structures. The Al-Massa Group, one of his primary ventures, purported to operate in
diverse sectors, including jewellery, luxury cars and yacht rentals, petroleum product shipping, and home appliances. The
Panel assessed that these entities are used to facilitate financial transfers in support of illicit smuggling activities.
45. Sharfeddin is a direct associate of Ali al-Mashai144 and engaged in money laundering activities that supported LAAF
top command. Al-Mashai’s command and control over military personnel stationed at Benghazi port and the overall
resources of its old harbour were decisive factors in transforming it into a fuel smuggling hub.
46. The network operated through at least 24 tanker vessels averaging 6,700 DWT that were each owned, managed and
operated by single-fleet, shell companies, most of which had been registered in the Marshall Islands. This was made possible
through network member Selçuk Esenyel, head of a law office based in Istanbul (Türkiye), who served as a qualified
intermediary for company registries; he rubber-stamped due diligence checks or provided “seasoned” clean companies for
the network’s use. Almost all members of the network identified by the Panel in table 9.2 held statutory officer positions
and shares in one or more of these companies. In 2025, Marshall Islands progressively struck out several of these companies
from its register for non-compliance with its terms. A list of the vessels operated by the network, including the network
members and shell companies they are linked to, is in sub-appendix 9.C.2.
47. Since the Panel’s last report, the network increased its efforts to obfuscate its activities. To avoid direct links to Libya,
network vessels often consecutively transferred cargo to one another at STS locations, so cargos would be divided up into
several tranches until they reached their buyers. Some vessels loaded the cargo in Libya, while others remained at STS
locations to then undertake the distribution to buyers.
48. Throughout the reporting period, transfers at and to Hurds Bank by network vessels decreased. During the same
period, exports from Tubruq increased with OPL Port Said as their main destination. Network member Osman Hamed
operated an onshore storage in Alexandria and Port Said, where network vessels called several times to discharge their cargo
__________________
143 S/2024/914, para. 110 and annexes 64-65.
144 A.k.a. Ali Abdel Salam Ahmed; Nationality: Libya; DOB: 18 August 1985.

S/2026/224
(sub-appendix 9.C.6). Network vessels were also active within the 18 nautical miles zone off Port Said where Egypt levies
fees for commercial activities.145
49. The Panel estimates that the combined exports between March 2022 and December 2025 by the network’s vessels
that the Panel identified amounted to 1.25 million metric tons of diesel (1.5 billion litres). At a conservatively estimated
average market rate for diesel in the Mediterranean region of USD 750 per MT between March 2022 and October 2025, the
market value of these criminal network exports would equal almost USD 1 billion.
2. Attacks on State institutions on an international scale: the case of the MT Nobel
50. Illicit fuel exports from Libya went as far as Central America; MT Nobel (IMO 9105114), one of the network’s most
utilized vessels, was carrying a cargo of fuel, part of which had been illicitly exported from Libya, when Panama prevented
her transiting through the Panama Canal in mid-July 2025, despite having paid a fast-track premium.146 This was due to a
commercial dispute lodged with a Panamanian court against the vessel. MT Nobel was sailing under the command of
network member Rajesh Goli with forged insurance certificates, barred maritime safety device147 and no ship logs. The
cargo manifest identified the shipper as Futura Energy DMCC (UAE), with the listed consignee not clearly identified.
51. Network member Esenyel travelled to Panama to obtain the vessel’s release and continue transit through the Canal.
Forged documentation was presented both in Panama and Cameroon, with the latter being the vessel’s Flag State, to feign
a previous transfer of ownership of the vessel. Sharfeddin personally travelled to Cameroon in November 2025, in an effort
to meet a high government official. To facilitate this, the Libyan Embassy to Cameroon requested that meeting by a note
verbale. The involvement of the Libyan Embassy showcased the network’s capacity to mobilize the Libyan Ministry of
Foreign Affairs officials that are affiliated with LAAF. Apart from lobbying, the network also attempted to bribe officials
in both countries, with sums amounting to more than USD 1 million.
3. Impunity at sea
52. The network is also responsible for acts that meet the threshold of criminal acts under applicable maritime
conventions and customary international law. To the Panel’s knowledge, no investigations were launched by any Libyan
authorities regarding these acts.
Armed robbery against MT Florian
53. On 6 December 2023, armed elements controlled by Ali al-Mashai approached the MT Florian (IMO 9046590),
which was at anchorage off Benghazi within Libyan territorial waters, and seized the crew’s passports and ship documents.
On 10 December 2023, the same armed elements returned, accompanied by network members Aleksandros Cenevezoz148
and Sadik Inan. The armed elements rendered the vessel’s communications and camera systems unusable, assaulted the
crew, and threatened them at gunpoint. Under instructions received by phone from Sharfeddin, they compelled the crew to
bring the vessel into Benghazi old harbour. The crew was arbitrarily detained for several weeks in inadequate conditions.
Sharfeddin-controlled ventures financed the provision of the MT Florian’s communications access during the period of
detention, which the group repurposed as a local bunker for its fuel trafficking activities. The Panel classified the incident
involving the MT Florian as an armed robbery against ships.149
Act of piracy against MT Anhona
54. On 24 May 2024, the MT Anhona (IMO 9354521) was located approximately 35 nautical miles north of Benghazi,
outside of Libya’s territorial waters, awaiting the arrival of MT Nobel (IMO 9105114) for a STS transfer of 42,700 metric
tonnes of ultra-low sulphur diesel from the MT Anhona. A Damen Stan vessel150 approached the MT Anhona, purporting
to transport surveyors for cargo sampling. Instead, network members Cenevezoz and Inan, accompanied by armed elements

162 Aka Ali Abdel Salam AHMED; DOB: 18 August 1985; Nationality: Libya.
26-02825 73/299
[[page 74]]
S/2026/224
1. Saddam Haftar relied primarily on Refat al-Abbar163 to exercise his influence and advance his interests within the oil
sector. Al-Abbar began his oil sector career at the Arabian Gulf Oil Company (AGOCO) in Benghazi as a mid-level manager.
Political arrangements surrounding the formation of the GNU led to his appointment as Undersecretary of Oil and Gas in
2021. In that capacity, al-Abbar used his position to develop an in-depth understanding of the institutional and operational
structure of the oil sector and positioned himself effectively to later become Saddam Haftar’s agent within the sector.
2. Al-Abbar functioned as “shadow chairman” of the NOC, exerting direct pressure on its leadership and engaging
directly with international partners, often proposing to resolve crises that he himself manufactured. Although officially
functioning as an adviser to the NOC Chairman, the Panel found that, in practice, al-Abbar was the principal decision-maker
in key negotiations involving the NOC.
3. His affiliation with Saddam Haftar enabled him to monetize his influence with operators accustomed to seeking the
backing of armed groups to obtain or safeguard contracts. Al-Abbar’s association with a longstanding operator in the oil
sector specializing in drilling services illustrated this practice. The Panel found that Al-Abbar coerced the NOC and its
subsidiaries into validating inflated contracts, exceeding standard market rates for similar services, which included daily oil
drilling rig rates reaching USD 24,000 per rig for the benefit of that operator. Increases in the price for that operator’s
services were approved in a fast-tracked manner, inconsistent with existing NOC practices.
4. At the time of reporting, the GNU had not allocated an operational budget for 2025 to the NOC, leaving the
Corporation and its subsidiaries unable to meet their contractual obligations to international partners. The NOC publicly
announced its inability to pay operational subcontractors.164 However, following al-Abbar’s direct intervention, the
abovementioned operator was able to secure the payments for his rigs, bypassing established payment suspension policies.
5. Regarding service contracts in general, the Panel assessed that the lack of transparency in the payments resulted in
some subsidiaries reverting to payment-by-proxy. In payment-by-proxy operations, international partners pay operational
expenses in exchange for a larger share of crude oil which further enabled bypassing oversight in the oil sector for the benefit
of armed group actors.

A. Overview
1. The Panel found that tanker vessels were not used for illicit exports of petroleum in western Libya. However, tanker
vessels illicitly exported petroleum from eastern ports.. Tanker vessels are instrumental to such operations because of their
unparalleled capacity to transfer large amounts of cargo that can in turn be sold at high seas expediently. The Panel has been
reporting about the illicit exports of petroleum via tanker vessels from Benghazi old harbour since March 2022.165 During
the present reporting period, the Panel identified Tubruq port and Ras Lanuf harbour as additional locations for illicit export
of refined petroleum products via tanker vessels, including but not limited to diesel fuel.
2. During the reporting period, the Panel found that all illicit exports took place from commercial sections of Benghazi
old harbour, Tubruq port and Ras Lanuf harbour. These locations operate outside of the control of the National Oil
Corporation (NOC), the only entity in Libya authorized to export refined product. Around 70 percent of all diesel in Libya,
and more recently also heavy fuel oil, are imported to Libya; the NOC reconfirmed to the Panel that it never exports diesel,
gasoline or any other fuel that has been imported.
3. Sub-appendix 9.C.1 provides an updated list of tanker vessels identified by the Panel in Benghazi old harbour. The
Panel identified unique visits only; unless the Panel assessed that a vessel left and returned, continuing presence in Benghazi
old harbour over longer periods is only reflected with the date of the first sighting.
B. Benghazi old harbour
1. Continued exports and subsequent infrastructure upgrade
4. Benghazi old harbour remained the epicentre of illicit export of refined petroleum, namely diesel, from Libya (figure
9.C.1). Since the Panel’s last report,166 illicit exports of petroleum by tanker vessels from Benghazi old harbour (LYBEN)
continued at comparable rates until October 2025, albeit with variations in frequency of vessels present simultaneously in
the harbour. The number of vessels peaked at 16 vessels in December 2024, representing the highest number observed by
the Panel to date. Average numbers reduced to five vessels until the summer of 2025, when the number of vessels peaked
again, reaching 13. (table 9.C.1).
5. Late September of 2025 witnessed a sudden drop in vessel visits.167 The decrease in the number of vessel visits
coincided with infrastructure changes in the harbour. Throughout 2025, pre-existing shelters168 were gradually dismantled;
these shelters provided cover to tanker trucks and housed pumps, hoses, and other equipment needed to transfer the fuel
from trucks to vessels. In early January 2026, six new, larger pumps without shelters were erected on each side of the harbour.
These investments by LAAF demonstrated their long-term commitment to continue and increase illicit exports of petroleum.

166 S/2024/914, paras 106-110 and annex 62.
167 Analysis based on 209 satellite images reviewed by the Panel (source: Planet Labs).

84/299 26-02825
[[page 85]]
S/2026/224
6. The Panel found that a higher than previously observed number of large vessels visited Benghazi old harbour. These
included: a) the two largest vessels operated by the Maeen Sharfeddin criminal network (see also sub-appendix 9.C.2), MT
Nobel (IMO 9105114)169 and MT Angel K (IMO 9208473)170; b) MT Eni (IMO 9258674);171 and c) MT Golroo (IMO
9167148).172 Periodic satellite imagery also showed a higher than previously observed number of tanker trucks on the pier
(see above figure 9.C.1).
7. Between 13 October 2024173 and 31 December 2025, the Panel identified at least 110 visits to Benghazi old harbour
undertaken by 36 different tanker vessels (sub-appendix 9.C.1 for the entire list). Out of these 36, 14 were newly identified
vessels, while 22 had conducted visits previously. The vessels with the most visits to Benghazi old harbour were MT Marisa
N (IMO 8004090)174 and MT Mardi (IMO 8853673),175 which both had visited 23 times since March 2022. Both were
operating under the control of the Maeen Sharfeddin criminal network. Of the 14 newly identified vessels, the Panel
established that four were controlled by the Maeen Sharfeddin criminal network. In total, the number of vessels the Panel
identified as being under the control of the network almost doubled from 12 to 24 (see sub-appendix 9.C.2 for the entire
list).
8. In relation to the 36 different tanker vessels that visited during the reporting period, the most represented flag State
continued to be Cameroon (nine vessels). The remaining predominantly represented flag States were Tanzania (four vessels)
and Comoros (three vessels). Meanwhile, the flags of Liberia, Palau and Saint Kitts and Nevis were used by two vessels
each. Another nine vessels had a different flag State each. The flag States of five vessels were unknown.
9. Since March 2022, the Panel identified a total of 296 visits made by 61 different tanker vessels. Each of these visits
represented an illicit export of petroleum. However, to the Panel’s knowledge, the relevant Libyan authorities did not launch
any investigations into such visits, including through the 2146 focal point mechanism. Sub-appendix 9.C.1 provides a list
of all the visits identified.
10. The Panel estimated that the combined exports since 13 October 2024 amounted to 578,000 metric tons of diesel
(688 million litres), bringing the total illicit exports between March 2022 and December 2025 to 1.73 million MT (2.07
billion litres). At a conservatively estimated average market rate for diesel in the Mediterranean region of USD 750 per MT
between March 2022 and October 2025, the market value of these exports would equal USD 1.3 billion. This amount directly
corresponds to the loss of State revenue. This is a conservative estimate, as these exports only refer to vessels that the Panel
could identify. Therefore, the actual volume of illicit exports from Benghazi old harbour is likely higher.
C. Tubruq port
11. The Panel identified Tubruq port (LYTOB) as an additional point of origin for illicitly exported refined petroleum
products. The trafficking patterns differed significantly from those out of Benghazi old harbour. Vessels operated by the
Maeen Sharfeddin criminal network also exported illicitly from Tubruq, mostly without AIS.
12. The Panel found that illicit exports by tanker vessels took place from Tubruq port’s western quays, both as clandestine
and grey market exports. Refined petroleum products came from two different sources: a) tanker vessels delivering imported
fuel to Tubruq port upon order by the NOC on behalf of Brega Company (see below paras.); and b) the Arabian Gulf Oil
Company (AGOCO) Marsa al-Hariga refinery. In both cases, exports took place without NOC approval.
13. The Brega Company fuel depot in Tubruq is located on the southern shore of Tubruq Bay, adjacent to the Tubruq
power station and AGOCO’s Marsa al-Hariga (LMHR) maritime terminal. The depot operates its own maritime terminal at
Marsa al-Hariga, known as the “Tubruq Petroleum Terminal – Brega Jetty”, which is situated east of the main terminal. On
the opposite side of the bay, next to the city of Tubruq, lies Tubruq Port (LYTOB), which comprises both commercial and
military harbour facilities (sub-appendix 9.C.3, figure 9.C.3.1).
14. Until October 2021, the Marsa al-Hariga Brega Jetty was used for fuel deliveries to Tubruq. Between October 2021
and October 2025, no tanker vessels discharged at the jetty. Fuel deliveries by tanker vessels resumed in October 2025. The
__________________
169 Flag: Cameroon.
170 Flag: Tanzania.
171 Flag: unknown.
172 Flag: Iran.
173 S/2024/914, annex 62.
174 Flag: Cameroon.
175 Flag: Cameroon.
26-02825 85/299
[[page 86]]
S/2026/224
interruption was attributed to a) intermittent technical defects during this period; and b) direct interference by the LAAF in
the delivery process. The Panel found that although the jetty was frequently unserviceable, it was operational during certain
periods, including the summers of 2024 and 2025.
15. The Panel further determined that claims of persistent technical defects were used as a pretext to justify a) fuel
deliveries by tanker trucks from the Benghazi depot instead of Tubruq; and b) fuel deliveries by tanker vessels to Tubruq
port instead of the Marsa al-Hariga Brega Jetty. These tanker vessel deliveries began in March 2024, following the
installation of the necessary infrastructure on Tubruq Port’s eastern quay which was designated “Brega Terminal Berth No.
10”. As this location was not a dedicated maritime terminal, vessels remained at berth for extended periods, while cargo was
gradually discharged into tanker trucks, incurring extra costs. Both the use of tanker trucks for deliveries to Tubruq prior to
2024, and deliveries to the commercial port between March 2024 and October 2025 significantly eased illicit exports by
reducing the transportation distances. However, it had twice the negative impact on the NOC and Brega Company; it incurred
extra costs for planned truck movements that never took place, as well as the prolonged overstay of vessels discharging
through tanker trucks.
16. The Panel identified multiple instances in which tanker vessels were instructed to discharge at Marsa al-Hariga’s
Brega Jetty but were redirected upon arrival by LAAF to discharge instead at Brega Terminal Berth No. 10 at Tubruq port.
17. While tanker vessels were discharging fuel from the eastern quay into tanker trucks, other tanker vessels were
simultaneously loading fuel from tanker trucks at the western quays. The Panel found that the tanker trucks moved directly
from the eastern quay to the western quays, shuttling the fuel between delivering and illicitly exporting vessels.
18. Between November 2023176 and 31 December 2025, the Panel identified at least 86 visits to Tubruq’s western quays
undertaken by 30 different tanker vessels, which loaded cargo from tanker trucks. Out of these 30 tanker vessels, 6 operated
– at least on some occasions – without AIS, and nine were operated by the Maeen Sharfeddin criminal network. The Panel
assessed that at least those nine vessels engaged in clandestine exports, and the remainder in grey market exports. The
clandestine exports amounted to at least 106,680 metric tons of product; the remaining tankers exported at least 270,239
metric tons. Imagery of tanker vessels loading at Tubruq port and a list of vessels are at (sub-appendix 9.C.4).
D. Ras Lanuf harbour
19. Ras Lanuf harbour (LYRLA) is primarily an oil terminal in the Gulf of Sidra, whose quay serves a commercial
terminal for dry cargo shipments for local oil and petrochemical companies. The commercial terminal is not under the
control of the NOC. The cargo quay is seldomly used – in the first nine months of 2025, only three cargo vessels visited.
One or more LAAF TBZ FPVs vessels were usually berthed at the southern section of the quay.
20. In early October 2025, three pumping stations were erected on the northern section quay of that terminal.177 By late
October, tanker vessels controlled by the criminal network started berthing next to the pumps and loaded cargo from tanker
trucks. MT Angel K (IMO 9208473), one of the network’s largest vessels, served as “buffer” bunker vessel for smaller
vessels that would berth alongside her to undertake STS loading in the harbour. Vessels operated with and without AIS in
the harbour.
21. Several network vessels that loaded in Ras Lanuf also directly transported cargo to Hurds Bank and OPL Port Said
for STS transfer.
22. Between October 2025178 and 31 December 2025, the Panel identified six visits to Ras Lanuf harbour’s cargo quay
undertaken by six different tanker vessels, which loaded cargo from tanker trucks. Five of these vessels were operated by
the Maeen Sharfeddin criminal network. They illicitly exported at least 41,950 metric tons of product. Imagery of tanker
vessels loading at Ras Lanuf harbour and a list of vessels are at sub-appendix 9.C.4.
E. Changing trafficking patterns
23. Obfuscation and trafficking patterns further evolved since the Panel’s last report. While vessels continued operating
without AIS for extended periods, including during all port visits to Benghazi, additional STS loading instances, including
__________________
176 First identified suspicious visit by a tanker vessel to Ras Lanuf harbour’s cargo quay.
177 30° 29′ 30″ N, 18° 34′ 53″ E.
178 First identified suspicious visit by a tanker vessel to Tubruq’s western quays.
86/299 26-02825
[[page 87]]
S/2026/224
between vessels of the criminal network, were added to the transfer chains, to further conceal the origin of their cargo,
including at Benghazi anchorage.
24. The Panel found that: a) some vessels, which were not involved in illicitly exporting petroleum from Libya for longer
periods, had returned; b) while in 2024 trafficking occurred almost exclusively via STS loading, 2025 saw increasing
deliveries to Member States not only for grey market transfers but also for smuggling transfers, including to Egypt (sub-
appendix 9.C.5), Somalia and Sudan; and c) sporadic STS transfers took place at STS locations off Cyprus, Greece and
Türkiye.
25. While the previously identified locations for STS activities remained in use – Hurds Bank, off Malta, and another
location off Port Said, Egypt179 – the Panel identified two additional STS loading locations where transfers between network
vessels took place: a) in the vicinity of the location that the Panel previously identified as a site where vessels disconnect
their AIS when approaching or leaving Benghazi,180 about 130 nautical miles north of Benghazi;181 and b) about 60 nautical
miles north-north-east off Alexandria, Egypt182 (figure 9.C.2).
26. Following these multiple STS operations, the Panel established that the diesel illicitly exported from eastern Libya
is: a) sold as bunker fuel (marine gasoil, MGO) at Hurds Bank and OPL Port Said; and b) delivered to shore in Port Said
and sold onward via the sea route, including as MGO.
27. Both Hurds Bank and OPL Port Said are close to busy maritime routes and are crowded with vessels engaged in
legitimate bunkering business. This provided cover for vessels loaded with diesel illicitly exported from Libya to transfer
their cargo via STS loading. The Panel found that Maltese bunkering companies received fuel illicitly exported from Libya
via STS loading with vessels of the criminal network outside of Maltese territorial waters. This included Zeta Energy Ltd.,
whose vessels a) received fuel from criminal network vessels on at least three occasions between August 2022 and July
2024 at Hurds Bank and b) exported refined petroleum products from the western quays of Tubruq port on one occasion on
20 October 2025.
28. Zeta Energy Ltd. (Malta), in response to the Panel’s findings of it receiving fuel at Hurds Bank on three different
occasions, confirmed only one instance of this taking place – claiming that on that occasion it did bunker but only supplied
fuel rather than received it. Regarding the two other occasions, it denied any STS transfers.
29. Regarding the export from Tubruq, Zeta Energy Ltd. shared the contact details of the shipper but claimed that its
vessel had been under voyage charter at the time. Despite the Panel’s request, no copy of the charter contract was shared,
nor was the identity of the voyage charterer disclosed, for alleged confidentiality reasons. Based on the evidence gathered
and all circumstantial evidence considered, the Panel found Zeta Energy’s claims in relation to the STS activities at Hurds
Bank not credible. Regarding the export from Tubruq, without a copy of the voyage charter, the Panel was unable to confirm
whether the vessel was under a voyage charter at the time. Notwithstanding a voyage charter contract, this does not absolve
the shipowner and operator of its due diligence responsibilities.

S/2026/224
A. General threat environment
1. The Panel assessed the threat linked to Libya primarily through the persistence of enabling and facilitative ecosystems,
rather than through immediate operational activity of terrorist groups and affiliated individuals. Structural drivers, including
porous borders, limited State presence and the spillover effects of conflicts and political crises in neighbouring States,
continued to render southern Libya a fostering environment in which entrenched smuggling economies and cross-border
criminal corridors converged. Within this context, terrorist groups and affiliated individuals embedded themselves within
broader illicit systems to generate revenue, particularly those linked to migrant smuggling and natural resource trafficking.
2. The Panel identified multiple instances in which individuals affiliated with terrorist entities leveraged these
ecosystems to generate revenue and facilitate movement. In at least four newly documented cases, terrorist-affiliated
individuals used hawala-based financial transfers to obtain funds from the families of migrants smuggled through Libyan
territory. Such practices allowed these actors to access financing while minimizing exposure to any counter-terrorism efforts.
3. At the international level, the increasing scrutiny to identify suspicious financial transactions compelled terrorist
groups and individuals linked to Libya to adapt their operating models of collecting and managing revenue. The Panel
observed a shift towards low visibility operative models through: a) a diversification of revenue sources; b) an increased
reliance on informal and digital financial tools; and c) an exploitation of online platforms for recruitment. The Panel found
that terrorist groups based in Libya played the role of intermediary in the recruitment for other groups active in the region.
While these adaptations reduced traceability, they preserved regional connectivity, reinforcing Libya’s role as a logistical
and facilitation hub within the wider North African and Sahelian terrorist and criminal networks.
B. Islamic State in Iraq and the Levant - Libya (ISIL-Libya) (QDe.165)
4. The Panel assessed that ISIL-linked activities connected to Libya continued to shift from operational planning
towards a facilitative and enabling role within regional networks. Due to the limited ability to sustain territorial control in
Libya, ISIL-Libya prioritized building resilience, force regeneration and cross-border connectivity over large-scale
attacks.184 As a result, Libya functioned less as a primary theatre of violence and more as a permissive environment
supporting financing and logistical coordination for ISIL-aligned actors operating in Tunisia, Niger and Sudan.185
5. ISIL-linked networks operating in and through Libya benefitted from their ability to embed themselves within
existing criminal economies and migratory corridors, particularly in southern Libya. These environments provided access
to revenue streams, established smuggling routes and vulnerable populations, while enabling ISIL-affiliated individuals to
maintain a low profile and limit exposure to counter-terrorism measures. Concurrently, ISIL increasingly relied on digital
tools, online platforms and emerging financial technologies to sustain recruitment, communication and coordination across
borders.
6. In the Panel’s assessment, these developments indicated that ISIL-linked activities connected to Libya during the
reporting period were predominantly structural and facilitative, rather than dynamic or attack-oriented. The emphasis
remained on maintaining presence, relevance and connectivity within regional criminal and terrorist ecosystems, rather than
on projecting violence within Libyan territory.
C. Organization of Al-Qaida in the Islamic Maghreb (QDe.014)
7. The Panel documented that Al-Qaida affiliates, both Libyan and foreign terrorist fighters (FTFs), remained embedded
within transnational criminal networks operating across southern Libya. These networks continued to provide their affiliates
with protection, access, and logistical support. The Panel observed that such relationships are largely based on tribal and
family ties, which allowed affiliates to avoid detection and sustain their presence in areas where security institutions remain
weak.
8. The Panel further found that these affiliations facilitated the involvement of Al‑Qaida‑linked elements in multiple
illicit activities, including the smuggling of subsidized Libyan fuel to Sahelian markets, arms trafficking across Libya’s
southern borders, and the smuggling of gold from artisanal mining sites in southern Libya and neighbouring countries. For
__________________
184 S/2024/914, para. 25.
185 Confidential sources (CS).
110/299 26-02825
[[page 111]]
S/2026/224
instance, in January 2025, the Panel determined that Al‑Qaida‑linked fighters were using tribal networks in the Murzuq and
Sabha areas to secure their safe transit and protection during their movement.
9. In February 2025, the Panel found that Al-Qaida affiliates collaborated with local smugglers in Umm al‑Aranib to
move fuel along established illicit supply chains. Similarly, in March 2025, Nigerien security forces intercepted small arms
shipments traced to Libya‑based Al‑Qaida facilitators operating along border routes.186
10. The Panel found that in April 2025, Libyan‑based gold traders collaborated with terrorist foreign fighters linked to
Jamaʿa Nuṣrat ul‑Islām wa al‑Muslimīn (JNIM, QDe.159) to export gold from artisanal mining sites in the Sahel, through
Libyan territory, to international markets. These findings indicate that, while the affiliates have limited capability to conduct
large‑scale attacks within Libya, they remain an integral part of regional transnational criminal and terrorist financing
networks.
11. The Panel also found that tribal ties allowed AQIM (QDe.014) affiliates to evade counter‑terrorism efforts in southern
Libya. In several instances, security operations targeting Al‑Qaida‑linked individuals were unsuccessful as tribal
connections provided them with shelter, advance warning, and facilitated their movement across porous borders.187

26-02825 111/299
[[page 112]]
S/2026/224
1. Following the dismissal of Hassan al-Zadma, the logistical support system sustaining the Rapid Support Forces (RSF)
through southern Libya underwent a significant reconfiguration. Between January 2025 and January 2026, with the tacit
approval of the Libyan Arab armed forces (LAAF), the Subul Al-Salam battalion consolidated control over supply chains
used to provide fighters and deliver arms and related materiel from Libya into Sudan in support of RSF-affiliated factions.
The Panel found that the leader of Subul Al-Salam, Abdulrahman Hashim al-Kilani, leveraged his personal connections
within the Tubu communities in southern Libya and strong network in Al-Al-Kufra area to ensure the continuity of the
supply chain to RSF through Libya. This reconfiguration altered the regional power balance in southern Libya by
concentrating cross-border logistical influence within a single armed actor. By allowing Subul Al-Salam to have a near-
monopoly on the supply chains, rendering it easier for anti-RSF actors to identify and target them, increasing the
vulnerability of Libya to spillover from the Sudan conflict. The Panel assessed that Subul Al-Salam assuming such a pivotal
role could not have come about without – in the very least – the tacit consent of LAAF.
2. This consolidation relied on Subul Al-Salam’s territorial presence, knowledge of desert routes and tribal connections,
resulting in the use of multiple and adaptable corridors. In response to the evolving regional security dynamics, Subul Al-
Salam and RSF alternated between routes, maintaining parallel logistical pathways to ensure the continuity and efficiency
for the provision of fighters, and deliveries of fuel, arms and related materiel in an interconnected and centralised manner.
Within Libya, this adaptability reinforced Subul Al-Salam’s de facto control over southern transit corridors, in a security
environment regulated by armed actors rather than the State.
3. On 13 November 2025, the LAAF and the Chadian National Army announced the establishment of a joint force to
secure the Libya-Chad border, with the Libyan component drawn from Subul Al-Salam. The Panel assessed that this
arrangement placed Subul Al-Salam in a position to further consolidate control over key routes used to supply the RSF
through Libya and Chad, largely across territory under the effective control of Tubu communities.
4. The Panel found that in its newly assumed role, Subul Al-Salam exercised functional control across multiple stages
of the RSF supply chain, encompassing key logistical, security and facilitation components required to sustain these flows.
In early June 2025, Subul Al-Salam directly supported the RSF armed operations by deploying units on the ground,
providing fighters to RSF-affiliated factions, escorting them across Libyan territory, and facilitating access to fuel and
vehicle spare parts. These actions culminated in the RSF advancing into the Al-Aouinat area, where they consolidated
control over key positions. This level of operational integration blurred the distinction between local Libyan armed actors
and foreign conflict parties, particularly during armed clashes.
5. The Panel assessed that Subul Al-Salam’s support to RSF had a direct impact on border security in southern Libya,
including through the facilitation of cross-border illicit activities and the empowerment of non-State armed actors in the
Fezzan region. As a result, southern Libya increasingly functioned as a fostering environment for terrorist groups and
transnational criminal activities, amplifying insecurity across the Libya/Chad/Sudan tri-border area.
6. The continued flows generated a parallel market, resulting in the diversion of arms and related materiel initially
destined for RSF towards markets in Libya, Niger and Chad, further entrenching a war economy along Libya’s southern
borders. The Panel documented one case in which ammunition, a type previously reported by the Panel189 and later identified
in Misrata, originally intended for RSF, was diverted and resold to individuals involved in gold trafficking in Niger and
linked to ISIL (7.62×39 mm; packaging marked “7.62S 120PC 352”, locally referred to as “malleable (lay-yina) bullets”).
The Panel also found that Libyan and Chadian brokers periodically visited the Al-Al-Kufra area to secure a portion of
consignments destined for RSF, which they subsequently resold to armed actors across the region. These diversion patterns
illustrated the convergence of armed conflict, organized crime and terrorist financing across the Sahelian route, reinforcing
mutually sustaining illicit ecosystems.
7. The RSF-affiliated fighters remained present in Libya during the reporting period, resulting in armed clashes between
the Sudanese parties on Libyan territory, notably in June 2025 and November 2025. This presence went beyond tactical
incursions. The Panel assessed that RSF benefited from a rear base operating under the auspices of LAAF, specifically
through Subul Al-Salam, located approximately 75 km south-south-west of Al-Al-Kufra, which enabled RSF to: (a)
coordinate logistical operations from Libyan territory; (b) access the Maateen al-Sarrah airbase; and (c) use facilities190 near
__________________
189 S/2013/99, para. 171.
190 24°14'38.9"N, 23°14'04.3"E.

[[page 113]]
S/2026/224
Al-Al-Kufra airbase that served both as transit points for Colombian fighters and as sites for the modification of vehicles
imported through Libya.
8. From these facilities, joint movements of militarised vehicles and Colombian fighters departed towards Sudan. These
movements further diversified the conflict dynamics, linking Libya-based logistical infrastructure to international fighter
recruitment and deployment patterns.

26-02825 113/299
[[page 114]]
S/2026/224
1. During the reporting period, all activities of the 5+5 Joint Military Commission (JMC) remained focused on ceasefire-
related matters, including the reaffirmation of existing principles, the definition of implementation frameworks and the
advancement of confidence-building measures. Between November 2024 and December 2025, the JMC members
participated in internationally sponsored capacity-building and technical engagements; explored proposals aimed at the
creation of a joint military and security force; issued public statements calling for restraint against hate speech; and engaged
in civil society fora, including outreach to youth on ceasefire implementation; and the development of a database on foreign
fighters. These initiatives, however, remained limited to reinforcing the ceasefire and building confidence among members
of the JMC.
2. The Panel observed growing interest among Libyan military actors in the establishment of joint military or security
bodies. This was partially driven by expectations that progress towards joint structures could facilitate a gradual alleviation
of arms embargo restrictions. The Panel found that alternative or parallel formulas were being explored to bring about
military reunification outside the 5+5 JMC framework. However, the Panel assessed that in the absence of a unified
command structure and effective oversight mechanisms, such initiatives risked remaining ad hoc and creating incentives for
attempts to circumvent the arms embargo under the guise of coordination or cooperation.
3. Since the Panel’s last report (S/2024/914), no progress was achieved toward the creation of a unified joint military
unit. The Chiefs of General Staff in both the west and the east continued to play largely symbolic roles and lacked the
authority or influence necessary to oversee or enforce concrete steps toward military reunification. The persistent
fragmentation of command and the absence of political consensus on security sector governance made any progress on
military reunification unlikely.

26-02825 115/299
[[page 116]]
S/2026/224
A. Overview
1. This annex presents evidence of serious violations of international human rights law committed in the context of
deprivation of liberty: a) in the detention center under the control of the Deterrence Apparatus for Combating Organized
Crime and Terrorism (DACOT) (section B); b) in detention settings under the control of the Internal Security Agency (ISA)
in Tripoli (section C); and c) in detention settings under the control of the Libyan Arab armed forces (LAAF) (section D).
B. Violations of international human rights law committed in Mitiga detention facility under the control of
DACOT
2. The Panel identified six cases of arbitrary and unlawful detention, enforced disappearance, torture and other ill-
treatment, and serious violations of fair trial rights committed against detainees that took place between May 2024 and
December 2025 in Mitiga detention facility in Tripoli under the effective control of DACOT.201 Two of these six individuals
died in custody, with one specifically as a combined result of torture and medical negligence.
3. Five out of six detainees were consistently detained for several years without any judicial review of their detention,
unable to challenge the lawfulness of their detention. They were denied access to a fair and public hearing within a
reasonable time by a competent, independent and impartial tribunal established by law in full accordance with international
standards of fair trial. In four of the six cases documented, when they were belatedly presented before a judge and the court
ordered their release as there were no grounds to keep detaining them, the detaining authority refused to implement these
judicial orders in violation of detainees’ rights to a fair trial and to liberty. None of those released were provided with release
forms or discharge paperwork, rendering it difficult to prove that they were detained in the first place and subsequently
without access to effective remedies and reparation for gross human rights violations that they had allegedly suffered.202
4. The Panel found that the consistent denial of habeas corpus to the detainees held in Mitiga detention facility and the
development of a bespoke parallel quasi-legal system under DACOT203 have been part of a detention regime created to
remove the protection of judicial oversight. In the absence of habeas corpus, detainees have been subjected to indefinite and
incommunicado detention, enforced disappearance, torture or other ill-treatment, inadequate medical care and subpar
conditions of detention, as well as death in custody.
5. All six detainees were subjected to torture or other ill-treatment, including physical and psychological, which fall
under the categories of either treatment or material conditions:
(a) Indefinite and prolonged systemic incommunicado detention, without contact with the outside world for years,
including visits from family or lawyers. Victims spoke of how they were cut off from the outside world as
punishment and how fleeting and intermittent visits from families were when they eventually did take place.
(b) Deliberate denial of the necessary medical care for serious conditions or injuries, in some cases leading to the
deterioration of the health of detainees and in one case, death. The Panel documented incidents where detainees
went on hunger strike to protest their indefinite detention without judicial review or charges. Medical care was
withheld in one case documented by the Panel as punishment for going on hunger strike. Another detainee on
hunger strike was not brought before the court to hide his deteriorating health, even though the court session went
ahead.
(c) Systemic overcrowding due to inadequate physical conditions and insufficient space for each detainee. Victims
described dire detention conditions where 80-120 detainees at a time shared a 25-meter square cell. Detainees had
to take turns sleeping and using the toilet, with their turn only coming every second day.204
__________________
201 Panel interviews with former detainees, family members and eyewitnesses. Articles 7, 9 and 14 of the International Covenant on Civil and
Political Rights (ICCPR).
202 Articles 2(3), 9(4) and 9(5) of the ICCPR.
203 S/2024/914, appendix A to annex 20.
204 The International Committee of the Red Cross (ICRC) recommends 5.4 square meters of minimum floor space per detainee to
prevent conditions that may amount to cruel, inhumane, or degrading treatment. The recommended minimum floor space must be
considered in conjunction with the length of time spent in the cell, sanitation and hygiene conditions, access to sunlight and
ventilation, and the amount of time detainees can spend outside their cells.
116/299 26-02825
[[page 117]]
S/2026/224
(d) Systemic denial of access to outdoors for prolonged periods of time. Victims reported not being out of their
overcrowded cells for up to a year, depriving them of fresh air, sunlight and physical activity. This contributed to
a deterioration in their health generally, including skin diseases, muscle atrophy and mental health issues.
(e) The use of torture or ill-treatment, whether physical or psychological, is widespread and systematic. It is used
during interrogations to extract confessions, including:
▪ Being beaten while being hung from their wrists intermittently on a hoist using a machine, raising them
for 4-5 minutes at a time, with one-minute breaks. In one case documented, this lasted for four hours.205
▪ Being locked up in a coffin made of metal for up to a couple of hours at a time.
▪ Being locked up inside a fridge for prolonged periods of time.
(f) Torture and other forms of ill-treatment were also used for disciplinary reasons, including:
▪ Psychological ill-treatment, including verbal abuse and watching other detainees being beaten up or
dying.
▪ Using shovels and plastic tubes (BBR) all over the body, including on genitalia.
▪ Dismemberment of digits.
1. Recruitment of detainees and forced labour
6. The Panel found that DACOT continued to leverage its direct access to the penitentiary system to forcibly recruit
detainees into their ranks and use them for labour.206 Under the orders of Osama Najim, specific individuals in senior
positions, identified by the Panel, who superve detainees in Mitiga detention facility pressured detainees to join their ranks
in exchange for freedom or reduced sentences or minor benefits. These individuals are in turn promoted and rewarded for
the number of detainees they recruit. In the latest rounds of recruitment, DACOT focused on detainees with specific skillsets
in the areas of information technology, engineering or cybersecurity. These detainees were then made to run social media
platforms on behalf of DACOT, including the production and dissemination of tailored content to whitewash the image of
DACOT and discredit their adversaries. The recruitment focused on detainees who were from Tripoli to allow Osama Najim
to pressure the families of these recruited detainees.
7. Detainees with specific skillsets, such as metalworkers, have also been used for construction work, specifically in
the building of new sections of Mitiga detention facility throughout 2024 and 2025. Whilst some detainees engaged in this
work willingly in exchange for some minor benefit, such as family contact or improved access to fresh produce such as
fruits and vegetables, others were forced and beaten up if they dared to refuse. Most of these detainees are usually foreign
detainees who are deemed to be a low-flight risk.
8. The prohibition of forced or compulsory labour in the context of detention is well established.207 The only exception
that permits such labour is if it was stipulated as part of a sentence handed down by a competent court, which is not the case
in the cases documented by the Panel.208 In fact, in cases documented by the Panel, the labour was neither voluntary nor
compensated, benefiting the private interests of DACOT. Given the prevailing circumstances in places of detention, the
voluntary nature of such labour should be called into question in many cases.
2. DACOT response to the findings of the Panel
9. In its opportunity to reply to the Panel, DACOT claimed that it had no responsibility for detention-related matters
inside Mitiga detention facility as it is merely responsible for providing external security outside the premises.209 However,
DACOT did not provide any supporting evidence to counter the Panel’s findings backed up by extensive independent
documentary and testimonial evidence.

C. Violations of international human rights law committed in places of detention under the control of the
Internal Security Agency in Tripoli
10. The Panel identified one case of arbitrary and unlawful detention, enforced disappearance, torture and other ill-
treatment, violations of fair trial rights and the prohibition of arbitrary deprivation of life,210 specifically in the case of activist
Abdel Mun’im al-Maryami, at the hands of the Internal Security Agency (ISA).211
11. Al-Maryami was initially abducted by a group of six armed individuals in civilian clothing from a shop in Sorman,
west of Tripoli, on 30 June 2025. At the time of the abduction, he was only accompanied by his children, who were left
alone in the car for hours late into the evening. The family of Al-Maryami only learned about the incident when locals
contacted them to alert them about the unaccompanied children.
12. It was only on 3 July 2025 when the family of Al-Maryami officially discovered his fate; on that day, his family was
informed that he was admitted to hospital after falling down the shaft of the stairwell in the office of the Attorney General.
In a public statement on their official Facebook page dated 4 July 2025, the office of the Attorney General announced his
death – under what the Panel assessed as suspicious circumstances – while the deceased was in the office of the Attorney
General, after he was handed over by the ISA.212
13. Confirming the account of the office of the Attorney General to a certain degree, in a public post on their official
Facebook page dated 6 July 2025, the ISA in turn issued a statement retroactively admitting that they had detained Al-
Maryami in line with the applicable framework in consultation with the office of the Attorney General, before they had
handed him over to his office on 3 July 2025.213 On that same day, the office of the Attorney General published a video
footage of Al-Maryami, dated 3 July 2025, which shows his arrival to the office of the Attorney General and his subsequent
fall. In its statements, the Office of the Attorney General labelled the arrest as an “abduction”.
14. The Panel examined documentary evidence including a police report opened following his disappearance, a
preliminary forensic report, a death certificate and a burial permit. The preliminary forensic report stated that Al-Maryami
died due to severe injuries sustained following a fall, including a brain bleed, a skull fracture, a broken spine and ribs, and
damaged internal organs. Al-Maryami family had yet to receive the final forensic report and the outcomes of the
investigation as of January 2026.
15. The nature and consistency of the evidence that the Panel gathered from independent sources, including authenticated
video evidence, established the factual circumstance of the investigated incident and associated IHRL violations that rebut
the information submitted by the GNU.
D. Violations of international human rights law committed in detention places under the control of LAAF units
16. The Panel identified eight cases of arbitrary and unlawful detention, enforced disappearance, and torture and other
ill-treatment committed against detainees that took place throughout 2024 and 2025 in detention places under the effective
control of the LAAF.214 Three of these eight individuals died in custody throughout 2025, all as a combination of torture
and medical negligence.
17. All documented cases concern individuals who were detained for months and years without any judicial review of
their detention, unable to challenge the lawfulness of their detention. Most of them were unaware of their charges or the
reason for their detention in the first place. Six out of eight cases were subjected to enforced disappearances, with the
detaining authority concealing the fate and whereabouts of those detained from their family. Two out of the eight cases
remain forcibly disappeared for the last two years, including one child.
18. At least two of the eight cases documented were detained under the effective control of Tariq Bin Ziyad (TBZ)
brigade in Sidi Faraj, Benghazi. All those detained in Sidi Faraj were civilians, even though LAAF maintained in previous
correspondences that the detention facilities at the TBZ brigade’s military base in Sidi Faraj are exclusively for its military

personnel.215 In fact, LAAF had maintained that there are no facilities within the base for holding or detaining civilians.
However, based on cases documented by the Panel, the TBZ brigade military base in Sidi Faraj have at least two different
detention facilities, all holding civilians: a detention facility for civilians who are deemed to be dissidents who challenged
the status quo, activists or political opponents, and a detention facility called the “forgotten” where detainees have entered
and never emerged.
19. All eight detainees were subjected to different forms of physical and psychological torture or other ill-treatment,
including solitary confinement, hangings and beatings, waterboarding and watching other detainees being physically
tortured. In the case of two of the deaths in custody, when the bodies of those were handed over to their families, fresh
bruises, open wounds and broken bones were observed. One of those deaths in custody concerned an individual who was
detained in Gernada detention facility216 prior to his death.
20. In two of the eight cases, detainees were filmed by LAAF units when in custody to humiliate and degrade them. In
one of those cases, the objective was explicitly to expose the detained person to public scrutiny as the video was published
exposing the identity of the person detained. In such cases, the Panel found that the implicated LAAF units violated the right
to privacy and human dignity, presumption of innocence, and protection from degrading treatment.217
21. In its response regarding torture and ill-treatment, LAAF maintained that “any incidents of torture or ill-treatment do
not represent a general policy or systemic practice” and such allegation are dealt with accordingly in line with the applicable
laws.218 LAAF further stated its “unwavering commitment” to and respect for international law, including the Torture
Convention.219 Meanwhile, in its response on Gernada detention facility, LAAF stated that while the facility and all its
sections fall exclusively under the authority of the ministry of justice, there are in fact two sections inside the Gernada
compound: a) a civilian detention facility under the effective control of the ministry of justice, the office of the attorney
general and the military police; b) a military detention facility under the effective control of LAAF and the military
prosecution. This directly contradicted previous statements by LAAF that the facility and all its sections fall exclusively
under the authority of the ministry of justice, and that there is no separate detention wing under the command of LAAF and
its units such as TBZ brigade.
22. In line with their legal obligation to address and investigate all documented violations of international human rights
law in the context of detention, LAAF stated that it took a number of steps, including activating internal oversight
mechanisms, launching investigations into alleged violations and guaranteeing the right of detainees to file complaints.

26-02825 119/299

120/299 26-02825
[[page 121]]
S/2026/224
A. Overview
1. Paragraph 11 of resolution 2571 (2021) provides the Panel with the mandate to identify individuals and entities
engaging in or providing support for acts that obstruct or undermine the elections, as part of a wider mandate to identify acts
that obstruct or undermine the successful completion of the political transition in Libya. The Panel has yet to determine if
specific individuals or entities meet the designation criteria.
2. Several rounds of municipal council elections concluded during the reporting period; the first round of the municipal
council elections took place in November 2024, as planned. Partially due to interruptions caused by the clashes in and around
Tripoli in May 2025, the second round of municipal council elections was pushed to mid-August 2025. However, this August
round was staggered across three stages in August, October and December 2025 due to several incidents that fell within the
scope of paragraph 11 of resolution of 2571 (2021).
3. The Panel identified 16 incidents in the lead-up to the second round of municipal elections that partially took place
in August 2025 that, considered together with other disruptions, obstructed or undermined the municipal council elections.220
All incidents identified involved perpetrators that were armed actors operating under the de facto authority of armed groups.
The Panel found that armed groups were directly or heavily involved with the day-to-day running of local authorities,
including by having their own candidates and lists, whilst playing a key role in the organization of the municipal council
elections by providing security arrangements. Moreover, following attacks on the premises of the High National Electoral
Commission (HNEC), armed groups positioned themselves afterwards to provide security for the HNEC premises. This
showed that HNEC could not function independently and had to adapt to armed group dynamics to implement their mandate.
B. Disruptive political and legal decisions
4. In the lead-up to municipal elections and thereafter, disruptions were caused by continuous attempts of Libyan
political figures and legal bodies to change municipal boundaries as currency to reward support of armed groups to gain and
maintain control over the local governance, or to weaken opposing armed groups. By way of example, in an attempt to retain
or consolidate power in key areas such as Zliten – one of Libya’s biggest business hubs – political actors across the divide
utilized executive and legal decisions to stymie municipal elections. To that effect, on 14 August 2025, a Benghazi court
issued an injunction to halt the elections in Zliten citing unspecified “challenges”. At the time, this solicited a public reaction
by the High Council of State, which outright rejected the injunction.
C. Coordinated attacks on HNEC premises in the west
5. Immediately before this injunction, in the early hours of 12 August 2025, the office of the HNEC in Zliten was
attacked. At least two cars carrying unknown armed individuals in civilian clothing fired a round of shots at the outer
perimeter wall, the main door and the room of the present guards. The attackers also launched a grenade, which missed the
HNEC office and landed 300 meters southwest of its location, resulting in damage to a civilian home but no casualties. Later
that very morning, an improvised explosive device was also found adjacent to the gate of the Zliten HNEC office, forcing
everyone to evacuate.
6. In a similar fashion, the HNEC offices in Zawiya and Al-Sahel al-Gharbi were attacked simultaneously on 15 August
2025, both suffering arson attacks. In Zawiya, eight unidentified armed men dressed in civilian clothing and balaclavas
arrived at the Zawiya HNEC office in the early morning hours of 15 August 2025. After they imposed confinement on the
two unarmed guards on duty, they proceeded towards the Zawiya warehouse adjacent to the office. The Panel assessed that
the attack was calculated and planned in nature, taking a mere 10 minutes to execute; the attackers used a ladder that they
brought to climb up to warehouse windows to break them all systematically, throwing fuel inside the building using jerrycans.
The fire led to the burning of all election materials destined to be used in local elections in Sorman and Sabratha.
Concurrently, the Sorman Security Directorate (20km west of Zawiya), an entity that is in charge of security during elections,
was attacked with a launched grenade.
__________________
220 Panel interviews with twelve confidential sources including candidates, monitors, local electoral authorities and witnesses.
26-02825 121/299
[[page 122]]
S/2026/224
7. The office of the HNEC in Sahel al-Gharbi (45km west of Zawiya) suffered an arson attack at the exact same time
on 15 August 2025. At the time, five unidentified armed men dressed in civilian and military clothing jumped the HNEC
premises wall and beat up the guards. In a similar fashion to the Zawiya attack, they proceeded to break all the windows of
the warehouse and set it on fire using fuel, leading to the burning of electoral materials.
8. The Panel found that the attacks on the HNEC premises in the west were coordinated and led to the temporary closure
of the HNEC offices for a number of days and the postponement of elections in a number of impacted municipalities to 23
August 2025.
D. Threats against and arrest of electoral candidates and their families, and HNEC staff
9. The Panel identified at least six cases – four in the west and two in the east – of threats, judicial harassment, arbitrary
and unlawful detention, enforced disappearances, and torture and other ill-treatment committed against electoral candidates
and their families, forcing them to withdraw or relocate altogether. One electoral candidate in the east was detained for four
days and interrogated after putting his name forward in the municipal elections. In one case, a female candidate in the west
was forced to withdraw after she was threatened by an armed group, who was backing the opposing list, with the kidnapping
of her daughter. Another male candidate in the west was forced to withdraw and relocate for six months after he and his
family were directly threatened by an armed group, as they wanted their own local candidates to win. In one egregious case
in the west, two of the candidate’s family members were arbitrarily detained for several days and tortured to pressure the
candidate to withdraw. In the east, the Internal Security Agency (ISA) forced any prospective candidates to go through
another layer of security clearing even though they had already been cleared by the central authorities in Tripoli, by making
them fill out a detailed and intrusive security clearance form. Separately, the Panel also identified three incidents of direct
threats against the HNEC staff, two in the west and one in the east.
E. Halting of election centres in the east
10. In an organised, simultaneous and systematic manner, the ISA in the east prevented the distribution of voter cards,
confiscated those already distributed cards, confiscated ballot boxes and closed election centres in Benghazi, Tobruq, Marj,
Brak al-Shati, Sabha and Sirte days prior to the planned elections on 16 August 2025. In some instances, the ISA arrived at
election centres the night before or the day-of, armed and threatening in demeanour, to enforce the closures.
122/299 26-02825
[[page 123]]
S/2026/224
A. Overview
1. The following presents evidence of serious violations of international human rights law committed in the context of
the May 2025 events, with a specific focus on the arbitrary deprivation of life of Abdelghani al-Kikli and seven of his
guards.221
B. Killing of Abdelghani al-Kikli
2. Abdelghani al-Kikli, former head of the Stability Support Apparatus (SSA), otherwise known as Ghneiwa, was killed
in the early evening of 12 May 2025, alongside seven of his guards in Tekbali camp in Tripoli. Tekbali camp is under the
effective control of 444 Brigade.
3. The operation involving the killing of Al-Kikli formed part of a broader campaign against the SSA, which included
coordinated action by armed groups mobilized in support of Prime Minister Abdelhamid Dbeibah. In the weeks prior, Tripoli
witnessed the arrival of reinforcements from Misrata, including the Joint Operations Force, and last-minute recruited ad hoc
fighters (see paras. 13-14 of the main body of the report). Concurrently, in the weeks leading up to 12 May 2025, the
outcomes of meetings that took place between Al-Kikli and other armed group leaders did not bode well for the heightened
tensions. In fact, in the days immediately preceding his killing, Al-Kikli remained house-bound and steered clear of meeting
with Prime Minister Abdelhamid Dbeibah and armed group actors. Therefore, the killing of Al-Kikli was neither fortuitous
in nature nor did it happen in a vacuum.
4. On 12 May 2025, Al-Kikli eventually agreed to a meeting in Tekbali camp amidst the rising tensions. Even though
he was warned against attending the meeting in Tekbali camp by close advisors, he arrived with twelve of his guards – all
on motorbikes.222 At approximately 07:30PM local time on 12 May 2025, coinciding with sunset prayers, locals living
opposite Tekbali camp reported hearing gunfire which continued for several minutes before it subsided.
5. Within the hour, footage of the killed individuals emerged on social media. One specific scene inside Tekbali camp
was filmed from three varying angles in three different videos corroborated by the Panel. Two of those videos showed 444
Brigade elements kicking the dead bodies; one of those two videos showed at least 19 elements of the 444 Brigade in military
uniform inside Tekbali camp milling around the bodies. In the third video, one 444 Brigade individual can be heard saying
“let him die”. Later that evening, to confirm his death, a photo of Al-Kikli was also shared on social media, showing his
body riddled with at least seven bullets, including one that had led to bleeding at the back of his head. Most of those who
died alongside Al-Kikli exhibited multiple gunshot wounds, one with more than a dozen. Five of his guards who survived
were treated for similarly sustained injuries, specifically multiple gunshot wounds, in two different clinics opposite Tekbali
camp. The video and photo materials were hastily taken down in the hours and days that followed.
6. Following the 12 May 2025 events, Prime Minister Abdelhamid Dbeibah and General Mahmoud Hamza both
released public statements describing the killing of Al-Kikli as a “law enforcement operation”. In a near-ten minute speech
on 18 May 2025, Mahmoud Hamza spoke in front of a room full of 444 Brigade officers. He congratulated them on the
“victory” they had achieved by “overthrowing the Ghneiwa empire” that had been in place since 2011 and encompassed the
biggest geographical area in Tripoli.223 He stated that “every tyrant has an end”,224 adding that “through our operation as the
Libyan army… oppression fell within the hour”.225 In the same vein, in a televised speech on 18 May 2025, Abdelhamid
Dbeibah justified the operation against Abu Salim and Al-Kikli because he had amassed a “militia” whose activities did not
__________________
221 Panel interviews with 27 different confidential sources including victims and eyewitnesses. The Panel reviewed 23 documents
including death certificates, burial certificates and forensic reports. Moreover, it analysed and corroborated videos and
photographs of the victims.
222 Video of Al-Kikli and his guards arriving on motorbikes to Tekbali on record with the Panel.

224 Ibid at 02:00, 18 May 2025
