26-02825 123/299
[[page 124]]
S/2026/224
come under the effective control of the state.226 He said that these activities had “surpassed” their limits227 and he could no
longer put up with this “farcical” situation.228 He concluded by stating that the operation was successful by all measures.229
7. The right to life is deemed as fundamental and non-derogable.230 While the right to life is not absolute, an end to life
cannot be brought about in an arbitrary manner.231 Any deprivation of life that is not anchored in a legal basis or came about
due to acts or omissions is deemed arbitrary in nature.232 The use of force must be exceptional and strictly necessary, and
proportionate and incrementally used depending on the demands of the situation, to achieve a legitimate law enforcement
objective.233 The objective of the use of force must be to protect any person from unlawful violence or effect a lawful arrest
or prevent escape of an arrested person. In all cases, the objective must always be to arrest rather than kill, even if the
individuals are deemed to be violent or belligerent. The use of force must also respect the principle of precaution, which
dictates that such operations must be well planned and carried out in a way that reduces the risk of casualties.234
8. The Panel assessed that Abdelhamid Dbeibah and Mahmoud Hamza should have known that a planned law
enforcement operation such as this one would result in casualties, given the nature and location of the meeting, coupled with
the security status of those attending. Mahmoud Hamza, acting in an official capacity as head of the 444 Brigade, carried
out a law enforcement operation – on his own turf - that resulted in eight deaths. The Panel assessed that it would be
impossible for Mahmoud Hamza to carry out such an operation without the consent/acquiescence of or direct order by his
Minister of Defence, Abdelhamid Dbeibah. In fact, in his televised speech on 18 May 2025, Abdelhamid Dbeibah directly
implied that the operation had his approval. Given the build-up of tension in and around Tripoli in the days and weeks prior,
and amongst the involved actors themselves, the intention to kill rather than arrest Al-Kikli and his guards can be inferred
from the set-up of the meeting in Tekbali camp, coupled with forensic and circumstantial evidence; all of those who had
been killed were shot numerous times. The mistreatment of the remains further implies an intent to degrade and humiliate,
constituting a violation of the dignity of the deceased. In the hours that followed, armed elements – including those belonging
to 444 Brigade – targeted anyone deemed to be aligned with Al-Kikli and proceeded to burn, ransack and destroy their
private property, forcing many to flee the area and permanently relocate. This was followed by an immediate issuance of a
decision to dismantle the SSA and stop their salaries overnight.
9. Given the preponderance of evidence, the Panel found that the killing of Al-Kikli and his guards amounted to
violations of, amongst others, the prohibition of arbitrary deprivation of life, the right to privacy, and the prohibition of cruel,
inhuman and degrading treatment.
1. Response of the Government of National Unity to the Panel’s findings
10. In response to the Panel’s findings, the Government of National Unity (GNU) confirmed the killing of Al-Kikli
alongside other SSA elements who accompanied him on 12 May 2025 inside Tekbali camp.235 According to the reply,
despite being permitted into Tekbali camp after he presented himself at the gate alongside his men, Al-Kikli was killed on
the grounds of armed and impermissible entry into Tekbali. A verbal altercation between Al-Kikli and a number of 444
Brigade elements led to an exchange of fire, resulting in casualties on both sides. The Panel found the explanation
contradictory and inconsistent with its findings. The GNU also stated that an investigation was still ongoing by the
Military Prosecution.

1. The Panel identified identical uncrewed aerial vehicle (UAV) ground control stations (GCS) which were being
constructed at Mitiga,236 Misrata237 and Al Khadim238 airbases. The Panel found that the planned use of these infrastructures
included stationing and launching of Turkish Bayraktar UAVs. These stations would have the capacity for stationing 12-13
UAVs of different sizes. One Turkish Songar and three smaller UAVs had already been stationed at Misrata airbase.239
2. With varying starting times of construction (see figures 17.1 to 17.9), these infrastructures were identical both in shape
and size (see figures 17.10 to 17.12). In the shape of the English letter “E”, the UAV GCS consisted of a main structure and
three extensions on one side. The size of the base of the GCS was about 110x50 metres, with each extension approximately
30 metres long. The height of the underground section at the Misrata UAV GCS was about 9 to 10.5 metres. The reinforced
structure with underground section suggested that their use may include UAV ammunition storage, in addition to UAV
stationing and controlling.
3. The construction of the UAV GCS at Mitiga airbase began on 24 July 2023. As at the time of writing of this report,
the concreting for the underground section was completed and the construction of ground structure was ongoing. Situated
at the south-western side of Mitiga airbase, the UAV GCS is about 570 metres away from the two hardened shelters used
for Bayraktar TB-2 UAV operations (figure 17.13 and 17.14).
4. The construction of the UAV GCS at Misrata airbase began in early March 2024. The concreting of the underground
base was completed. Situated at the south-eastern part of Misrata airport, the UAV GCS was about 250 metres away from
and of similar size of the Akinci uncrewed combat aerial vehicle (UCAV) hangar, which was reported in S/2024/914, annex
37 (see figure 17.15).
5. As at the time of writing of this report, the ground excavation of the UAV GCS at Al Khadim airbase, which began
on 4 October 2025, was still ongoing.

1. Violations of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011) in relation to civilian aircraft are reflected in appendices A

128/299 26-02825
[[page 129]]
S/2026/224
1. In its previous reports,240 the Panel reported on the various air routes used on the airbridge from the United Arab
Emirates (UAE) to areas under Libyan Arab armed forces (LAAF) control and the activities of Kyrgyzstan-registered
operator Sapsan Airlines LLC (Sapsan), operating on these routes. In the last reporting period,241 the Panel identified that
Sapsan conducted 221 flights on the airbridge by its two aircraft.
2. The Panel identified that during the current reporting period, in addition to Sapsan Airlines, three additional
Kyrgyzstan-registered operators, Flysky Airlines, New Way Cargo Airlines and Skyline Airlines, operated on the airbridge.
The Panel identified that these airlines conducted 458 flights from late October 2024 to the end of 2025, using 11 Ilyushin-
76TD (IL-76TD) cargo aircraft (see appendices 18.B to 18.E to this annex). During this period, more flights were conducted
between May and October 2025 than the previous reporting period (see tables 18.A.1 and 18.A.2).
3. These aircraft operated on the airbridge via multiple flight paths, including some between Bossaso, Somalia, and
Benghazi and Al-Kufra, Libya. Cargo flight arrivals identified at the Al-Kufra airbase, which had been seldomly used for
such flights before February 2025, increased tenfold from 24 in 2024 to 239 in 2025.242 In line with the pattern of monthly
flight numbers on the UAE-eastern Libya airbridge, more flights to the Al-Kufra airbase were conducted between May and
October 2025 (see table 18.A.3) than other time of the reporting period. During the same period, a hangar at the north end
of the airbase was being reconstructed as at the time of writing (see figures 18.A.1 to 18.A.4).
4. IL-76TD, with a volume of 253 m3 and maximum payload of 50 tons, is a four-engine strategic multirole transport
aircraft.243 The maximum delivery capacity of 455 flights could reach a volume of 115,115 m3 and maximum payload of
22,750 tons. With an estimated 50% of the capacities, a total of 57,557.5 m3 and 11,375 tons of cargo could have been
delivered in the period between October 2024 to the end of 2025.

26-02825 131/299
[[page 132]]
S/2026/224
1. In previous Panel reports,244 the Panel reported on the various air flight paths being used on the airbridge from the
United Arab Emirates (UAE) to east Libya in support of Libyan Arab armed forces (LAAF). The Panel identified that one
Kyrgyzstan-registered operator, Flysky Airlines conducted 259 flights by its five Ilyushin 76 (IL-76) cargo aircraft (EX-
76003, EX-76006, EX-76017, EX-76018 and EX-76022) to and from Libya from October 2024 to the end of 2025. The
Panel further identified that Flysky’s aircraft conducted flights between Bossaso, Somalia and eastern Libya, and between
Istanbul, Türkiye and Benghazi, Libya (tables 18.B.1-18.B.5).
2. In response to the Panel’s inquiry, Flysky confirmed the performance of certain flights, but did not confirm other
flights or claimed that the flights did not occur.245 Flysky claimed that among the flights from different airports of UAE to
Benghazi, Libya: a) aircraft EX-76003 conducted a flight on 8 February 2025; b) EX-76006 conducted flights on 26 October
and 1 November 2024; c) EX-76017 conducted flights on 16 March 2025, “04/09/2025” (the Panel understands Flysky
refers to 9 April 2025), 15 April, 2, 12, and 27 May 2025; (d) EX-76018 conducted flights on 4 and 17 February 2025.
3. In addition to the flights confirmed by Flysky, the Panel identified that: a) the flight paths of the flights conducted by
EX-76003 on 28 January and 1, 3, 4 and 6 February 2025, were identical to the flight conducted by the same aircraft on 8
February 2025, with all flight paths having ended off the Libyan coast near Benghazi with flight status showing as “landed”
(examples of flight data are provided in figures 18.B.1 and 18.B.2); b) although not confirmed by Flysky, the displayed
arrival airports for the flight by EX-76003 on 4 March 2025 was “Benghazi” (figure 18.B.3); c) the displayed departure
airport of the flight by EX-76017 on 16 March 2025 was “Istanbul”, Türkiye, instead of Al Ain, UAE (figure 18.B.4); and
d) the rest of the flights conducted by Flysky as in tables 18.B.1 to 18.B.5 of this annex are listed as examples in figures
18.B.5 to 18.B.8. The Panel found that the information provided by Flysky lacked credibility and was inconsistent with the
Panel’s findings.
4. Flights conducted by Flysky to Libya have met several air delivery profile indicators, including: a) significant number
of flights over a period of time; b) signals from aircraft ADS-B transponders not being available until sometime after take-
off to conceal departure airports; c) signals from aircraft ADS-B transponders not being available before landing to conceal
arrival airports; d) lack of corporate website or very limited contact information on website; and e) limited, inaccurate or
no information provided to requests for information. Thus, the Panel assessed that flights conducted by Flysky Airlines
constituted violations of paragraph 9 of resolutions 1970 (2011).

142/299 26-02825
[[page 143]]
S/2026/224
1. In previous Panel reports,247 the Panel reported on the various air routes being used on the airbridge from the United
Arab Emirates (UAE) in support of the Libyan Arab armed forces (LAAF). The Panel identified that a Kyrgyzstan-registered
operator, New Way Cargo Airlines conducted 15 flights by its Ilyushin 76 (IL-76) cargo aircraft (EX-76026) on the same
airbridge and between Bossaso, Somalia and east Libya from July to September 2025 (see table 18.C.1). Examples of these
flights are provided in figures 18.C.1 to 18.C.3.
2. The Panel requested further information from New Way Cargo Airlines.248 No response was received.
3. Flights conducted by New Way Cargo Airlines to Libya have met several air delivery profile indicators,249 including:
a) significant number of flights over a period of time; b) signals from aircraft ADS-B transponders not being available until
sometime after take-off to conceal departure airports; c) signals from aircraft ADS-B transponders not being available before
landing to conceal arrival airports; d) lack of corporate website or very limited contact information on website; and e)
limited, inaccurate or no information provided to requests for information. Thus, the Panel assessed that flights conducted
by New Way Cargo Airlines constituted violations of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011).

26-02825 145/299
[[page 146]]
S/2026/224
1. In previous Panel reports,250 the Panel reported on the various air flight paths being used on the airbridge from the
United Arab Emirates (UAE) to east Libya in support of the Libyan Arab armed forces (LAAF) and the activities of a
Kyrgyzstan-registered operator Sapsan Airline LLC operating on these flight paths. During this reporting period, the Panel
identified that Sapsan continued to operate flights to Libya, mostly on the same airbridge, by its four Ilyushin 76 (IL-76)
cargo aircraft (EX-76005, EX-76008, EX-76025 and EX-76030), including flights between Bossaso, Somalia and eastern
Libya. The Panel further identified that Sapsan’s aircraft conducted flights between Türkiye and Benghazi (tables 18.D.1-
18.D.4).
2. In response to the Panel’s inquiry, Sapsan confirmed the performance of certain flights, but did not confirm other
flights or claimed that the flights did not occur.251 Sapsan claimed that among the flights from Al Ain, United Arab Emirates
to Benghazi, Libya: a) aircraft EX-76005 conducted flights on 25 November 2024, 23, 25, 27 and 29 April and 14 May
2025; b) EX-76008 conducted flights on 25 March, 1 and 16 April, 11 and 24 May 2025; c) EX-76008 conducted flights on
flight paths other than to Libya on 22 and 24 November, 6, 8, 9, 11 and 12 December 2024; d) EX-76025 conducted flights
on 6 February, 27 March and 13 April 2025; e) EX-76025 conducted a flight on flight path from Al Ain, United Arab
Emirates to Luxor, Egypt instead of to Libya on 21 January 2025; f) EX-76005 was deregistered on 20 May 2025 (Certificate
of Deregistration No. 0522) and EX-76025 was deregistered on 13 May 2025 (Certificate of Deregistration No. 0521).
3. The Panel further identified that, in addition to the flights confirmed by Sapsan: a) the flight paths of the flights
conducted by EX-76005 on 27 and 31 December 2024 were identical to the flight conducted by the same aircraft on 25 April
2025, with all flight paths having ended at locations near Benghazi, Libya with flight status as “landed” (figures 18.D.1 and
18.D.2); b) although not confirmed by Sapsan, the displayed arrival airports for the flight by EX-76008 on 17 March 2025
was “Benghazi”, Libya (figure 18.D.3); c) the flights on 22 and 24 November, 6, 8, 9, 11 and 12 December 2024 were
conducted by EX-76005 and the previous identification of these flights that were conducted by EX-76008 was a result that
Flightradar24.com registered EX-76008’s MSN number of 1033416515 under EX-76005; d) flight data indicated that on 21
January 2025, EX-76025 conducted a flight from a location near UAE to a location near Benghazi with a status of “landed”
(figure 18.D.4); and e) examples of other flights conducted by aircraft of Sapsan taking different flight paths of the airbridge
as in tables 18.D.1 to 18.D.4 of this annex areprovided in figures 18.D.5 to 18.D.10. The Panel found that the information
provided by Sapsan lacked credibility and was inconsistent with the Panel’s findings.
4. Flights conducted by Sapsan Airline to Libya have met several air delivery profile indicators, including: a) significant
number of flights over a short period; b) signals from aircraft ADS-B transponders not being available until sometime after
take-off to conceal departure airports; c) signals from aircraft ADS-B transponders not being available before landing to
conceal arrival airports; d) lack of corporate website or very limited contact information on website; e) current or previous
listings of owner, operator, or aircraft; and f) limited, inaccurate or no information provided to requests for information.
Thus, the Panel assessed that flights conducted by Sapsan Airline to Libya constituted violations of paragraph 9 of resolution
1970 (2011).

26-02825 155/299
[[page 156]]
S/2026/224
1. In previous Panel reports,252 the Panel reported on the various air flight paths being used on the airbridge from United
Arab Emirates (UAE) to east Libya in support of the Libyan Arab armed forces (LAAF). The Panel identified that one
Kyrgyzstan-registered operator, Skyline Airlines, conducted 59 flights using its Ilyushin 76 (IL-76) cargo aircraft EX-76012
on the same airbridge and between Bossaso, Somalia and eastern Libya from February 2025 to the end of 2025 (table 18.E.1).
2. In response to the Panel’s inquiry, Skyline a) confirmed that it conducted flights to Benghazi, Libya by aircraft EX-
76012 on 18, 20 and 22 May 2025; and b) stated it had no information about flights on 8 February, 24 and 26 March and 26
May 2025.253
3. The Panel has further identified that, in addition to the flights confirmed by Skyline: a) the flight paths of the flights
conducted by EX-76012 on 8 February and 26 March 2025 were identical to the flight conducted by the same aircraft on 18
May 2025, with all flight paths having ended at locations off the Libyan coast near Benghazi with flight status as “landed”
(see figures 18.E.1 and 18.E.2); and b) examples of other flights conducted by aircraft of Skyline on the abovementioned
airbridge are listed in figures 18.E.3 and 18.E.4. The Panel found that the information provided by Skyline lacked credibility
and was inconsistent with the Panel’s findings.
4. Flights conducted by Skyline to Libya have met several air delivery profile indicators, including: a) significant number
of flights over a period of time; b) signals from aircraft ADS-B transponders not being available until sometime after take-
off to conceal departure airports; c) signals from aircraft ADS-B transponders not being available before landing to conceal
arrival airports; d) lack of corporate website or very limited contact information on website; and e) limited, inaccurate or no
information provided to requests for information. Thus, the Panel assessed that flights conducted by Skyline Airlines
constituted violations of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011).

158/299 26-02825
[[page 159]]
S/2026/224
1. The Panel identified254 that an Ilyushin IL-62M transport aircraft (registration: EW-450TR, MSN: 4546257), operated
by RADA Airlines, conducted eight flights from locations near Orsha, Belarus, to Benghazi, Libya between 12 November
2024 to 11 February 2025. The aircraft also conducted a flight from a location near Benghazi, Libya, to a location near
Orsha, Belarus on 19 March 2025 without information of a preceding flight to Benghazi (table 18.F.1).
Table 18.F.1
Flights conducted by aircraft EW-450TR
Date Start of Route End of Route Flight Number Status (local)
12 Nov 2024 Near Aerodrome Orsha * Benghazi RDA3910 Landed 5:29 PM
14 Nov 2024 Near Aerodrome Orsha * Benghazi RDA3910 Landed 10:17 PM
18 Nov 2024 Near Aerodrome Orsha * Benghazi RDA3910 Landed 1:07 AM
25 Nov 2024 Near Aerodrome Orsha * Benghazi RDA3910 Landed 11:57 PM
27 Nov 2024 Near Aerodrome Orsha * Benghazi RDA3910 Landed 8:25 PM
4 Dec 2024 Near Aerodrome Orsha * Benghazi RDA3349 Landed 5:36 PM
18 Dec 2024 Near Aerodrome Orsha * Benghazi RDA3520 Landed 9:09 PM
11 Feb 2025 Near Aerodrome Orsha * Benghazi RDA3910 Landed 3:37 AM
19 Mar 2025 Off the coast of Benghazi* Near Aerodrome Orsha * RDA5136 Unknown
*Signal from the aircraft transponder unavailable to allow the display of departure and destination airports on flight tracking platform.
Source: Flightradar24.com
2. These flights have met several air delivery profile indicators, including: a) significant number of flights over a period
on a previously little used route; b) signals from aircraft ADS-B transponders not being available until sometime after take-
off to conceal departure airports; c) signals from aircraft ADS-B transponders not being available before landing to conceal
arrival airports; d) lack of corporate website or very limited contact information on website; and e) limited, inaccurate or no
information provided to requests for information.
3. In response to the Panel’s request for further information, RADA Airlines: a) confirmed that it operated flights to
Libya for commercial and technical landing and refuelling (purposes); b) stated that, in accordance with the documents
provided by the senders, all transported cargo were “general cargo”, including food products, perishable goods, clothing
items, personal hygiene items, medicine, etc. and excluding military or dual-use cargo; c) stated that the documentation
requested by the Panel could not be provided due to (obligations under) national legislation; and d) informed the Panel that
the temporary absence of flight data was not a result of the ADS-B transponders onboard of the two aircraft being switched
off, but was due to the “specifics of the navigation equipment on Soviet-made aircraft (e.g. IL-62) and its interaction with
ADS-B ground stations, as well as insufficient coverage of these stations in some countries, especially on the African
continent.” 255
4. Of the eight flights to Benghazi, Libya conducted by aircraft EW-450TR from November 2024 to February 2025, the
flight routes started from locations close to Aerodrome Orsha (IATA code: TXC, ICAO code: UMIO), Belarus. Despite the
lack of information about the departure airport of these flights on flight tracking platform flightradar24.com (example of
five flights to Benghazi, Libya in November 2024 in figure 18.F.1), speed and altitude of the aircraft during these flights
indicated that the departure airport was indeed Aerodrome Orsha (figure 18.F.2). Aerodrome Orsha was also not displayed
as the arrival airport for the preceding flights (figure 18.F.3). However, the Panel found that Aerodrome Orsha was displayed
as the arrival airport for flight RDA1977, conducted by the same aircraft EW-450TR, from Minsk, Belarus on 2 June 2025
__________________
254 flightradar24.com and ch-aviation.com, accessed on 24 June 2025.
255 RADA letter of 28 May 2025.
26-02825 159/299
[[page 160]]
S/2026/224
and the departure airport for flight RDA1975 to Dubai, United Arab Emirates on 12 June 2025 (figure 18.F.4). This is a
strong indicator that this aircraft operates from/to Aerodrome Orsha.
5. The Panel requested clarification in relation to the response of RADA Airlines on 28 May 2025, including the
technicality of the ADS-B transponders onboard the two aircraft, relevant national legal obligation preventing RADA
Airlines sharing commercial cargo information, and the fact that Aerodrome Orsha was not displayed as departure and
arrival airport for the eight flights to Libya, but was displayed for other flights. RADA airlines responded256 by asking the
Panel to address further inquiries about the type of air navigation equipment of RADA airlines’ aircraft and Belarusian
legislation of protection of commercial secrets to the Permanent Mission of the Republic of Belarus to the United Nations.
RADA Airlines further noted that the service of flightradar24.com is “more of an entertainment nature due to the lack of
obligations or guarantees from the developer”, and it did not know the reason for the discrepancy of Aerodrome Orsha not
being displayed as departure and arrival airport for the eight flights to Libya but being displayed as arrival and departure
airports for other flights.
6. In the absence of cargo documentation, technicality of the aircraft equipment, and Belarussian legislation, although
requested, the Panel assessed that the clarification provided by RADA Airlines lacked credibility. Based on the assessment
of the abovementioned factors, in addition to RADA Airlines’ flights having met five air delivery profile indicators, the
Panel found RADA Airlines in violation of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011) and non-compliant with paragraphs 24
and 25 of resolution 2769 (2025).

162/299 26-02825
[[page 163]]
S/2026/224
1. The Panel identified257 that an Ilyushin IL-76TD cargo aircraft (registration: EW-383TH, MSN: 1013405177),
operated by Rubystar Airways (Rubystar) conducted flights on route from Minsk, Belarus to locations close to Benghazi on
8 and 12 December 2024 (see table 18.G.1 and figure 18.G.1). The Panel also identified that another Ilyushin IL-76TD cargo
aircraft (registration: EW-567TH, MSN: 0083485554) of Rubystar conducted a flight to Benghazi on 14 December 2024
with the status of “landed” (see table 18.G.2 and figure 18.G.2).
Table 18.G.1
Flights of aircraft IL-76TD EW-383TH
Date Start of Route End of Route Flight Number Status
8 Dec 2024 Near Minsk Airport * Off the coast of Benghazi * RSB7682 Unknown
12 Dec 2024 Minsk Off the coast of Benghazi * RSB7681 Unknown
Table 18.G.2
Flights of aircraft IL-76TD EW-567TH
Date Start of Route End of Route Flight Number Status
14 Dec 2024 Minsk Benghazi RSB7681 Landed 6:22 AM (local)
* Signal from the aircraft transponder unavailable to allow the display of departure and destination airports on flight tracking platform.
2. The Panel requested further information from Rubystar Airways. The Permanent Mission of the Republic of Belarus
to the United Nations transmitted a response from the “competent authorities” of the Republic of Belarus which stated that:
a) the flights of Rubystar Airways on the route from Minsk to Benghazi were carried out for the purpose of supplying
assembly units for buses, fully assembled tractors and other agricultural machinery, as well as a wide range of consumer
goods, including food products (milk powder and baby food), clothing, medical equipment and medicines; b) these supplies
were delivered as part of support to the Libyan people in food security and other humanitarian areas, and under commercial
contracts concluded on the basis of requests from Libyan representatives, including local authorities.258
3. These flights have met several air delivery profile indicators, including: a) signals from aircraft ADS-B transponders
not being available until sometime after taking-off to conceal departure airports; b) signals from aircraft ADS-B transponders
not being available before landing to conceal arrival airports; c) lack of corporate website or very limited contact information
on website; d) limited, inaccurate or no information provided to requests for information; and e) utilising a “blocking”
service provided by open-source ADS-B monitoring providers. In the absence of cargo documentation, although requested,
the Panel was unable to assess the veracity of the claimed transported cargo. Based on the assessment of Rubystar’s flights
having met five air delivery profile indicators and the failure to provide commercial cargo documentation, the Panel found
Rubystar Airways in violation of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011), and non-compliant with paragraphs 24 and 25 of
resolution 2769 (2025).

Flights of aircraft B747-329 EW-465TQ

24 Mar 2025 Minsk, Belarus Locations near Benghazi, Libya TXC9731 Unknown
2. The Panel requested further information from the Republic of Belarus. The Permanent Mission of the Republic of
Belarus to the United Nations transmitted a response from the “competent authorities” of the Republic of Belarus which
stated that: a) the flights of TAE on the route from Minsk to Benghazi were carried out for the purpose of supplying assembly
units for buses, fully assembled tractors and other agricultural machinery, as well as a wide range of consumer goods,
including food products (milk powder and baby food), clothing, medical equipment and medicines; b) these supplies were
delivered as part of support to the Libyan people in food security and other humanitarian areas, and under commercial
contracts concluded on the basis of requests from Libyan representatives, including local authorities.260
3. These flights have met several air delivery profile indicators, including: a) significant number of flights on a previously
little used route; b) signals from aircraft ADS-B transponders not being available until sometime after take-off to conceal
departure airports; c) signals from aircraft ADS-B transponders not being available before landing to conceal arrival airports;
d) lack of corporate website or very limited contact information on website; and e) limited, inaccurate or no information
provided to requests for information. In the absence of cargo documentation, although requested, the Panel was unable to
assess the veracity of the claimed transported cargo. Based on the assessment of TAE’s flights having met five air delivery
profile indicators and the failure to provide commercial cargo documentation, the Panel found Transaviaexport Airlines in
violation of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011), and non-compliant with paragraphs 24 and 25 of resolution 2769 (2025).

1. The Panel identified that during the current reporting period, four Kyrgyzstan-registered airlines, Flysky Airlines, New
Way Cargo Airlines, Sapsan Airline, and Skyline Airlines, operated on the United Arab Emirates (UAE)-eastern Libya
airbridge. The Panel identified that these airlines conducted 458 flights between late October 2024 and end of 2025 by 11
Ilyushin-76TD (IL-76TD) cargo aircraft.
2. The Panel also identified the operation of six cargo aircraft by three Belarussian-registered airlines, RADA Airlines,
Rubystar Airways and Transaviaexport Airlines, on the air route from the Republic of Belarus to eastern Libya. One
Belarussian commercial airline, Belavia Airlines, provided flights for the Libyan Arab armed forces (LAAF).
3. The Panel assessed that the actions of these airlines constituted violations of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011),
as summarized in table 18.I.1 below.

170/299 26-02825
[[page 171]]
S/2026/224
1. In paragraph 6 of resolution 2769 (2025), the Security Council introduced a new exemption measure to the arms
embargo, stipulating that, at the request of the President of the Presidential Council, the measure imposed by paragraph 9
of resolution 1970 (2011) shall not apply to any technical assistance or training […] intended solely to promote the process
of reunification of Libyan military and security institutions […] as notified in advance to the Committee.
2. Panel’s engagement with relevant stakeholders revealed that the reason for the infrequent reliance on paragraph 6 was
twofold: a) disagreements amongst the Libyan parties on who should participate; and b) to a lower degree, the unclear role
of the Presidential Council in the process. Both the Government of National Unity (GNU) and the Libyan Arab armed forces
(LAAF) stated that trainings had already been conducted.
3. As of the time of writing, the United States submitted two notifications pursuant to paragraph 6 of resolution 2769
(2025) to the Committee. In one case, the United States notified261 the Committee that, the United States military, at the
request of Libyan authorities, would provide training on military air traffic control in Sirte, Libya, involving one United
States aircraft.
4. The Panel identified, from multiple open-source platform, including the official website of the United States Africa
Command (AFRICOM),262 AFRICOM’s social media accounts263 and the “Libyan army media division”,264 that such
training, involved two United States B-52 Stratofortress aircraft (registrations of 61-0006 and 61-0012, figures 19.1 to 19.3)
transiting Libyan airspace.

5. The Panel requested further information from the United States, the GNU and LAAF, including clarification about the
discrepancies in the number of participating military aircraft. The United Stated responded without clarifying the
discrepancy.265 GNU and LAAF did not respond.
6. The Panel determined that the notification by the United States was not comprehensive due to the discrepancy in the
number of participating military aircraft. Thus, the Panel found the United States in non-compliance with paragraph 6 of
resolution 2769 (2025).

172/299 26-02825
[[page 173]]
S/2026/224
1. The provision set forth in paragraph 7 of resolution 2769 (2025) stipulates that the arms embargo is not to be applied
to military aircraft and naval vessels temporarily introduced into the territory of Libya, solely to deliver items or facilitate
activities otherwise exempted or not covered by the arms embargo.
2. The Panel continued to investigate such temporary entries by military aircraft based on profile indicators (annex 3) to
assess the compliance with paragraph 7 of resolution 2769 (2025), including by submitting enquiries to relevant Member
States. The Panel identified 124 flights of military cargo aircraft based on these profile indicators, undertaken by Italy, the
Russian Federation, Türkiye, the United Kingdom and the United States, using Libyan airfields during the reporting period.
These flights are detailed in tables 20.1 through 20.5 of this annex. The Panel requested further information on the purposes
of these flights and items delivered from these Member States, to establish whether the temporary entries had been
undertaken under the exception of paragraph 7 of resolution 2769 (2025).
3. The Russian Federation responded that: a) it carried out military-technical cooperation with other countries in strict
compliance with the provisions of international law and the requirements of the Security Council; and b) the Russian Armed
Forces air vessels did not transport cargoes falling under the arms embargo.266
4. Türkiye responded that: a) it had been undertaking assistance and advisory efforts in Libya in the fields of training,
demining and disposal of improvised explosive devices (IEDs) as well as humanitarian aid and assistance in the health
sector; b) these activities were being conducted in line with the requests received from the official Libyan authorities, based
on the legitimate Memoranda of Understanding which have been duly signed and implemented in accordance with
international law; c) the support and consultancy activities were designed to assist the formation of a unified regular army
and to help increase the capacity of the Libyan Armed Forces to combat terrorist organizations, especially Da’esh, and to
prevent irregular migration; and 4) the flights were organized to meet the logistical needs of its Armed Forces military
advisers that were in Libya to provide training, assistance and consultancy services to the Libyan Armed Forces within the
established legal framework.267
5. The United Kingdom confirmed that flight number RRR4100 on 25 August 2025, 24 September 2025, 28 July 2025,
and 19 May 2025, and flight number RRR6162 on 2 November 2025, were conducted and stated that the flights were
compliant with resolution 1970 (2011).268
6. Italy and the United States did not respond.
7. Owing to the insufficient information provided by the Russian Federation, Türkiye, and the United Kingdom, and
the non-responsiveness of Italy and the United States, the Panel found these five Member States to be non-compliant with
paragraphs 24 and 25 of resolution 2769 (2025).

A. Overview
1. The Panel identified transfers of 23 vessels to Benghazi between September 2024 and October 2025. Of these, 21
had been transferred from the United Arab Emirates (UAE) and 2 from Singapore. The vessels had been transported to
Benghazi by commercial heavy-lift vessels. The Panel classified five of the transferred vessels as naval-type vessels, and
thus military equipment, and one vessel as dual-use vessel whose transfers to Benghazi constituted violations of paragraph
9 of resolution 1970 (2011) (table 21.1). The Panel was unable to obtain sufficient information on the remaining 17 vessels
to make a determination as to their classification under the arms embargo (table 21.2). All transfers had been organized by
two UAE-based individuals, one of whom the Panel previously identified as having violated the arms embargo.269 The Panel
assessed that the end-user for all vessels was LAAF, specifically Tareq Bin Zeyad (TBZ) brigade.

S/2026/224
B. Grandweld vessels
1. Overview
2. Of the 21 vessels that had originated from the UAE, 15 had been built by Dubai-based Grandweld Shipyards (a
branch of Atos International LLC) in Dubai Maritime City (UAE),270 Port Rashid Mubarak Group Shipyard (AEPRA). All
15 Grandweld vessels had initially been registered under the Saint Kitts and Nevis flag; registrations were terminated
between July and December 2025.
3. Grandweld Shipyards was also the producer of the first 42-metres vessel named after TBZ brigade, the MV Tareq
Bin Zeyad (IMO 9889930), which was transferred to Benghazi in 2023.271 The company did not respond to the Panel’s letter
dated 9 January 2024 in relation to the transfer of that vessel. Appendix 21.A contains sample images of one of the 42-
metres vessels MV TBZ3 (IMO 1044895).
4. With all circumstantial evidence considered, the Panel found that Grandweld Shipyards was wilfully negligent in its
due diligence obligations vis-a-vis the end-user and the intended destination of the 15 vessels.
2. Involvement of network of Amro Ibrahim and Omar Mustafa
5. The Panel identified an additional member and additional companies being part of the previously reported272 network
of Amro Salem Ismael Ibrahim.273 The newly identified individual is a Jordanian national named Omar Mahmoud Hasan
Mustafa.274 The Panel found the network responsible for the commissioning and registration of the vessels, and their
transfers to Benghazi. These individuals, based out of UAE, utilized a set of shell companies, in an attempt to disguise their
involvement.
6. Omar Mustafa utilized the company Multham International Limited (Hong Kong, China) to commission the
construction of at least six of the Grandweld TBZ vessels. The company was the owner, manager and operator of all
Grandweld TBZ vessels. Before their transfer to Multham, TBZ15 and TBZ16 were briefly owned, managed and operated
by 2020 Volume Boats Maintenance & Repairing FZE (UAE), a company controlled by Amro Ibrahim. Before their onwards
transfer to Benghazi, the two Grandweld Al Karama fast interceptor craft (FIC) were owned by Aerotel FZCO (UAE), one
of Amro Ibrahim’s shell companies.
7. Multham International Limited, 2020 Volume Boats and II Solution FZE (UAE) served as shippers on cargo
manifests.275 II Solution FZE had the same business address as a company called 2020 Research & Development Centre by
Volume UAE,276 demonstrating that it operated under the same network. Due to Amro Ibrahim’s repeated utilization of the
number 2020 and the term “volume” across his corporate registration, the Panel identified this company as being under his
control.277
8. The same network was also responsible for an attempted transfer of vessels from the UAE to Benghazi that was
intercepted by Spain in August 2025 (annex 33).
9. In reply to the Panel’s opportunity to respond, Amro Ibrahim a) minimized the extent of his involvement with the
abovementioned companies; b) denied any role in sales and customer interactions regarding the transfers; c) asserted the
civilian nature of the Al Karama vessels. The Panel found the statements made and documentation provided to be
contradictory. Owing to the large amount of evidence the Panel gathered, coupled with all circumstantial evidence, it found
Amro Ibrahim’s statements not credible.

S/2026/224
3. Shipment by MV BBC Rheiderland (IMO 9614696)
10. MV BBC Rheiderland (IMO 9614696)278 transported, as per cargo manifest, seven 22-metres Grandweld TBZ
vessels, and two 17-metres Al Karama vessels, from Port Rashid, UAE (25 August 2024) to Benghazi, Libya (30 September
2024). The Panel classified the two Al Karama vessels as naval-type fast interceptor craft (FIC), and thus military equipment,
whose transfer to Benghazi constituted a violation of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011). Panel classification of the 22-
metres TBZ vessels is pending.
Involved parties
11. Omar Mustafa’s Multham International Limited (Hong Kong, China) was the shipper indicated on the cargo manifest
for the Grandweld TBZ vessels. The shipper for the Grandweld Al Karama FICs was a different company, owned and
controlled by Amro Ibrahim.279 All Grandweld vessels destined to Benghazi were declared as “crew boats” on the cargo
manifest, including the two FICs. The vessel names “TBZ#” and “Al Karama#” were included in the cargo description.
12. A fictitious company, Sabre Yaching [sic] Limited (Isle of Man, UK) was indicated as consignee in Benghazi for all
Grandweld vessels. Multham International Limited and the shipper of the Al Karama vessels, respectively, were indicated
as notify parties. The Panel identified Cigisped Middle East LLC (UAE) as the deck space charterer.
13. BBC Chartering GmbH & Co. KG (Germany),280 the operator of the MV BBC Rheiderland, responded to the Panel
that: a) the signing of carriage contracts had been concluded with a reputable freight forwarder, Cigisped Middle East LLC,
with whom BBC Chartering had years-long experience; b) the contract contained “warranties of the freight forwarder that
the cargo is and will remain non-military, lawful and not violating sanctions”; c) Cigisped Middle East LLC had chartered
the full deck space; and d) details of the cargo had only been obtained once the cargo had been custom cleared, and that the
export procedure had been the freight forwarder’s responsibility.
14. The Panel assessed that none of the above justifications absolve the carrier BBC Chartering from the collective
responsibility and enhanced due diligence obligations deriving from the context of a transfer of cargo to a country under a
United Nations arms embargo: a) neither reputation nor longstanding business relationships guarantee sanctions compliance
by these partners; b) contractual warranties or indemnity clauses only regulate the contractual relationship and eventual civil
liabilities between the parties; c) deck space chartering differs from bareboat chartering in that the carrier retains operational
control of the vessel, and thus bears collective responsibility; and d) export clearances may be based on misdeclarations.
15. BBC Chartering further held that based on the information available to them, they were unable to identify whether
the vessels fell under the arms embargo. The Panel did not concur with this assessment, for the following reasons: a) on 9
July 2024, the Panel sent a correspondence to BBC Chartering relating to the previously reported transfer in March 2024 of
naval-type vessels from Port Rashid to Benghazi by the MV BBC Alaska (IMO 9453793)281 – another vessel operated by
the company – in violation of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011).282 b) immediately after delivery to Benghazi, the MV
BBC Alaska was inspected by authorities of a Member State in relation to the transfer; c) the vessels’ names starting with
“TBZ” and “Al Karama” are clear indicators for military end-users in the context of Libya, referring to LAAF’s TBZ brigade
and the Libyan national army’s 2014 Al-Karama operation; and d) the consignee being a non-existing company in a foreign
jurisdiction is a red flag indicating illicit transfers. Despite its request, the Panel was not provided with the photographic
documentation taken during cargo loading and offloading activities, as customary for transfers of this type.
16. Consequently, the Panel assessed that a) when the MV BBC Rheiderland left Port Rashid in August 2024, BBC
Chartering was aware that the previous transfer to Benghazi by the BBC Alaska in March 2024 was scrutinized not only by
the Panel but also by Member States; b) given the circumstances, BBC Chartering would have had to be aware of the vessels
that the MV BBC Alaska had transported to Benghazi in March 2024, which had been marked with “police” and “coast
guard”, making their official use by armed groups irrefutable. The Panel therefore assessed that, prima facie, BBC Chartering
was wilfully negligent when undertaking the transfer by MV BBC Rheiderland in September 2024.

180/299 26-02825

S/2026/224
17. Cigisped Middle East FZE, without providing any supporting documentation, informed the Panel that: a) they acted
as freight forwarded procuring the deck space for a(n unnamed) client; b) they had not “had sight” of the cargo
documentation at the time of shipment; c) in hindsight and in light of the Panel’s findings, they “recognised that certain
vessel characteristics may warrant enhanced scrutiny when Libya is the destination”; and d) they had taken concrete steps
to improve their due diligence.283
18. The Panel assessed that Cigisped Middle East FZE bears collective responsibility for the transfer for which it arranged
the deck space. Deck space charterers can request cargo documentation anytime; the freight contract itself must rely on
underlying documentation. Wilful blindness is no justification for due diligence shortcomings. Post-factual compliance
improvements, while laudable, do not change the underlying responsibility for an occurred violation.
Voyage irregularities
19. MV BBC Rheiderland sailed around the African continent on its voyage from Port Rashid to Benghazi. BBC
Chartering informed the Panel that initially, the vessel should have discharged some cargo at Gibraltar (United Kingdom)
and then sailed onwards to discharge the Grandweld vessels in Benghazi; that order had then been inversed, upon request
by the deck space charterer, Cigisped Middle East.
20. The Panel observed284 that as the MV BBC Rheiderland approached Gibraltar from the south-west, its automatic
identification system (AIS), for a brief period, changed from Gibraltar to Benghazi as the vessel’s next port of call but
subsequently switched back to Gibraltar. The vessel then crossed the Gibraltar Straight, still broadcasting Gibraltar as its
next port of call. This broadcast was maintained during the port call in Benghazi and the return voyage, until the vessel
reached the Gibraltar Straight.
21. BBC Chartering held that there was no intention to obfuscate the MV BBC Rheiderland’s real destination but did not
provide an explanation as to the reasons for the omitted port declaration. Given BBC Chartering’s previous involvement in
a transfer of naval-type vessels to Benghazi by MV BBC Alaska, and the circumstances surrounding that transfer,285 the
Panel found BBC Chartering’s statement not credible.
Grandweld Al Karama FICs
22. The Panel identified the two FICs named Al Karama 1 and Al Karama 2 as GrandSwift Interceptor vessels produced
by Grandweld Shipyards, marketed for official use.286 The Panel assessed that the two Al Karama vessels were prototypes
as presented on Grandweld’s website, including in a promotional video.287 A model of the same vessel type was also
exhibited, with gun mount, at the Grandweld stand at the 2025 naval defence exhibition (NAVDEX) held from 17 to 21
February 2025 in Abu Dhabi (appendix 21.B).288 Owing to their design, structure and designation, the Panel assessed these
vessels as naval-type vessels, and thus military equipment, whose transfer to Benghazi constituted a violation of paragraph
9 of resolution 1970 (2011).
23. The Panel found the following entities in violation of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011) for the transfer of the
two Al Karama FICs to Benghazi:
a) BBC Chartering GmbH & Co. KG (Germany) as the carrier;
b) Cigisped Middle East FZE (UAE) as the deck space charterer;
c) Grandweld Shipyards (UAE) as the producer;
d) Aerotel FZCO (UAE) as the registrar;
e) Amro Salem Ismael Ibrahim289 as representative Aerotel FZCO and the shipper;
f) LAAF as the end-user.
24. The Panel found the UAE in non-compliance with paragraph 19 of resolution 2213 (2015), for failing to inspect the
vessel despite indicators of prohibited items.

4. Shipment by MV Bahri Diriyah (IMO 9498482)
25. MV Bahri Diriyah (IMO 9498482)290 transported, as per cargo manifest, four 35-metres Grandweld TBZ “work
boats”, four 14-metres “central console boats” and two 14-metres “landing craft” from Port Rashid, UAE (30 May 2025) to
Benghazi, Libya (22 June 2025). The Panel found that the TBZ vessels’ dimensions had been misrepresented in the cargo
manifest, which in fact were 38 metres long. The Panel classified the four Grandweld TBZ vessels as 38-metres naval-type
fast patrol vessels (FPV), and thus military equipment, whose transfer to Benghazi constituted a violation of paragraph 9 of
resolution 1970 (2011).
26. The remaining two undefined “central console boats” and “landing craft” had the same dimensions indicated in the
cargo manifest as vessels indicated in the cargo manifest of the cargo onboard the MV Lila Mumbai (IMO 9253143),291
which had been intercepted by Spain and inspected by the Panel. In that case, the dimensions had also been misrepresented
on the cargo manifest and corresponded to 15-metres FICs and 17-metres fast landing craft (FLC) marketed by Amro
Ibrahim’s 2020 Volume Boats, both types of which the Panel classified as military materiel under paragraph 9 of resolution
1970 (2011) (annex 33).
Involved parties
27. The shipper was Omar Mustafa’s II Solution FZE (UAE). The vessel names TBZ13 through TBZ16 were included
in the cargo description.292
28. The consignee was Danube Shipping Company (Benghazi), a company that repeatedly appeared as consignee in
Benghazi for transfers and attempted transfers of arms and related materiel whenever Amro Ibrahim’s network was
involved.293 The company had no online presence and did not respond to the Panel’s letter offering an opportunity to reply.
On the occasion of the attempted transfer of UAV components to Benghazi that were seized by Italy in 2024 (annex 35),
Omar Mustafa’s phone number and II Solution FZE email address was provided on the bill of lading as contact details for
Danube Shipping Company as consignee.
Transit through Türkiye
29. On its way from Port Rashid to Benghazi, the MV Bahri Diriyah made a port call in Izmir, Türkiye on 15 June 2025.
When leaving Izmir on 17 June 2025, the vessel declared Benghazi as the next port of call and sailed onwards. Türkiye did
not respond to the Panel regarding this transit.
30. While documentary review of cargo documentation is the standard procedure for port authority clearances, the Panel
assessed that owing to the TBZ vessels’ design and size, their destination being Benghazi, and the name indicating a military
end-user, there would have been “reasonable grounds” for an inspection under paragraph 19 of resolution 2213 (2015).
Grandweld TBZ FPVs
31. A model of one of the 38-metres TBZ vessels transferred to Benghazi by the MV Bahri Diriya, MV TBZ13 (IMO
1089106), was exhibited at the Grandweld stand at the 2025 naval defence exhibition (NAVDEX) held from 17 to 21
February 2025 in Abu Dhabi (UAE).294 The model was navy grey, clearly indicated “TBZ13” and “IMO 1089106”, and had
a bow-mounted machine gun (appendix 21.C). In November 2025, the Panel inspected two TBZ vessels of the same make
and model as the ones transferred to Benghazi by MV Bahri Diriya. Spain had intercepted these vessels in transit to Benghazi
in 2025 (annex 33). Owing to their design, structure and designation, the Panel assessed the 38-metres TBZ vessels as naval-
type vessels, and thus military equipment, whose transfer to Benghazi was a violation of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970
(2011).
32. The Panel found the following entities in violation of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011) for the transfer of the
four 38-metres TBZ FPVs to Benghazi:
a) II Solution FZE (UAE) as the shipper;
b) Danube Shipping Company (Libya) as the consignee;

C. Shipment by MV St Maria (IMO 9467134)
34. MV St Maria (IMO 9467134)297 transported the 40-metres Penguin Flex Fighter Max MV TBZ11 (IMO 1032244)
and the 55-metres Penguin landing craft MV TBZ12 (IMO 9709908), both produced by Penguin International Limited
(Singapore), from Singapore (14 December 2024) to Benghazi (6 January 2025). The Panel classified TBZ11 as naval-type
fast patrol vessel (FPV), and thus military equipment, whose transfer to Benghazi constituted a violation of paragraph 9 of
resolution 1970 (2011). The Panel classified the MV Penguin Transporter as dual-use vessel also subject to paragraph 9 of
resolution 1970 (2011).
1. Involved Parties
35. The producer and seller of the two Penguin vessels was Penguin International Limited (Singapore). The Panel
identified that 2020 Volume Boats Maintenance & Repairing FZE (UAE), and its representative Amro Salem Ismael Ibrahim,
had commissioned the construction of the Flex Fighter Max TBZ11 vessel, and was the buyer of both Penguin vessels.
Penguin International Limited informed the Panel that the vessels’ destination was Libya, and that the end-user of the vessels
was the “Libyan Coast Guard”. No end-user certificates were issued. The sales contracts included a condition that “the
buyers shall not utilise the vessel for military purposes” and that the vessels were “meant for civilian use only”.298 The
contract between Penguin International Limited and 2020 Volume Boats was for delivery in Singapore, rendering 2020
Volume Boats responsible for organizing onwards transport. Penguin Shipyard Asia PTE Ltd (Singapore) was the shipper
indicated on the cargo manifest.
36. The transporting vessel, MV St Maria, is owned by Alicem Shipping Ltd (Liberia) and operated and managed by
HGF Denizcilik Ltd Sti (Türkiye).299 The deck space charterer was Cigisped Middle East (UAE). The Panel also identified
the parties who were responsible for loading the vessels onboard the MV St Maria.300
37. The Panel found that Multham International Limited (Hong Kong, China) was involved in the export procedure, and
that the agent in Benghazi handling the unloading of the two Penguin vessels in Benghazi was Danube Shipping Company
(Benghazi). Both companies are linked to Omar Mustafa.
2. Documentation inconsistencies
38. The cargo manifest showed Penguin Shipyard Asia PTE Ltd (Singapore) as shipper and Benghazi as destination, but
left consignee and notify party fields blank (appendix 21.D figure 21.D.1). Penguin International Limited claimed that for
practical reasons and as standard practice, their company had been indicated as shipper on the cargo manifest (which stated
Benghazi as destination), but that they had not been “privy to the shipping documents”.301 The cargo manifest was stamped
__________________
295 DOB 28 May 1991; nationality: Jordan; Jordanian passport no. R734621 (Expiration: 4 March 2028).
296 National of Jordan; date of birth: 1 June 1986.
297 Flag State: Liberia.
298 Communication from Penguin International Limited, 5 May 2025.
299 Both companies share the same address in Istanbul, Türkiye; S&P Maritime Intelligence Risk Suite and communication from

[[page 184]]
S/2026/224
by Elara Shipping Co Ltd (Liberia),302 Alicem Shipping’s previous company name,303 and signed by the MV St Maria’s
master.
39. The Panel notes that usually, if no consignee or notify party is indicated in a cargo manifest, the bill of lading becomes
a negotiable document of title for the cargo. In international shipping, this can occur if the cargo remains unsold at the time
of lading or if there is no transfer of ownership. In the present case, however, the transport followed a sales contract. In such
cases, the absence of a bill of lading is unusual. Ordinarily, the carrier issues the bill of lading upon receipt of the cargo, and
it becomes a legal title for the cargo that impacts aspects related to its transport, such as liability. The cargo manifest is
merely a summary of all bills of lading relating to the cargo loaded on a vessel.
40. The shipowner, Alicem Shipping, responding to a Panel email addressed to HGF Denizcilik (the shipmanager and
operator) from an email address of Elara Shipping (the legal predecessor of Alicem), informed that no bill of lading had
been issued, and confirmed that Penguin Shipyard had been both shipper and consignee.304 Asked by the Panel for an
explanation for the absence of a bill of lading, Alicem Shipping stated that “shipping by cargo manifest is a common practice
for sea transportation, and the presence of a bill of lading is not necessary.”305
41. The Panel notes that when shipping by cargo manifest, the absence of a bill of lading must be contractually agreed
(contract of carriage). The chartering agreement shared by Alicem with the Panel, however, did not include such agreement;
conversely, it included general terms for bills of lading, suggesting that there had been an intention to issue one.
42. In most Member States, a bill of lading is a legal requirement for the export and import of goods – this is also the
case for Singapore.306 While Libya’s Customs Law No.10307 does not explicitly require a bill of lading, it speaks of a
“general ship’s manifest” in its article 61, also requiring that such document include the name of the consignee. In the present
case, the cargo manifest left the consignee blank.
43. The Panel found that the export clearance for both Penguin TBZ vessels had been obtained by Penguin Shipyard
Asia Pte. Ltd. on behalf of Multham International Limited. To obtain the clearance, the port of Marmaris, Türkiye, had
fraudulently been declared as final destination of the cargo, thus avoiding implementation of paragraph 19 of resolution
2213 (2015) by Singapore.
44. The Panel assessed that the cargo documentation was inaccurate, misleading and incomplete, as: a) the cargo manifest
indicated Penguin Shipyard as the shipper, although the real shipper was 2020 Volume Boats; b) the cargo manifest indicated
no consignee and/or notify party in Benghazi, which concealed the real consignee; and c) by lack of a bill of lading, the
cargo documentation provided to the Panel did not fulfil the legal requirements for export from Singapore and import to
Libya.
3. Transit through Türkiye
45. The two Penguin vessels were loaded on the MV St Maria at Singapore port (SGSIN) between 12 and 13 December
2024. They were transported visibly on the MV St Maria’s weather deck, without any tarpaulin cover,308 together with seven
smaller, civilian vessels, with destinations Marmaris, Türkiye, and Genoa, Italy.
46. The MV St Maria sailed on 14 December 2024 for a westwards journey taking her through the Suez Canal on 31
December 2024. She then proceeded northwards and called at the port of Marmaris (Türkiye, TRMRM) on 2 January 2025.
There, she discharged four of the seven civilian vessels. The port clearance application submitted to the Turkish authorities
by the MV St Maria indicated Benghazi as the vessel’s next port of call. She declared her cargo as “yacht, ship, ferry or

307 Law No. (10) of 1378 FDP (2010 AD), https://lawsociety.ly/en/legislation/law-no-10-of-2010-regarding-customs/; English
translation at https://security-legislation.ly/latest-laws/law-no-10-of-2010-on-customs/.
308 Confidential Member State source.
184/299 26-02825
[[page 185]]
S/2026/224
boat”, with no reference to any military and/or dual-use nature. The port authority authorized the vessel’s clearance to
depart.309
47. While documentary review of cargo documentation is the standard procedure for port authority clearances, the Panel
assesses that the absence of a consignee and notify party in Benghazi, as well as the name of the vessel, “Flex Fighter”,
would have provided “reasonable grounds” for an inspection under paragraph 19 of resolution 2213 (2015).
4. Voyage irregularities
48. After leaving Türkiye, and having indicated Benghazi as the next port of call to the Turkish authorities, the MV St
Maria’s automated identification system (AIS), however, did not indicate Benghazi as destination. Rather, the AIS indicated
Genoa, Italy, as the next port of call, at a time when she was evidently already en route to Benghazi.
49. When EUNAVFOR MED Operation IRINI, only having an aerial asset in the vicinity at the time, conducted a hailing
of the MV St Maria by an aerial asset on 5 January 2025, she informed the Operation that Benghazi was her next port of
call, despite her AIS still indicating Genoa. In the evening of the same day, the MV St Maria’s AIS signal disappeared about
90 nautical miles northeast of Benghazi and only reappeared close to midnight on 8 January 2025, about 9 nautical miles
northwest of Benghazi. The Panel found that during that timeframe, she visited Benghazi port without registering a port call
and unloaded the two Penguin vessels there, between 11:00 UTC on 6 January and 16:30 UTC on 8 January 2025. Alicem
Shipping Ltd (Liberia), the owner of MV St Maria, explained to the Panel that the vessel’s AIS had been “probably blocked
at the moment due to military operations in this territory”.310 The Panel found this explanation not to be credible, as: a)
electronic signal jamming does not cause a total blackout of an AIS signal but rather erratic data points; b) nine other vessels
in the area at the same time encountered no AIS outages and also registered port calls in Benghazi; and c) Panel sources
confirmed that no perceivable military operations had taken place in that area during that time frame. The Panel thus assesses
that the AIS was deliberately disabled by the crew of the MV St Maria.
50. Following the MV St Maria’s departure from Benghazi, EUNAVFOR MED Operation IRINI undertook an
inspection of the MV St Maria. The master provided the boarding team the cargo manifest and claimed that while no bill of
lading was available, he had been authorized to sign such and that he would receive the bill of lading at his next port of call
(Genoa, Italy).311
51. The Panel assessed that the crew on the MV St Maria attempted to conceal the vessel’s location and port visit to
Benghazi, based on: a) the discrepancy between the next port of call, as declared to authorities and as registered in the
vessel’s AIS; b) the gap on AIS coverage during the vessel’s port call to Benghazi; and c) the lack of a bill of lading and the
unusual explanation that such could be obtained post-facto.
5. Penguin TBZ vessels
52. The MV TBZ11 (IMO 1032244) is an a 40-metres monohull aluminium vessel, model Flex Fighter Max, marketed
by Penguin as “multi-role security vessel”312 and as “crew boat/security boat”.313 The vessel was navy grey at the time of
transfer to Libya. It was built in 2024 under the name of Pelican Bold,314 with hull no. 496, upon order from Amro Ibrahim.
It was renamed “TBZ11” in May 2024. The vessel was painted in navy grey, outfitted with STANAG L1 all-round
wheelhouse ballistic protection, two gun mounts with ballistic shields and a concealed utility room with arms locker. The
Panel classified the MV TBZ11 as a naval-type fast patrol vessel and thus military equipment, and its transfer to Benghazi
a violation of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011) (appendix 21.D, figure 21.D.2).
53. The MV TBZ12 (IMO 9709908) landing craft, previously named “Penguin Transporter”, with hull no. 219, was built
in 2014 and is a 55-metres steel-hull dual-ramp landing craft. The vessel was initially white315 but was painted navy grey
before its delivery to Libya.

26-02825 185/299
[[page 186]]
S/2026/224
54. The Panel identified both the FPV TBZ11 (IMO 1032244) and the MV TBZ12 (IMO 9709908) moored at a military
section of Benghazi port, berthed on the same pier (pier 09) as certain rigid-hulled inflatable boats (RHIBs) and smaller
patrol vessels in use by LAAF (appendix 21.D figure 21.D.3).316 Periodic satellite imagery reviewed by the Panel showed
that the vessel remained stationed there.
55. Accordingly, despite its previous civilian use, the Panel classified the MV TBZ12 as a dual-use vessel whose transfer
fell under the category of paramilitary equipment in paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011), owing to its end-user being
LAAF and it being employed for military use.317
6. Party justifications
56. Penguin International Limited told the Panel that: a) “both vessels are essentially civilian vessels and generally have
only regular marine equipment;”318 b) the Flex Fighter Max has ballistic protection in its standard configuration and had
been sold mainly to “private companies for oilfield security and merchant vessel escorts, essentially [for] civilian use;”319
c) it was aware that the vessels’ intended destination was Libya, and that the end-user of the vessels was the “Libyan Coast
Guard”, but no end-user certificates had been issued; and d) the sales contracts included a condition that “the buyers shall
not utilise the vessel for military purposes” and that the vessels were “meant for civilian use only”.320
57. The Panel assessed the following: a) the Flex Fighter Max TBZ11 has wheelhouse ballistic protection, two gun
mounts with ballistic shields and a concealed utility room with arms locker; this is neither customary for civilian vessels nor
regular equipment. The foreseeable end-user and end-use have to be taken into consideration when considering export to a
country under a UN arms embargo, in particular in the present case when the export destination is not under control of the
Libyan Government. b) Standard configurations and customers in non-embargoed environments are irrelevant in the context
of the Libya arms embargo. The Panel found that the Flex Fighter Max had also been supplied to at least one navy.321 c)
Any Libyan coast guard in Benghazi is not under the control of the Libyan Government. d) The end-user supposedly being
the Libyan Coast Guard is in direct contradiction with the alleged civilian use.
58. Alicem Shipping told the Panel that they had not had any direct communication “with the manufacturer, shipper or
consignee” of the transported vessels, as agents had taken care of this. The Panel assessed that a lack of communication and
oversight does not excuse the carrier from due diligence obligations.
59. Cigisped Middle East FZE, without providing any supporting documentation, claimed that: a) their role had been
limited to procuring deck space onboard the MV St Maria; b) they had merely been acting as a broker and not otherwise
involved in other aspects of the transport, in particular not the export procedure;322 c) they had not been provided with
technical drawings that would have allowed them to identify the FlexFighter Max as military equipment; d) in hindsight and
in light of the Panel’s findings, they “recognised that certain vessel characteristics may warrant enhanced scrutiny when
Libya is the destination”; and e) they had taken concrete steps to improve their due diligence.323
60. The Panel assessed that: a) a deck space charterer bears responsibility for the transported cargo that goes beyond
ensuring safe cargo transportation and includes sanctions compliance; b) export clearances bear no relevance on individual
due diligence by each concerned party; and c) the relevant cargo documentation clearly identified the type of vessel and
Libya as its destination; a vessel with the name “fighter” that is destined to Benghazi is a clear indicator for military end-
users in the context of Libya. Post-factual compliance improvements, while laudable, do not change the underlying
responsibility for an occurred violation.
61. The Panel assessed that the enhanced due diligence responsibilities emanating from transports into a country under
a United Nations arms embargo apply collectively to all parties that have operational control, even if only about a section
of the transfer. This applies equally to the charterer and the carrier, in particular in cases where the operational control

remains with the latter. Together with other parties involved in maritime transport, such as deck space charterers or freight

7. Panel assessment
62. The Panel found the following in violation of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011) for the transfer of the two
Penguin TBZ vessels to Benghazi:
a) Penguin International Limited (Singapore) as producer and seller;
b) Penguin Shipyard Asia PTE Ltd (Singapore) as shipper;
c) 2020 Volume Boats Maintenance & Repairing LLC (United Arab Emirates) as buyer and responsible for
organizing the transfer to Libya;
d) Amro Salem Ismael Ibrahim324 as representative of 2020 Volume Boats;
e) Multham International Limited (Hong Kong, China) as party involved in fraudulent export declaration;
f) Omar Mahmoud Hasan Mustafa325 as representative of Multham International;
g) Cigisped Middle East FZE (UAE) as deck space charterer;
h) Alicem Shipping Ltd (Liberia) as owner of the MV St Maria (IMO 9467134);
i) HGF Denizcilik Ltd Sti (Türkiye) as operator and manager of the MV St Maria (IMO 9467134);
j) Danube Shipping Company as representative of LAAF handling the Penguin TBZ upon arrival in
Benghazi;
k) LAAF as the end-user.
63. The Panel further finds Türkiye in non-compliance with paragraph 19 of resolution 2213 (2015).

A. Overview
1. In July 2025, the vessel MV Aya-1 (IMO 9293557)326 carried a shipment of vehicles from the United Arab Emirates
(UAE). The vessel was intercepted by EUNAVFOR MED Operation IRINI but was subsequently released and continued to
Libya. The cargo contained a shipment of 154 vehicles referenced in an end-user certificate issued by the Libyan Ministry
of Defence (MoD), as well as more than 86 additional vehicles of relevance to the arms embargo that were not part of the
certificate. The vehicles were delivered to Misrata and Benghazi.
B. Transfer to Libya onboard the MV Aya-1 (IMO 9293557)
2. The vehicles were part of a containerized cargo onboard the MV Aya-1, loaded in Jebel Ali (UAE, AEJEA) between
30 June and 1 July 2025. The vessel sailed on 1 July 2025, declaring first Fujairah (UAE) and then Suez (Egypt) as next
port of call. After transiting the Suez Canal between 14 and 16 July 2025, the vessel declared Benghazi (LYBEN) as next
port of call.
3. On 18 July 2025, around 25 nautical miles north of Derna, the vessel was approached by EUNAVFOR MED
Operation IRINI, which subsequently undertook an inspection at sea, identified missing cargo documentation and discovered
12 militarized vehicles in some of the containers.
4. Operation IRINI inspected six containers, each of which held two armoured vehicles adding up to 12 armoured
vehicles. Based on the documentary information that was made available to the Panel at a later stage, the Panel assessed that
these 12 vehicles fell into the category of military equipment, as they consisted of: a) four Toyota Land Cruiser (LC) 70-
series-based armoured personnel carriers (APCs); and b) four Toyota LC70-series armoured pickup trucks with gun ports.
As the Panel was not granted the possibility to undertake its own inspection of the cargo, it was not in a position to determine
the make and model of the vehicles beyond the fact that they had been militarized after their exports by Toyota in civilian
configurations to several Member States in the Persian Gulf region. This style of post-export militarization was consistent
with militarized vehicles seized and previously reported on by the Panel, which had been militarized in the UAE.327
5. According to Operation IRINI, the vessel was held for several days at sea, awaiting the assignment of a port of
diversion for a full inspection. The vessel then requested provisioning and servicing, and was ordered to sail to the port of
Astakos, Greece (GRAST). The vessel remained in Astakos port (GRAST) between 27 and 31 July 2025.
6. Immediately after the vessel’s detention, exchanges took place between Operation IRINI and the Libyan Government
of National Unity (GNU). As a follow-up to these discussions, Operation IRINI received on 31 July 2025 an end-user
certificate for 154 vehicles dated 6 May 2025, signed by Abdelhamid Dbeibah, in his capacity as Minister of Defence, and
by the MoD director of military procurement. Subsequently, on 1 August 2025, Operation IRINI released the MV Aya 1
after having requested from the vessel’s owner, UDS Shipping Services LLC (UAE), that the vessel call at Tripoli port
(LYTIP) to deliver the vehicles there under the supervision of the GNU. Concurrently, Operation IRINI wrote to the Libyan
MoD encouraging the GNU to ensure that all military vehicles listed in the end-user certificate were properly unloaded and
placed under its control.
7. The MV Aya-1 then proceeded towards Tripoli until 3 August 2025 when it changed course around eight nautical
miles north-north-east of Tripoli, and called at Misrata (Qasr Ahmed) port (LYMRA) on 4 August 2025. On 5 August 2025,
the vessel left and made subsequent port calls to Benghazi (LYBEN), from 7 to 10 August 2025, and Tubruq (LYTOB),
from 14 to 15 August 2025 (vessel voyage at appendix 22.A).
C. Vehicles onboard the MV Aya-1
1. Vehicles that were part of the end-user certificate
8. The abovementioned end-user certificate for 154 vehicles did not contain information beyond the generic make
and/or base model and vehicle identification numbers (VINs). Some VINs contained minor transcription errors. The Panel
assessed that the VINs referred to: a) 109 Toyota Land Cruiser (LC) 70-series pickup trucks; b) 13 Toyota LC70-series vans;
__________________
326 Flag: Panama.
327 S/2023/673, paragraphs 103 to 105 and annex 71 and S/2024/914, annex 52.
194/299 26-02825
[[page 195]]
S/2026/224
c) 10 LC200-series vans; d) 10 Toyota LC300-series SUVs; and e) 12 Ford F-550 Super Duty with incomplete chassis cab.
The end-user certificates do not indicate any type of armouring or militarization.
9. The cargo manifest contained a total of 359 VINs, including a) 155 Toyota LC 70-series pickup trucks; b) 47 Toyota
LC70-series vans; c) 10 LC200-series vans; d) 28 Toyota LC300-series SUVs; e) 12 Ford F-550 Super Duty with incomplete
chassis cab; f) one Dodge Ram with incomplete chassis cab; g) five GMC Yukon SUVs; and h) 101 SUVs, pickup trucks,
passenger cars and motorcycles of different makes.
10. All 154 VINs indicated in the end-user certificate were also contained in the cargo manifest, taking into consideration
some minor transcription errors (likeness over 90 per cent). The Panel thus assessed that the vehicles contained in the end-
user certificate were those onboard the MV Aya-1. None of the vehicles on the cargo manifest were represented with weights
that would indicate armouring. The Panel assessed that this could not be accurate, because: a) the Libyan MoD informed
the Panel that the cargo was a mix of armoured and unarmoured vehicles;328 and b) of the 12 vehicles documented by
Operation IRINI, and which the Panel classified as military equipment, only the VINs of six vehicles were part of the end-
user certificate. The weight of these six vehicles on the end-user certificate was misdeclared, as the stock curb weight was
provided as opposed to the up-armoured weight, to give the impression of a civilian configuration. The weight of the six
vehicles that were not listed in the end-user certificate had been correctly declared.
2. Additional armoured vehicles identified by cargo weight
11. Based on the weight information contained in the cargo manifest, the Panel identified 86 vehicles whose indicated
cargo weight significantly exceeded the heaviest curb weight of that respective vehicle type. The Panel assessed this as
indicating after-market armouring. As per the cargo manifest, these vehicles included: a) 41 Toyota LC70-series pickup
trucks; b) 31 Toyota LC70-series vans; c) 8 Toyota LC300-series SUVs; d) 1 Dodge Ram with incomplete chassis cab; and
e) 5 GMC Yukon SUVs.
12. None of the VINs of these armoured vehicles were reflected in the end-user certificate. Given the preponderance of
evidence, the Panel assessed that these vehicles were intended for military end-use.
D. Destinations and involved companies
1. 154 vehicles contained in EUC
13. The cargo manifest indicated that 93 of the 154 vehicles contained in the end-user certificate were supposed to be
delivered to Misrata (LYMRA) and 61 to Benghazi (LYBEN), both batches coming from the same shipper in the UAE and
destined to the same consignee with address in Misrata. After the vessel’s interception by Operation IRINI, 123 vehicles
were delivered to Misrata and 31 to Benghazi (table 22.1).

14. The shipper, Noor Al Arab Cargo LLC (UAE) told the Panel that a) they had checked cargo documentation and had
not identified any type of armoured vehicles loaded in the containers; b) all containers contained “normal Toyota” vehicles;
and c) their clients loaded the containers, and they received them in loaded condition.329
15. The consignee, Libomar Shipping Agencies (Misrata), informed the Panel330 that a) the unloading in Misrata of the
containers with the 30 vehicles intended for delivery to Benghazi was done upon instruction from the Libyan authorities; b)
the 30 vehicles had been handed over to a company in Misrata;331 and c) the 31 vehicles that were delivered to Benghazi
had been handed over to two companies in Benghazi.332
16. The importer, and thus the buyer, was indicated in the end-user certificate as Zuhal Aadida for Importing Various
Means of Transporting (Tripoli), based on a contract dated 4 May 2025. The Libyan MoD explained to the Panel that private
companies were used for import of materiel to avoid identification of the MoD as a consignee. The MoD further clarified
that it would face challenges in completing business transactions if it was identified as the recipient of the cargo, due to a
higher level of due diligence applied by the banking sector to them as opposed to private companies.333
17. The email address and the phone number provided for Zuhal Aadia on the end-user certificate were invalid. The
Panel found that a) the email address indicated, “zohil@yahoo.com”, had been deactivated due to inactivity,334 suggesting
that it had not been used since at least October 2024; and b) the company received letters of credit from the Central Bank of
Libya in 2022 (USD 243,188), 2023 (USD 149,337) and 2024 (USD 1,193,558).335 While 2025 numbers have not yet been
released, the purchase of 154 vehicles would have required a significantly larger sum.
18. The Panel assessed that Noor Al Arab Cargo LLC and Libomar Shipping Agencies did not fail in their due diligence
assessments of the cargo since: a) the transfer was a containerized transfer; b) cargo weights were misdeclared to fraudulently
represent civilian-type vehicles; c) both companies were cargo handlers; and d) the containers were delivered to private
companies. Due to these specific circumstances, the Panel further assessed that the three private companies that received
the vehicles in Misrata and Benghazi, and Zuhal Aadia, were either unaware of their name being used or compelled to act
as fronts for armed groups, and thus not responsible for their actions.
19. The Panel assessed the type of transport of the 154 vehicles to Libya as a case of smuggling, owing to misdeclared
cargo and nominal shippers and consignees.
2. Armoured vehicles inspected by Operation IRINI and not part of the EUC
20. As mentioned above, of the 12 vehicles inspected by Operation IRINI, 6 were in included in the end-user certificate.
The remaining six vehicles were four APC-type vehicles, supposed to be delivered to Tripoli, and two armoured pick-up
