22. FDZ Logistics LLC (UAE) informed the Panel that: a) it was only a freight forwarding company acting as a shipping
agent; b) it was not the owner of the vehicles; c) it did not verify contents of containers; and d) that it did not have any clients
in Libya and only followed instructions by clients in the UAE.336
23. The Panel assessed that FDZ Logistics LLC was responsible for serious due diligence failures in respect of the
transfer, given that a) cargo weight clearly indicated a significantly higher weight than the curb weight for the vehicles and
b) Katiba 55 was the consignee. The justifications provided were not valid, as a freight forwarder carries collective
responsibility in the enhanced due diligence environment emanating from a transfer to a country under a United Nations

S/2026/224
arms embargo; in particular, cargo documentation verification would have immediately identified the cargo weights and the
consignee as a red flag. When FDZ Logistics LLC agreed to take on the role as shipper in the transaction, this company bore
the full responsibility in terms of sanctions compliance. The same applied for all companies named in table 22.3 that acted
as shippers for these transfers.
24. The Panel assessed that Sincere Car Trading FZCO (UAE) was the real shipper for the vehicles consigned to the
MoD and the MoI. In the Panel’s assessment, the two companies indicated on the cargo manifest as shipper and consignee
were only nominal consignees.
25. One of the consignees was Katiba 55, otherwise known as 55 Brigade, which is an armed group based in Warshefana,
which was found in violation of international human rights law in the Al Maya detention centre.337 The Panel confirmed that
Katiba 55 received the 26 vehicles, including the six vehicles inspected by Operation IRINI and found by the Panel to have
been APC-type vehicles.
4. Carrier of the vehicles
26. The MV Aya 1 (IMO 9293557, now Zulfa 1) is owned by UDS Shipping Services LLC (UAE) and operated by Al
Fooz Al Qadem Shipping Ltd (Marshall Islands). The Panel found that both companies are controlled by Ahmed Alushibe
(aka Ahmed Gadalla or Ahmed Jadallah) through Alushibe Group.
27. UDS Shipping and Al Fooz Al Qadem showed the capacity to provide end-to-end logistics for UAE-Libya container
transport. UDS Shipping owns a small fleet of cargo vessels and exclusively used shipper-owned-containers on these transits,
which allowed Alushibe to counteract eventual due diligence efforts by involved trade actors. UDS Shipping could offer its
containers to actors that intended to transport embargoed goods to Libya and guarantee safe passage. The interception by
Operation IRINI demonstrated the advantages of containerized transport for such activities: sea-side inspections can only
reveal the contents of accessible containers, while a full inspection is a significant logistical challenge. Al Fooz Al Qadem
also has dependencies at Libyan ports (Al Fooz Shipping), who act as agents in Libya for the receipt of the containers,
enabling a system of cargo documentation using nominal consignees in Libya.
28. The Panel found Ahmed Alushibe as responsible for this transfer.
5. End-users
29. The end-user certificate dated 6 May 2025 identified the end-user of the 154 vehicles as the Border Guard and Critical
Facilities Directorate, for exclusive use by the Libyan MoD. The Panel found that this unit is under the effective control of
Abdelsalam al-Zobi (para 16 of the report).
30. The 86 additional armoured vehicles identified by the Panel were divided up as follows: a) 26 for Katiba 55; b) 23
for the Libyan MoI; c) 16 for the Libyan MoD; and d) the remaining 21 vehicles were unaccounted for (table 22.3).
E. Panel assessment in relation to the arms embargo
31. The MoD contended338 that the Committee had approved a related arms embargo exemption request; the Panel
identified that request as having been contained in a note verbale dated 29 July 2025, thus three days before the release of
the vessel by Operation IRINI. The note verbale referred to paragraphs 2(d) and 2(e) of resolution 2769 (2025), thus
simultaneously invoking an exception and submitting a request for approval. The note verbale included the abovementioned
end-user certificate.
32. The intended end-user as per note verbale and the end-user certificate – the Border Guard and Critical Facilities
Directorate under the Ministry of Defence, and any units under its command – was not included among the forces notified
to the Committee as being under the control of the Libyan Government. The three security forces declared by letter dated
27 May 2017 to be under the control of the Libyan Government are the Libyan Coast Guard, Explosive Ordnance Disposal
units, and the Presidential Guard.339 Of the 154 vehicles in the end-user certificate, 31 were delivered to Benghazi.
33. The two sets of stamp and signature on the end-user certificate were those of Abdelhamid Dbeibah, in his capacity
as Minister of Defence, and that of the Director of the Military Procurement Department of the Libyan MoD. However, the
__________________
337 S/2023/673, paras. 64-65.
338 Meeting with Libyan MoD, 16 September 2025
339 See also S/2023/673, para. 76 and annex 24.
198/299 26-02825
[[page 199]]
S/2026/224
name and signature of the latter individual were not those that had been provided to the Committee by the Libyan
Government. The update reflecting the name and signature of the individual on the end-user certificate was eventually done
in December 2025.
34. Paragraph 2 of resolution 2769 (2025) provides that the measures imposed by paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011),
do not apply to the sale, supply or transfer to Libya of: … (d) non-lethal military equipment, and the provision of any
technical assistance, training or financial assistance, when intended solely for security or disarmament assistance to the
Libyan Government; and (e) arms and related materiel of all types, including technical assistance or personnel, training,
financial and other assistance, as approved in advance by the Committee.
35. Based on the general type of vehicles, and in particular their intended end-user, the Panel assessed the 154 vehicles
to be military equipment. Given that the intended end-user is not among the forces declared under the control of the Libyan
government, and given that one signatory of the end-user certificate was not the one notified to the Committee, the Panel
assessed that the transfer in question, did not meet the technical requirements for the exception to the arms embargo under
paragraph 2 (d) of resolution 2769 (2025). As of the time of writing of this report, no arms embargo exemption request
under paragraph 2 (e) of the same resolution had been approved by the Committee in advance of the transfer. Thus, the
transfer was in violation with paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011).
36. The Panel assessed the transfer of the 86 additional armoured vehicles not contained in the end-user certificate as a
violation of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011), as none of the recipients was among the forces declared to the Committee
to be under the control of the Libyan Government. Out of the 86 vehicles, 39 were consigned to the GNU MoD and the MoI.
The Panel assessed the transfer of these 39 vehicles to Libya as a case of smuggling, owing to misdeclared cargo and nominal
shippers and consignees.
37. The Panel found that, in relation to this transfer, Operation IRINI failed to implement its mandate under paragraph 5
of resolution 2292 (2016), as amended by paragraph 2 of resolution 2733 (2024). Operation IRINI did not take the
appropriate steps after the identification of 12 vehicles it assessed as military equipment. After receiving the end-user
certificate, Operation IRINI failed to identify that the forces listed in the end-user certificate were not among the forces
declared under the control of the Libyan Government. Additionally, it failed to identify that six of these vehicles had not
been included in the end-user certificate, and that their intended recipient had been an armed group, Katiba 55, previously
reported by the Panel in violation of international human rights law. Further, Operation IRINI failed to undertake a thorough
review of the cargo documentation, which would have revealed inconsistencies in terms of consignees and the cargo weights,
and allowed the identification of the additional 86 armoured vehicles.
1. Violation of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011)
38. The Panel found the following in violation of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011) for the transfer of the 240
vehicles onboard the MV Aya 1 (IMO 9293557) to Misrata and Benghazi:
a) UDS Shipping Services LLC (UAE) as owner of the MV Aya 1 (IMO 9293557);
b) Al Fooz Al Qadem Shipping Ltd (Marshall Islands) as operator of the MV Aya 1 (IMO 9293557);
c) Ahmed Alushibe (aka Ahmed Gaddalla) as the responsible party? for the transfer;
d) FDZ Logistics LLC (UAE) as shipper;
e) Sincere Car Trading FZCO (UAE) as shipper;
f) Mujaf Middle East FZE (UAE) as shipper;
g) Africa Auto FZE (UAE) as shipper;
h) Al Bahar Motors (UAE) as shipper;
i) Atqan Company to Import Motors and Transportation (Libya) as consignee;
j) Al Baariq Company for Importing Transportation and its Accessories (Libya) as consignee;
k) Etehad Libya to Import Cars and Parts Company (Libya) as consignee;
l) Al Katiba 55 Mushat (Libya) as consignee;
m) The GNU as the end-user of the 193 vehicles delivered to Misrata for the MoD and the MoI;
n) LAAF as the end-user of the 31 vehicles delivered to Benghazi.

1. On 15 May 2024, Libyan Arab armed forces (LAAF) announced the construction of a “military city” for “training on
all classes, weapons and security sciences” with an area of up to 5,467 hectares, including a seaport and a military airport.340
Located at about 60 kilometres to the southwest of Benghazi,341 the construction of the “military city” began around mid-
June 2023. To date, the construction of shooting ranges, a parade ground, training and several other facilities are completed.
There is no sign of the construction of the seaport or the military airport so far. The development of the construction work
of the military city can be found in figures 24.1 through 24.8.

204/299 26-02825

S/2026/224
2. On 26 May 2025, LAAF held a military parade at this newly constructed “military city”342 to mark the 11th anniversary
of Operation Al-Karama (Dignity). The event showcased an extensive arsenal of arms and related materiel, much of which
were previously unseen in Libya (see appendices A to J to this annex). Examples of showcased arms and related materiel
are provided in figures 24.9 through 24.15 of this annex.
3. A prominent feature of the parade was the display of large quantities of Russian-made or Russian-designed equipment,
most notably two advanced 9K331 Tor-M1-2U surface-to-air defence systems. These systems, capable of engaging targets
at ranges from 1.5km to 12 km and at altitudes from 10m to 6km,343 reinforced LAAF’s air defence capability. The systems
complemented LAAF’s existing Pantsir-S1 air defence systems,344 which were also showcased in the parade. Other
equipment included approximately 120 Spartak/VPK-Ural armoured personnel carriers (APCs) and 80 Tigr-M APCs, three
BTR-82AT infantry fighting vehicles (IFVs) fitted with Berezhok turret with improved electro-optics and fire-control
systems, four BMP-2M IFVs, four 33GT-34039B all-terrain armoured carriers and two BM-30 Smerch multiple rocket
systems.
4. The parade further highlighted the growing role of uncrewed aerial vehicles (UAVs) and electronic warfare (EW)
equipment with a large and diverse array of such systems on display. This included approximately 120 quadcopters with
vertical tubes in the payload bay and 21 vertical take-off and landing (VTOL) UAVs, largely bearing design features of
UAVs manufactured by Chinese companies. Additionally, approximately 225 sets of portable anti-UAV equipment,
including detectors and jammers, and EW equipment mounted on eight Chevrolet Suburban SUVs were showcased. This
underscored LAAF’s adaptation to the role of UAVs and EW equipment in the Libyan context, its evolving capabilities in
these domains, and its option to repurpose civilian products for military use (see appendices K to Q to this annex).
5. The LAAF also demonstrated its enhanced air transport capability. The previously unseen heavy-duty Mil Mi-26
helicopter, with a capability of 20-tons payload, was first identified at Benina Airport on 2 May 2025 via satellite imagery.
The Antonov An-12A cargo aircraft (owned by Space Cargo Inc FZE, UAE), which flew to Benghazi on 31 January 2021345
and has been based there ever since, was publicly displayed for the first time (appendix R to this annex).
6. The Panel found that Egypt provided military assistance to LAAF during the parade. Four Egyptian military transport
aircraft provided flight assistance for LAAF’s paratroopers (annex 32).
7. In response to the Panel’s inquiry,346 LAAF informed the Panel that: a) the majority of the materiel on display had
been previously in LAAF’s arsenal; b) some had been refurbished in “national factories”, without adding combat
improvements and without imports of spare parts; c) some were civilian vehicles militarized within Libya; d) some had been
locally manufactured replicas mimicking Turkish armoured vehicles; and e) some of the materiel had been borrowed from
“friendly and allied” countries and returned upon conclusion of the parade; hence, no purchase nor transfer had taken place.
Specifically regarding the UAVs, LAAF informed the Panel that they had been domestically sourced and manufactured and
were intended solely for “defence and reconnaissance purposes”.347
8. The Panel assessed that: for point a) much of the materiel was previously unseen in Libya; for points b) and c)
refurbishments of armoured vehicles or militarization of civilian vehicles are near-impossible without the imports of spare
parts, which would also fall under paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011); for point d) the Panel did not identify any vehicles
with Turkish design features displayed at the parade; and for point e) even temporary transfers – unless excepted under
paragraph 7 of resolution 2769 (2025) – are double violations of paragraphs 9 and 10 of resolution 1970 (2011) for transfers
to and export from Libya. Regarding the UAVs, the Panel notes that their military purpose and the end-user being LAAF
makes those subject to the arms embargo as paramilitary equipment.

1970 (2011).

S/2026/224
DS-220 UAVs
(LAAF)
(26 May 2025)
The Panel identified, including from open sources,376 the
presence of approximately 120 quadcopters uncrewed
aerial vehicles (UAVs), of which at least some with two
vertical tubes in the payload bay during the military
parade. These UAVs bore design features identical to DS-
220 quadcopters UAVs, which are manufactured by
DSTECHUAS located in China.
DS-220 UAV has a maximum payload of 30 kg, and its
flight time ranges from 40 minutes with a 20 kg payload
to 60 minutes without payload. It can be used in security,
agriculture, mapping, reconnaissance, inspection and other
scenarios. The vertical tubes in the payload bay of the
UAVs can be used for military purpose by installing motor
shells and grenades. 377 The Panel requested further
information from China and LAAF. China responded that
a) it had found no records indicating the export of the
relevant UAVs to Libya; b) it was difficult to determine,
solely on the basis of images, whether the UAVs were
produced by Chinese companies.378 LAAF’s response was
reflected in paragraphs 7 and 9 of this annex.
The Panel assesses that the DS-220 UAV is a dual-use
item. The arms embargo applies to this type of UAVs in
the configuration observed in Libya and used by LAAF for
military purpose.

Appendix 24.M: QP537/VT370 VTOL UAVs
QP537/VT370 VTOL UAVs
(LAAF)
(26 May 2025)
The Panel identified, including from open sources,379 the
presence of 21 vertical take-off and landing (VTOL)
uncrewed aerial vehicles (UAVs) during the military parade.
These UAVs bore design features identical to a) QP537
VTOL, manufactured by AheadX Tech (Beijing) Co., Ltd.;
b) Raefly VT370 VTOL, manufactured by Raefly
(Guangzhou) Technology Co., Ltd; and c) Volitation VT370
VTOL, manufactured by Xiamen Airmobi Technology Co.,
Ltd. located in China.
VTOL UAVs can be used in security, agriculture, mapping,
inspection and intelligence, surveillance, target acquisition
and reconnaissance (ISTAR). AheadX QP537 VTOL has a
maximum payload of 13 kg (including fuel) and flight time
of six hours.380 Raefly VT370 VTOL has a maximum
payload of 15kg (including fuel) and a flight time up to 10
hours.381 Airmobi Volitation VT370 VTOL has a payload
capacity of up to 18 kg (including fuel) and a flight time of
15 hours (with a 5 kg payload).382 The Panel requested
further information from China and LAAF. China stated a) it
had found no records indicating the export of the relevant
UAVs to Libya; b) it was difficult to determine, solely on the
basis of images, whether the UAVs were produced by
Chinese companies.383 LAAF’s response was reflected in
paragraphs 7 and 9 of this annex.
The Panel assesses that this type of UAVs is a dual-use item.
The arms embargo applies to this type of UAVs when used
by LAAF for military purposes.

S/2026/224
1. The Panel identified, including from open sources,384 the presence of approximately 225 anti-uncrewed aerial vehicles
(UAV) equipment during the military parade. The anti-UAV equipment bore design features identical to GW-UAV80P and
GW-UAV-BP15D jammers, manufactured by Shenzhen Greetwin Technology Co. Ltd located in China.385
2. Anti-UAV equipment is electronic counter measures used to block UAV control, GPS and video signals, or force
UAVs to land.386 These types of devices can be used for security and protection of public events and key infrastructures, i.e.
airports, border, and military bases against unauthorised or hostile UAV activities.
3. The Panel requested further information from China and the LAAF. In response, China stated a) it had found no
records indicating the export of the relevant UAVs to Libya; b) it was difficult to determine, solely on the basis of images,
whether the UAVs were produced by Chinese companies.387 LAAF’s response was reflected in paragraphs 7 and 9 of this
annex.
4. The Panel assesses that these types of anti-UAV equipment are dual-use items. The arms embargo applies to these
types of anti-UAV equipment when used by LAAF for military purposes.

226/299 26-02825
[[page 227]]
S/2026/224
1. The Panel identified, including from open sources,388 the presence of 25 vehicles bearing design features identical to
Tomcar all-terrain vehicles (ATVs) during the military parade. Tomcar ATVs are products of Tomcar LLC, located in the
United States.
2. The Panel requested further information from Tomcar and LAAF. In response, Tomcar stated that a) their internal
investigations found no evidence demonstrating that the company sold, supplied, or otherwise transferred any ATVs to
LAAF; b) Tomcar engaged in only one transaction involving parties outside North America of a sale of 25 ATVs, one
service repair parts package, and two service tool packages to Gulf Mobility System Limited, a company based in the
United Arab Emirates (UAE); and c) Tomcar’s ATVs were commercial-grade products and eligible for sale to most
foreign jurisdictions, including the UAE, without an export license or prior governmental authorization. 389 LAAF’s
response was reflected in paragraphs 7 and 9 of this annex.
3. Information available to the Panel indicated that a possible diversion took place after the shipment to Gulf Mobility
System Ltd. Although advertising itself as “a master distributor for Tomcar”, Gulf Mobility System Ltd was not affiliated
to Tomcar.390 Gulf Mobility System Ltd. was registered at Masdar Free Zone, UAE. The company’s website is no longer
accessible.391
4. The Panel assesses that this type of vehicle is a dual-use item to which the arms embargo applies when used by
LAAF for military purposes.

S/2026/224
Chevrolet Suburban SUVs
(LAAF)
(26 May 2025)
The Panel identified, including from open sources,392 the
presence of eight vehicles with design features identical to
the 12th generation Chevrolet Suburban sport utility
vehicles (SUVs) during the military parade. These
vehicles, assigned to an EW unit, were mounted with
electronic warfare equipment. Chevrolet Suburban SUVs
are manufactured by General Motors Company (GM)
located in the United States.393
The Panel requested further information from GM and
LAAF. In response, GM stated that: a) these SUVs were
2021–2024 model; b) GM has not identified any
authorized sales of Suburban model to Libya in recent
years, and its dealer confirmed it had not sold Suburban
SUVs to Libya since 2020. 394 LAAF’s response was
reflected in paragraphs 7 and 9 of this annex.
The Panel assesses that this type of vehicle is a dual-use
item to which the arms embargo applies when used by
LAAF for military purposes.

26-02825 229/299
[[page 230]]
S/2026/224
1. The Panel identified, including from open sources,395 the presence of 34 vehicles with design features identical Ford
F250 Super Duty trucks during the military parade. These vehicles were used to carry man-portable air-defense systems
(MANPADS) and tow armed rigid-hulled inflatable boats Ford F250 Super Duty pickup trucks are manufactured by Ford
Motor Company located in the United States.396
2. The Panel requested further information from Ford Motor Company and LAAF. In its response,397 Ford stated that: a)
Ford discerned the vehicles were P558 generation (Model Years 2020 to 2022) and current generation (Model Years 2023
to present) F-250 trucks; b) Ford and its distributors did not identify any sales of F-250s to Libya; c) Ford believed that the
vehicles likely were acquired on the open market through third-party resellers; d) Ford F-250 Super Duty trucks are
designated as “EAR99” under the UnitedStates Export Administration Regulations, so they are not controlled items and are
not subject to export restrictions to Libya; and e) The vehicles appearing to be carrying or towing other equipment, Ford is
unable to identify any military modifications made to the pickup trucks themselves. LAAF’s response was reflected in
paragraphs 7 and 9 of this annex.
3. The Panel assesses that this type of vehicle is a dual-use item to which the arms embargo applies when used by LAAF
for military purposes.

S/2026/224
1. The Panel reported on the illicit activities of the Antonov An-12A aircraft (msn:2340608) with fake Burundi
registration 9U-BBD in Annex 97 of S/2022/427 owned by Space Cargo LLC (United Arab Emirates). This aircraft flew to
Benina Airport (HLLB) of Benghazi, Libya on 31 January 2025.
2. The Panel identified that this aircraft, bearing the same unique blue and red colour scheme, was publicly showcased
during the military parade (see figure 24.R.1).398 The Panel further identified that this aircraft had been based at Benina
Airport since it flew there in January 2021 (see figure 24.R.2).
3. The Panel requested further information from Space Cargo LLC and LAAF regarding the change of ownership or
operator of this aircraft. No response was received from Space Cargo LLC. LAAF’s response was reflected in paragraphs 7
and 9 of this annex.

26-02825 233/299
[[page 234]]
S/2026/224
1. During his visit to Benghazi, Libya on 18 December 2025, a government representative of Pakistan presented at least
two weapons equipped with Eye systems to commander-in-chief and deputy commander-in-chief of LAAF.399
2. The Eye system, fitted with a camera and a fold-out screen to the unit side, provides the capability of enabling the
front portion of the unit to pivot 90 degrees to the left or right, allowing the user to fire around corners. The Eye system can
be mounted to a side arm, small grenade launcher or other compact weapons.400 Developed and produced by Pakistan
Ordnance Factories (POF),401 Eye system was unveiled at the 11th International Defense Industry Fair (IDEF) between 10-
13 May 2011 in Istanbul, Türkiye.402
3. The Panel requested further information from Pakistan and LAAF. Replies were not received. The Eye system is
military equipment. Thus, the transfer of both, the Eye system and the weapon, constituted violations of paragraph 9 of
resolution 1970 (2011) by Pakistan.

2. Tables 26.3 and 26.4 summarise arms and military materiel that have been reported in open sources as new transfers.
The Panel is still investigating these alleged transfers as: (a) in some cases the arms and military materiel were in the
inventory of the Libyan Armed Forces prior to the 2011 arms embargo; and/or (b) the imagery was not of high enough
resolution to identify serial numbers or lot/batch numbers to confirm post-2011 manufacture, and thus enable the initiation
of tracing requests to identify supply chains. The Panel continues to investigate to find confirmatory information to the
appropriate evidential standards.

246/299 26-02825
[[page 247]]
S/2026/224
1. In its report S/2024/914,456 the Panel identified the presence of Inkas Titan-S 4x4 Armoured Personnel Carriers
(APCs) operated by the Libyan Arab armed forces (LAAF) criminal investigation department. Similarly, the Panel identified
the presence of INKAS Titan-S 4x4 APCs operated by the Government of National Unity-affiliated forces (GNU-AF) on
two occasions: a) from the social media account of the 555 Infantry Brigade,457 displaying the APC during the visit of a
military delegation of a Member State to 22 Infantry Battalion on 3 May 2023 in Tripoli, Libya; and b) from the social media
account of the “Presidency of the General Staff” of the Libyan Army,458 presenting a graduation ceremony attended by
former Chief of the General Staff of the Libyan Army, Mohamed Al-Haddad on 25 February 2025 in Gharyan, Libya.
2. The Panel requested further information from the GNU and INKAS Armored Vehicles LLC, United Arab Emirates
(hereinafter INKAS UAE). INKAS UAE responded on 14 May 2025 stating that: a) INKAS UAE had never sold, exported
or transferred Titan-S APC or any other civilian or military vehicle to Libya; b) the presence of such vehicles in the region
was not the result of any direct or indirect transaction conducted by INKAS UAE; c) certain companies in other countries
had copied INKAS UAE designs; and d) INKAS UAE had discontinued production of the previous Titan-S model and had
introduced completely redesigned versions of both platforms with distinct structural and design features. GNU did not
respond.
3. In response to the Panel’s inquiry on the transfer of the INKAS Titan-DS APC to the LAAF in 2020, INKAS UAE
provided similar answers indicating that it had not exported any vehicles to Libya and referring to other companies’ similar
products. The Panel assessed in its report S/2021/229 that it was not convinced of the veracity of the manufacturer’s
response.459 As no further detailed information has been provided by INKAS UAE in its response of 14 May 2025 in this
regard, nor any specific information about the new version of its Titan-S APCs, the Panel’s assessment remained unchanged.
4. Titan-S 4x4 APCs are military equipment. This vehicle type appeared on open sources but the Panel was not able to
independently corroborate beyond geolocation or other means as of the time of writing, thus not reaching the evidentiary
threshold to substantiate the transfer. The cumulative evidence shows that the presence of this equipment in Libya is more
likely than not.

250/299 26-02825
[[page 251]]
S/2026/224
1. In its report S/2021/229,462 the Panel identified the presence of INKAS Titan-DS APC operated by Tariq bin Ziyad
(TBZ) of the Libyan Arab armed forces (LAAF). In the current reporting period, the Panel identified, from the social media
account of 111 Brigade,463 Government of National Unity-affiliated forces (GNU-AF), the presence of INKAS Titan-DS
APCs during a parade held on 12 November 2023 in the Brigade’s camp in Tripoli, Libya.
2. The Panel requested further information from the GNU and INKAS Armoured Vehicles LLC, United Arab Emirates
(INKAS UAE). INKAS UAE responded on 14 May 2025 stating that: a) INKAS UAE had never sold, exported or
transferred any Titan-DS or Titan-s APC to any other civilian or military vehicle to Libya; b) the presence of such vehicles
in the region was not the result of any direct or indirect transaction conducted by INKAS UAE; c) certain companies in
other countries had copied INKAS UAE designs; and d) INKAS UAE had already discontinued production of the previous
Titan-DS model and had introduced completely redesigned versions of both platforms with distinct structural and design
features. GNU did not respond.
3. In response to the Panel’s inquiry on the transfer of Titan-DS APC to LAAF in 2020, INKAS UAE provided similar
answers indicating that it had not exported any vehicles to Libya and referring to other companies’ similar products. The
Panel assessed in its report S/2021/229 that it was not convinced of the veracity of the manufacturer’s response. No further
detailed information has been provided by INKAS UAE in its response of 14 May 2025 in this regard, nor any specific
information about the new version of its Titan-DS APCs. The Panel’s assessment remained unchanged.
4. Titan-DS APCs are military equipment. This vehicle type appeared on open sources but the Panel was not able to
independently corroborate beyond geolocation or other means as of the time of writing, thus not reaching the evidentiary
threshold to substantiate the transfer. The cumulative evidence shows that the presence of this equipment in Libya is more
likely than not.

252/299 26-02825

S/2026/224
1. The Panel identified, from the social media account of the “Libyan army media division”,465 equipment with design
features identical to CZ Scorpion EVO 3 A1 submachine guns on two occasions: a) at the opening ceremony of Sky Dive
Benghazi Tower, held on 19 November 2024 at the headquarters of 87 battalion of Libyan Arab armed forces (LAAF) in
Benghazi; and b) at the graduation ceremony of “a new batch of officers”, held on 13 January 2025 in Sabha. CZ scorpion
EVO 3 A1 submachine guns are manufactured by Česká Zbrojovka a.s. in Uherský Brod, Czechia.466
2. This is the first time the Panel has identified such equipment in the context of Libya. The Panel requested further
information from Czechia and LAAF. In its response, Czechia stated that: a) the Czech authorities excluded supply, sale or
transfer of any submachine guns model CZ Scorpion EVO 3 A1 to Libya; and b) the Czech authorities cannot confirm with
certainty that the arms are submachine guns models CZ Scorpion EVO due to the missing serial numbers or other
identification elements.467 LAAF did not respond.
3. This equipment type appeared in open sources but the Panel was not able to independently corroborate beyond
geolocation or other means as of the time of writing, thus not reaching the evidentiary threshold to substantiate the
transfer.

1. The Panel found that trainings were provided for the Libyan Arab armed forces (LAAF) units in Belarus. The Panel
identified from multiple open-source social media accounts, including that of the “Libyan army media division”, that 36
special operation forces (SOF) battalion of LAAF completed military training courses of “intensive programmes to raise
combat efficiency and enhance readiness to carry out various missions with high efficiency, in the Republic of Belarus”.470
A graduation ceremony, attended by chief of staff of the land forces Saddam Haftar, featured a special operation exercise
which involved a military helicopter, armoured personnel carriers (APCs) and sniper shooting (figures 28.1 to 28.3).471
These social media accounts also reported on 28 February 2025 that the 36 SOF battalion returned to Benghazi from Belarus.

2. The Panel further identified that in addition to the special operations and sniper firing exercises at the ceremony,
parachute training was also a component of the training course. Posts of several social media accounts472 claimed to be
owned by elements of 36 SOF battalion who participated in the trainings in Belarus, documented their parachuting exercise
conducted in Vitebsk, Belarus.473
3. TikTok account A (TA-A) posted on 7 April 2025 a photo of a group of armed personnel posing in front of a UAZ-
452 vehicle in military green colour scheme with the license of “0833-0” (figure 28.4), which bore characteristics of
Belarussian military vehicle license plates (black letters/numbers on white background, four digits followed by a dash and
a letter/number). On 14 May 2025, the same account posted a short video footage captioned with “36 Special Forces”
showing a group of personnel in military uniforms boarding an Antonov An-2 aircraft with the registration of “EW-360AB”
(figure 28.5). This aircraft is operated474 by the Voluntary Society for Assistance to the Army, Aviation and Navy of the
Republic of Belarus (DOSAAF) (figure 28.6).475 Based in Vitebsk, Belarus, DOSAAF is tasked, according to the
organization’s website, with “training of specialists for the armed forces, other troops and military formations” (figure
28.7).476

5. The Panel further identified that elements of the 36 SOF battalion took a flight provided by Belavia Airlines (flight
number B28801, aircraft registration EW-457PA) from Minsk to Benghazi on 27 February 2025 (figures 28.11 and 28.12).
This aircraft conducted six flights480 between Minsk and Benghazi between 25 February and 17 April 2025.
6. The Panel requested further information from the Republic of Belarus and LAAF. Belarus responded that: a) Belarus
is committed to strict compliance with the sanctions resolutions of the United Nations Security Council, including with
regard to Libya; b) The Libyan citizens received special training in the Republic of Belarus in providing security for critical
infrastructure sites, in maintaining law and order and performing security and cash collection activities, and in ensuring the
safety of Libyan citizens in distress; c) These activities were carried out in full compliance with paragraph 10 of Security
Council resolution 2095 (2013); and d) The Libyan citizens were flown by Belavia Airlines on the Minsk-Benghazi route
on 27 February 2025”. 481 LAAF confirmed to the Panel that its forces were trained by Belarus, without referring to specific
trainings.482
7. The trainings, involving parachuting, special operations and sniper firing, were of a military nature. While paragraph
10 of resolution 2095 (2013) exempts the provision of training when intended solely for security or disarmament assistance
to the Libyan government, 36 SOF battalion is not among the forces under the control of the Libyan Government.483 The
provision of special operation trainings was a violation of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011) by the Republic of Belarus.

1. The Panel identified that members of 87 battalion of the Libyan Arab armed forces (LAAF) completed advanced
training courses in the Republic of Belarus during the reporting period.
2. According to multiple open-source social media accounts, including that of the “Libyan army media division”,484
elements of 87 battalion, out of approximately 400 to 430 in total, participated in multiple military exercises and were
awarded with certificates, with training time “from November 2024 to August 2025” and issued by “the Academy of
National Security of the Republic of Belarus”, at the closing ceremony for the completion of the training courses. Open
sources also showed multiple exercises, including sniper firing, special operations and parachuting, were conducted at
closing ceremony, which was attended by Deputy Defence Minister of Belarus, LAAF’s deputy chief of staff of the land
forces, director of the training department, and commander of the 87 battalion (see figures 29.1 to 29.7).
3. The Panel further identified the ceremony was held at the “Air Force and Air Defence Forces training ground
Domanovo”, about 180 km southwest of Minsk, Belarus (figures 29.8 and 29.10).485

1. The Panel identified that the Bilateral Assistance and Support Mission in Libya (MIASIT)490 of the Ministry of
Defence of Italy, conducted a training course of “military combat method”, which was completed on 26 December 2024,
for 27 cadets at the Military Academy of Tripoli. Instructors from the Mobile Training Team of the Parachuting Training
Centre and the 184th “Nembo” Command Unit of Italy carried out the trainings on the hand-to-hand combat techniques for
Libyan participants.
2. The Panel requested further information from Italy and the Government of National Unity (GNU).491 No reply was
received. The “military combat method” training was of military nature. Thus, the provision of such training for Military
Academy of Tripoli cadets constituted a violation of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011) by Italy.

1. The Panel identified from an open-source flight-tracking platform506 that two Egyptian C-130 military transport
aircraft (registrations of SU-BAD and SU-BEY) and two C295 military transport aircraft (registrations of SU-BTG and SU-
BTL) conducted flights from Benghazi in an eastern direction on 27 May 2025 (figures 32.1 to 32.4). Information about the

including social media accounts of the “general command of the Libyan Arab armed forces” (LAAF),507 the “chief of staff

or covered, bore features of the above-mentioned Egyptian military transport aircraft (registrations of SU-BAD, SU-BEY,
SU-BTG and SU-BTL), with one C-130 aircraft’s registration visually recognised as SU-BEY (figures 32.5 to 32.8).
2. The Panel requested further information from Egypt and LAAF. Egypt responded that: a) its four military transport
aircraft participated, upon the request of LAAF, in transporting and training its paratroopers during the parade; b) the four
aircraft did not participate in other activities; and c) the air route was confined between East Cairo airbase and Benghazi.512
LAAF’s response was reflected in paragraphs 7 and 9 of Annex 24 of this report.
3. Providing transportation with military aircraft for LAAF’s paratroopers during its military parade constituted a
violation of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011) by Egypt.

280/299 26-02825
[[page 281]]
S/2026/224
A. Overview
1. On 27 August 2025, Spain intercepted the MV Lila Mumbai (IMO 9253143),513 carrying 10 vessels, as she was
approaching Ceuta for provisioning. The vessel was en route from Port Rashid, United Arab Emirates (UAE), where she
loaded the vessels and departed from on 14 July 2025. The Spanish authorities offloaded the 10 vessels onboard the MV
Lila Mumbai in the port of Algeciras (table 33.1 and appendix 33.A, figure 33.A.1).
2. The Spanish authorities granted the Panel access to those vessels on 6 November 2025 for an inspection. The Panel
found that they consisted of a) two 36-metres Grandweld TBZ fast patrol vessels (FPV) of the same make and model that
were already transferred to Libya (annex. 21); b) six 15-metres 2020 Volume fast interceptor craft (FIC) with gun mounts,
and c) two 17-metres 2020 Volume fast landing craft (FLC). The FIC and FLC were in different stages of completion.
3. The Panel found that Amro Ibrahim’s network, that was responsible for the transfer of 23 vessels to Benghazi, was
also responsible for this attempted transfer (annex 21).
Table 33.1
Vessels intercepted en route to Benghazi

B. Grandweld vessels
1. Overview
4. The two TBZ FPVs were built by Dubai-based Grandweld Shipyards (a branch of Atos International LLC) in Dubai
Maritime City (UAE),514 Port Rashid Mubarak Group Shipyard (AEPRA).
5. With all circumstantial evidence considered, the Panel found that Grandweld Shipyards was wilfully negligent in its
due diligence obligations vis-a-vis the end-user and the intended destination of the ten vessels.
2. Involved parties
6. As per cargo documentation reviewed by the Panel, the cargo destined to Benghazi consisted of two 38-metres work
boats titled TBZ 17 and TBZ 18 (12.5 tons each), six 15-metres vessels titled Interceptor (6 tons each) and two 17-metres
landing craft titled as such (11 tons each).
7. The shipper was Omar Mustafa’s II Solution FZE (UAE) (see annex XX). The vessel named TBZ17 and TBZ18
were included in the cargo description. The consignee was Danube Shipping Company (Benghazi). For both, see also
annexes 21 and 35). Omar Mustafa represented II Solutions FZE in the organization of this transport. Omar Mustafa’s
Multham International Limited (Hong Kong, China) commissioned the construction of the two Grandweld TBZ vessels and
registered them under the Saint Kitts and Nevis flag. Saint Kitts and Nevis terminated the registration in late July 2025. The
__________________
513 Flag: Liberia.
514 25°15'55.9"N, 55°15'47.0"E.
26-02825 281/299
[[page 282]]
S/2026/224
company also was the owner, manager and operator of the TBZ vessels. Before the transfer of ownership to Multham of
TBZ17 and TBZ18, both were briefly owned, managed and operated by 2020 Volume Boats Maintenance & Repairing FZE
(UAE), Amro Ibrahim’s company.
8. The Panel identified a) AAL Shipping GmbH & Co. KG (Germany) as the time charterer of the vessel, and thus the
responsible carrier; and b) Cigisped Middle East FZE (UAE) as the deck space charterer, and thus the responsible voyage
charter party.
9. AAL Shipping responded to the Panel that a) it had a long-standing business relationship with the deck space
charterer; b) it screened the involved parties against sanctions lists; c) it recognized that their compliance policies had proven
to be insufficient; and d) it took concrete steps to improve its due diligence.515
10. The Panel assessed that a) neither reputation nor long-standing business relationships would guarantee sanctions
compliance by business partners; and b) screening against sanctions lists was insufficient, as restrictive measures like arms
embargoes were not contained in lists of individuals and entities under sanctions.
11. Cigisped Middle East FZE, without providing any supporting documentation, informed the Panel that a) it assumed
the role of deck space charterer only and was not the charterer of the MV Lila Mumbai; b) it acted as freight forwarder
procuring the deck space for the transport of the vessels from Port Rashid to Benghazi; c) it was not provided with technical
drawings that would have allowed them to identify the vessels as military equipment; d) it was not involved in the export
procedure; e) in hindsight and in light of the Panel’s findings, it “recognised that certain vessel characteristics may warrant
enhanced scrutiny when Libya is the destination”; and e) it took concrete steps to improve its due diligence.516
12. The Panel assessed that for above points a) and b), a deck space charterer bore responsibility for the transported cargo
that goes beyond ensuring safe cargo transportation and includes sanctions compliance; c) the relevant cargo documentation
identified the vessels’ names starting with “TBZ” and others as “interceptor”, which were clear indicators for military end-
users in the context of Libya, referring to LAAF’s TBZ brigade; and d) export clearances bore no relevance on individual
due diligence by each concerned party.
13. The Panel assessed that the enhanced due diligence responsibilities emanating from transports into a country under
a UN arms embargo apply collectively to all parties that have operational control, even if only about a part of the transfer.
This applies to the charterer and carrier alike, particularly in cases where the operational control remains with the latter.
Together with other parties involved in maritime transport, such as deck space charterers or freight loaders, all parties carry
collective responsibility for ensuring sanctions compliance.
3. Grandweld 38-metres TBZ fast patrol vessels
14. The Panel identified the two 38-metres vessels as the MV TBZ17 (IMO 1089144) and the MV TBZ18 (IMO
1089156) aluminium-hulled vessels produced by Grandweld Shipyard in the UAE. Both vessels were naval grey.
15. The Panel found that the vessels’ design, structure, propulsion, manoeuvrability, configuration, including force
commander cabin, armoury and fast-launch auxiliary vessel ramp, as well as the onboard equipment, including (unmounted
but pre-wired) police lights and sirens, tactical bridge lighting, communications suite, and navigational equipment classify
them as fast patrol vessels (FPV) intended for specialized official roles, such as maritime law enforcement, border patrol, or
military use.
16. The vessels had a protruding platform under the bridge in the bow section that was structurally reinforced in a manner
that it could support the mounting of a variety of small and medium calibre weaponry, realistically up to a 40mm cannon.
That platform is in the same place as where a model of the FPV TBZ13 (IMO 1089106) had its gun mounted; that same
model was displayed at the Grandweld stand at NAVDEX Dubai, February 2025 (annex 21, appendix 21.C).
17. The auxiliary vessels, one mounted on each of the TBZ vessels’ fast-launch ramps, were two-outboard 6.5-metres
rigid-hulled inflatable boats (RHIBs), identified by the Panel as pertaining to the workboat line of a UAE-based producer,
without any military modifications. The Panel assessed that that producer had sold the two RHIBs to Grandweld Shipyards
and was unaware of their export to Libya.
__________________
515 Meeting with AAL Shipping, 3 November 2025.
516 Communication from Cigisped Middle East, 20 January 2026.
282/299 26-02825
[[page 283]]
S/2026/224
18. The FPV TBZ17 had signs of previous waterborne activities evidenced by marine growth on its lower hull; this was
evidenced by a navigational report examined by the Panel during the inspection.
19. Onboard the FPV TBZ18, the Panel also observed technical documentation relating to the two TBZ FPVs and to
several FPVs of the same type delivered to Libya in 2025 (appendix 33.A and annex 21).
20. For inspection images of the FPVs TBZ17 and TBZ18 see appendix 33.A.
C. 2020 Volume Boats FICs and FLCs
1. Fast interceptor craft
21. The Panel identified the six interceptor vessels as 15-metres quad-outboard, stepped-hull design aluminium fast
interceptor craft (FIC). The vessels were naval grey.
22. The vessels were in various stages of completion, from fully finished (one vessel) to 20 to 40 percent built completion.
The unfinished vessels contained many boxes with boat building material including spare parts, navigation and
communication equipment, other vessel furnishing and used power tools.
23. The Panel assessed that the interceptor vessels were high-speed vessels intended for specialized official roles, such
as maritime law enforcement, border patrol, or military use. The assessment was based on their design, structure, propulsion,
manoeuvrability, seating structure and configuration, as well as onboard equipment, including police light and siren switches,
communications suite, and navigational equipment. Each vessel also had two pedestal gun mounts soldered to their aft
section, which were also identified as gun mounts in documentation onboard one the vessels examined by the Panel.
24. The Panel established that the FICs were marketed as Interceptor by “2020 Volume Boats by Asha Co”, Amro
Ibrahim’s company. 2020 Volume Boats Maintenance & Repairing FZE (UAE) was not registered as a shipyard and used a
local shipyard to produce the two types of vessels it marketed. With all circumstantial evidence considered, the Panel
assessed that Grandweld Shipyards was the producer of the FICs.
25. For inspection images of the 2020 Volume FICs, see appendix 33.A.
2. Fast landing craft
26. The Panel identified the two 17-metres landing craft as aluminium-hulled fast landing craft (FLC), marketed by Amro
Ibrahim’s “2020 Volume Boats by Asha Co”.
27. The two FLCs were at 40 percent and 70 percent stages of completion; particularly, their bridge sections were mostly
unfinished. Their hull sections were naval grey, whilst their bridge sections white. Both vessels contained large numbers of
boxes containing boat building material including spare parts, navigation and communication equipment, other vessel
furnishing and used power tools. One landing craft also contained a mould frame to produce fibre glass boats. With all
circumstantial evidence considered, the Panel assessed that Grandweld Shipyards was the producer of the FLCs.
28. For inspection images of the 2020 Volume FLCs, see appendix 33.A.
3. Response by Amro Ibrahim
29. In response to the Panel’s opportunity to reply, Amro Ibrahim, representing Asha Co. a) asserted the civilian nature
of both the FICs and the FLCs; b) provided schematic drawings of the vessels; and c) quoted regulatory oversight by the
UAE authorities. The Panel found the statements made and documentation provided to be contradictory to evidence gathered
by the Panel, including during the inspection of the vessels. The drawings of the interceptor model shared by Amro Ibrahim
did not correspond to the models inspected by the Panel; during the inspection, the Panel observed weapon mounts and
corollary documentation issued by Asha Co. Owing to the large amount of evidence gathered, and taking all circumstantial
evidence into consideration, the Panel found Amro Ibrahim’s statements not credible.
D. Panel assessment
30. The Panel assessed the FPV TBZ17, the FPV TBZ18 and the six FICs as naval-type vessels and thus military
equipment, whose delivery to Benghazi would have fallen within the scope of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011).
26-02825 283/299
[[page 284]]
S/2026/224
31. The Panel assessed that while the two FLCs may also have a civilian application, their design and the context qualifies
them as military equipment; their design, color and relatively low cargo capacity in terms of weight indicate an intended use
for platoon-size fast transport and landing rather than the transport of heavy equipment for uses such as disaster response.
Given that the two FLCs were part of the same cargo as the naval-type vessels, with the same shipper and consignee, both
of which have been identified by the Panel in several arms embargo violations and attempted arms embargo violations, the
Panel assessed that their intended end-user was the LAAF. Thus, the Panel classified the two FLCs as military equipment
whose delivery to Benghazi would have fallen within the scope of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011).
32. Lastly, the Panel assessed that, owing to the different stages of completion of the FICs and FLCs, the network of
Amro Ibrahim and Omar Mustafa either already possessed a shipbuilding workshop in Libya, or was in the process of
creating such a workshop.

26-02825 289/299

S/2026/224
A. Overview
1. On 18 and 28 June 2024, respectively, the vessels MSC Arina (IMO: 9839284) and MSC Apolline (IMO: 9896983)
arrived at Gioia Tauro port, Italy, following which the Italian authorities seized containers destined to Benghazi holding
concealed and mis-declared components of what the Panel identified as relating to two Fei Long-1 (FL-1) medium-altitude
long endurance (MALE) uncrewed aerial vehicles (UAV). The Panel examined the content of six containers in May 2025.
B. Fei Long (FL-1) UAV
1. Containers
2. On 20 and 21 May 2025, the Italian authorities provided access to the Panel to six containers in the port of Gioia
Tauro, which had been seized there in June 2024. The Panel observed that the components were in six 40-foot shipping
containers, some together with fake plywood wind turbine blades (table 35.1).

a Source: Confidential.
2. Fuselages
3. The Panel observed two fuselages, which it found to be consistent with the design features and dimensions of those
of the FL-1 UAV. The fuselages had been fully wrapped with white vinyl; one also had additional bubble wrap around it.
The nose and tail sections had plywood covers, also wrapped with white vinyl. On one of the two fuselages, the vinyl had
been partially removed. The fuselage with the fully intact vinyl and its covers had inscriptions in Chinese and English
language suggesting that they were wind power generators, including “The Wind moves the world, the energy-saving world”,
“Electricity – Keep away” and “Wind power maintenance hatch” (figure 35.1).
4. Under the white vinyl, the fuselages were military gray. The fuselages had no signs of wear and were primed with
cabling and hydraulics. The nose section of one of the fuselages contained some carbon plating on the inside. Each fuselage
had three fuel tanks mounted in their centre sections. Under the fuel tanks were some oil stains, suggesting previous use or
testing. The fuselages missed several components: a) the engine; b) engine bay covers and cowlings; c) the wing root
fairings; d) the landing gear; and e) the top and bottom covers of their nose sections that house aviation and electronic
components (figures 35.2 and 35.3).
5. Both fuselages had serial numbers in various places starting with “FL1-xx” (figure 35.6).
290/299 26-02825
[[page 291]]
S/2026/224
3. Wings and V-tail surfaces
6. The Panel observed two left and two right wings, and two V-tail surfaces, all of which it found to be consistent with
the design features and dimensions of those of the FL-1 UAV. The wings were each had two fake plywood/fiber-glass
turbine blades on either side. One set of wings and V-tail surfaces were fully covered in bubble wrap; on others the bubble
wrap had been mostly removed. (figure 35.4).
7. The wings and V-tail surfaces had no vinyl wrapping and were military grey. The wings had three multiple-pin and
one single pin connector each, consistent with connectors at the wing mounting points of the fuselages. The V-tail surfaces
had one multiple-pin connector. Each wing had twelve cabled hardpoints for the attachment of a total of three pylons per
wing (figure 35.5).
8. The wings and V-tail surfaces had serial numbers in various places starting with “FL1-xx” (figure 35.6).
4. Panel assessment
9. The Panel found that the components examined were consistent in design and dimensions with the Fei Long-1
(Flying Dragon-1 or FL-1) medium-altitude long-endurance uncrewed aerial vehicles (MALE UAV), as advertised by
Zhong Tian Guide Control Technology Company (ZT Guide) (China).517 Serial numbers visible on fuselages, wings and V-
tail surfaces also identified them as belonging to an FL-1 UAV.
10. The Panel assessed that owing to the multi-role platform capabilities of the FL-1, including the possibility to attach
pylons for payload mounting, the FL-1 would be considered military equipment in the context of paragraph 9 of resolution
1970 (2011). Therefore, the Panel assessed the FL-1 components as spare parts under the same paragraph, whose delivery
to Benghazi would have fallen within the scope of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011).
C. Shipper and consignee
11. Cargo documentation518 showed that the six containers in which the UAV components were discovered had been
loaded in the port of Qingdao, China (CNQIN). Container numbers MSDU5870446 and MSDU6752406 had been loaded
aboard the MSC Arina on 8 April 2024 in Qingdao, under bill of lading number MEDUQR400761. Container numbers
CAAU5613236, MEDU4500901, TGBU5535628, TGBU5589481 had been loaded aboard the MSC Arina on 8 April 2024
in Qingdao port and then transloaded aboard the MSC Apolline on 27 April 2024 in Ningbo port, China. After Qingdao, the
MSC Arina made a port call in the Republic of Korea and another five port calls in China (including Ningbo, where four
containers were transloaded to the MSC Apolline), one in Singapore and two in Spain. After Ningbo, MSC Apolline made
port calls in Singapore and Morocco. Container tracking showed that none of the containers had been transloaded in other
locations before arriving in Gioia Tauro port, Italy.
12. The UAV components had been declared as turbine blades and wind power equipment accessories in the cargo
documentation. The customs declaration for the export of goods also showed that the items were mis-declared as 12 packages
of wind turbine blades to the Chinese authorities.
13. The Panel identified Xinchang Midou Trading Co. Ltd (China) as the shipper for all six containers. The Panel also
identified the involvement of Amro Ibrahim’s network (annexes 21 and 34): Omar Mustafa’s Emirati phone number519 and
his II Solution FZE (UAE) email address520 were provided as contact details for the consignee and notify party for all six
containers, which was “Danube Shipping Co.” in Benghazi. That consignee is the same entity in Benghazi that the Panel
identified as consignee of several vessels, including naval-type vessels, to Libya.521

S/2026/224
A. MV BBC Alaska (IMO 9453793)
1. The Panel previously reported transfers of naval-type vessels by the MV BBC Alaska (IMO 9453793) from Port
Rashid, UAE, to Benghazi in March 2024.522 The Panel identified several individuals and entities as responsible for these
transfers in violation of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011). One of these entities was BBC Chartering GmbH & Co. KG
(Germany), which the Panel, by way of a typographical error, named as the “charterer” of the MV BBC Alaska. In fact,
BBC Chartering GmbH & Co. KG is the vessel’s operator.
2. On 23 December 2025, BBC Chartering responded to the Panel’s letter dated 9 July 2024, in relation to the transfer.
The points conveyed in that letter and the Panel’s assessment thereof are the same as laid out above (annex 21, section B,
subsection 3) and relate to a different transfer by another vessel (MV BBC Rheiderland) that the company operates. Owing
to the circumstances of the transfer onboard the MV BBC Alaska, the Panel’s finding regarding the company’s responsibility
remains unchanged.
3. The Panel newly identified the deck space charterer of that voyage, Peters & May Ltd (United Kingdom). The Panel
found Peters & May in violation of paragraph 9 of resolution 1970 (2011) for having procured the deck space for the
transport of the Interceptor vessel to Libya in January 2024. Peters & May Ltd did not respond to the Panel’s letter dated 26
December 2026.
